Measurements of the Higgs boson production
associated with a Z/W boson
in the decay channel to b-quarks
using pp collisions at →s = 13 TeV
A Dissertation Submitted for the Degree of Doctor of Philosophy
February 2025
The University of Tokyo - Department of Physics, Graduate School of Science
Aoto Tanaka
7-3-1 Hongo, Bunkyo-ku, Tokyo, 113-0033, Japan

=== PDF PAGE 4 ===


=== PDF PAGE 5 ===
v
Abstract
The Higgs boson was discovered by the ATLAS and CMS Collaborations in 2012 using the proton￾proton collisions at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC). The corresponding Higgs field is in charge
of giving masses of bosons and fermions in the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics. Since the
Higgs sector is not well-understood, probing the Higgs sector is important to understand physics
in electroweak phase transition in the SM. While the SM provides the successful description in
particle physics, given that the SM cannot explain remaining problems such as the hierarchy prob￾lem, new physics beyond the SM (BSM) is demanded. If the Higgs sector would be extended as
solutions for these problems, the fact could be observed by the properties of the Higgs boson such
as cross-section, which might be modified from the prediction in the SM. Since the extension of
Higgs sector is discussed in wide-ranged major BSM physics theories, it is motivated to use the
Higgs boson in physics analysis for probing the BSM physics. Therefore, measurements of the
Higgs boson properties with high precision are being conducted at the LHC.
The differential cross-section of the Higgs boson production associated with a Z/W boson (VH
production), and the coupling strength of the Higgs boson and the bottom quarks are two of the
sensitive observables to BSM physics. If significant deviations from the SM (∼5%) are observed,
they can imply new physics in energy scale of 1 - 3 TeV, and determine the direction of particle
physics for future. This study targets on VH(H → bb¯, V → leptons) process. Choosing a decay
mode of a pair of two bottom quarks with a branching fraction of 58 % is reasonable to obtain
enough statistics of VH production. Besides, focusing the VH production mode is reasonable to tag
the H → bb¯ decay events with high sensitivity. With these mutual profits, targeting the VH(→ bb¯)
process is effective approach to measure the differential cross-section of the VH production and
inclusive cross-section of the VH(→ bb¯) process that is equivalent to the coupling strength of the
H → bb¯ decay. Since the dominant uncertainty of the previous VH(→ bb¯) analysis comes from
systematic errors rather than its statistical uncertainty, improving analysis techniques to suppress
the systematic uncertainty is the major theme in this thesis.

=== PDF PAGE 6 ===
vi
This thesis presents studies for improvement of precise measurement of the VH(→ bb¯) process
using 140 fb−1 of proton-proton collision data at →s = 13 TeV collected in the ATLAS detector.
There are three major results from these studies:
• Inclusive cross-section of the VH(H → bb¯) process (the highest precision)
• Inclusive cross-section of both the WH and ZH production (the first observation)
• Differential cross-section of the VH production (the highest performance)
The observed signal strength of VH(→ bb¯) process, which is defined as a ratio of observed signal
yields to predicted signal yields with respect to the SM, is
µbb
VH = 0.92 +0.16
−0.14 = 0.92 ± 0.10(stat.) +0.13
−0.11(syst.).
According to the measurement of signal strength, the observed inclusive VH cross-section times
V → leptons and H → bb¯ branching ratio is 170.1 +29.6
−25.9 [fb] within the analysis phase space. These
are the most precise results of the VH production cross-section at →s = 13 TeV ever as of 2025.
In the measurement of inclusive cross-section of both of the WH and ZH production, observed
(expected) signal significance for background-only hypothesis in the WH production is 5.3(5.5)
σ, and the observed (expected) signal significance in the ZH production is 4.9(5.6) σ. Especially,
the observation over 5 σ is achieved in the WH(→ bb¯) process for the first time. The differential
cross-section measurement of the VH production as functions of Z/W boson momentum and jet
multiplicity is performed for the first time. The observed cross-sections in almost all phase spaces
are consistent with the SM predictions. In some high Z/W boson momentum categories, the ob￾served cross-sections are lower than the SM predictions within two standard deviations. In the
future VH(H → bb¯) analysis with more statistics, the analysis techniques improved in this thesis
can help to suppress the systematic uncertainty, which may results in the observation of significant
deviation from the SM prediction.

Chapter 1
Introduction
1.1 Theoretical backgrounds
1.1.1 The Standard Model (SM) of the particle physics
The Standard Model (SM) of particle physics is a relativistic field theory to describe motions
of particles and interactions between elementary particles as the exchange of the force carrying
particles. Elementary particles and their interactions in the SM are summarised in Figure 1.1.
Quarks and leptons are fermions and building blocks of matters. The interactions between fermions
are mediated by gauge bosons, which are also called as vector bosons. Each type of gauge bosons
is associated with a specific interaction *1 : photons for the electromagnetic interaction, gluons for
the strong interaction, and W/Z bosons for the weak interaction. The electromagnetic interaction
of quantum electrodynamics (QED) affects all the particles except an electrically neutral particle
such as a neutrino. The strong interaction of quantum chromodynamics (QCD) only interacts
with quarks due to their colour change. For the weak interaction, the W boson couples to only
left-handed particles, and the Z boson couples to both left-handed particles and right-handed anti￾particles. The SM is constructed based on gauge symmetry groups of SU(3) × SU(2)L × U(1)Y in
gauge theory, where the SU(3) symmetry represents the strong interaction, and SU(2)L × U(1)Y
symmetry represents the electroweak interactions. The Higgs boson is a scalar boson to provide
masses to massive elementary particles of the SM. The fermion masses are generated via Yukawa
interactions in the Higgs mechanism after spontaneous symmetry breaking happened, which is
briefly summarised in Section 1.1.2. More details of the Higgs boson are in Section 1.2.
*1 A gravitational force is not included in the SM

=== PDF PAGE 16 ===
2 1.1 Theoretical backgrounds
Fig. 1.1: Elementary particles in the Standard Model [1]. The quarks and leptons are fermion
particles with 1/2 spin. All matters are made up of these fermions. Anti-particles of fermions
are also available as particles with opposite sign quantum numbers. The vector bosons are 1 spin
particles and carry interactions. The Higgs boson is a 0 spin particle and gives masses to massive
elementary particles.
1.1.2 Spontaneous symmetry breaking
In the unbroken gauge symmetry system, all the elementary particles are massless. By intro￾ducing the Higgs mechanism to break symmetry spontaneously, the Higgs field gives mass to the
particles*2 as follows.
Lagrangian of the SM can be written as
LSM = LGauge + LHiggs + LYukawa. (1.1)
LGauge represents the gauge interactions of the elementary particles, and kinematic terms of
fermions and gauge bosons. LHiggs represents the Higgs term. LYukawa represents the Yukawa
interactions between fermions and Higgs.
*2 A photon and a gluon are known as massless particles

1.1.3 Extension of SM Higgs sector
The SM has been tested and provides successful descriptions for the results of particle physics
experiments for now. However, because the SM cannot explain some remaining problems such as
no candidate particle of the dark matter, hierarchy problem and so on, new physics beyond the SM

=== PDF PAGE 19 ===
Chapter 1. Introduction 5
(BSM) is demanded. There are some new BSM physics that can extend the Higgs sector in the
SM, for example, Minimal Supersymmetric Standard Model (MSSM) [3], and a composite Higgs
model [4]. These extensions of the SM Higgs sector propose the deviations from the SM that
can be observed in the high precise measurement. Therefore, the Higgs boson’s properties have
high sensitivity in searching for BSM. The precision of current measurements is still not enough to
figure out this evidence for BSM, but when the significant deviations from the SM can be observed,
some BSM theories can be chosen as candidates for new physics and more verifications for these
theories can be performed. With this evidence for BSM in the high energy frontier physics, it’s
expected to be able to decide the direction of elementary particle physics in the future. Therefore,
the precise measurements of the Higgs boson’s properties are very important to accomplish the
discovery of new physics.
Herein, as the important properties of the Higgs boson to test possible new BSM physics models,
the coupling between the Higgs boson and the other particle, and differential cross-section of the
Higgs production processes in certain phase space are measured. The extensions of SM Higgs
sector can modify the coupling between the Higgs boson and the other particles as shown in Figure
1.3. For differential cross-section measurement, the deviations can be interpreted by Effective Field
Theory (EFT) framework as discussed in Section 1.1.4. This thesis focuses on the Higgs boson
production associated with a Z/W boson in the decay channel of bottom quarks (VH(H → bb¯))
as described in Section 1.2.3, whose Feynman diagram is shown in Figure 1.4. Hence, this thesis
measures the inclusive cross-section of VH(H → bb¯) process that is equivalent to the coupling
strength of the H → bb¯, as explained in Section 1.3.1, and the differential cross-section of the VH
production, as explained in Section 1.3.2, to examine their deviations from the SM predictions.
The remarkable vertices in the VH(H → bb¯) process in these measurements are also shown in
Figure 1.4.
1.1.4 Probing new physics with Effective Field Theory (EFT)
Extension of the SM can be indirectly tested by the precise comparison of the experimental
observables and the SM predictions. By introducing many tree-level operators that can modify
Higgs observables, the deviations from the SM could be compared to the extended SM Lagrangian
with these operators in higher dimensions. The higher dimension operators are approximations of
the actual theory at the higher energy. The identification of the corresponding higher dimension
operators, which describe the deviations, could help us specify the actual new BSM physics theory
at higher energy scale, as an approximation of possible UltraViolet completion theories. This
theory to extend the SM with arbitral higher dimension operators is called Effective Field Theory

=== PDF PAGE 20 ===
6 1.1 Theoretical backgrounds
Fig. 1.3: The expected deviation from the SM prediction for the coupling constant between the
Higgs boson and other particles [5]. The left plot introduces a model of Supersymmetry(SUSY);
the MSSM model, and the Yukawa coupling of a bottom quark is predicted to be increased by
∼ 3 %. The right plot introduces a composite Higgs model, and the Yukawa coupling of a bottom
quark is predicted to be decreased by ∼ 4 %.
Fig. 1.4: Feynman diagram of VH(H → bb¯) process. The targeted physics signal process in this
thesis. The remarkable vertices for the measurements conducted in this thesis are shown with red
circles.
(EFT), and the framework of the SM extension with the EFT is called “SMEFT”.
Extra operators are added to the SM Lagrangian (LSM) to obtain an effective Lagrangian
(LSMEFT)[6, 7, 8], which is given as:
LSMEFT = LSM +

of Two-Higgs-Doublet model (2HDM) such as MSSM [9].
In this thesis, the differential cross-section of VH production (Section 1.3.2) is measured. The
Wilson coefficients and corresponding SMEFT operators, which are sensitive in the analysis of
VH production, are listed in Table C.1. Here, the following three operators affect the cross-section
of VH production as a function of transverse momentum of a vector boson (pV
T)[10, 11]. Q(3)
Hq has
a strong impact for both the ZH and WH cross-section depending on momentum of Z/W boson,
while QHu has a similar impact for only the ZH cross-section. QHW works for an overall modifi￾cation of the VH cross-section. The deviations from the SM on these three operators, especially
for Q(3)
Hq and QHu in high momentum of Z/W boson, can be simulated as shown in Figure 1.5.
The value of the Wilson coefficient adopted in this simulation is picked up from the upper limit of
the Wilson coefficient constraint (Figure C.1) of the previous VH(H → bb¯) analysis [12], where a
value of Λ = 1 TeV is assumed. Details of the SMEFT interpretation including constraints on the
Wilson coefficients in the previous analysis [12, 10, 11, 9] are described in Appendix C.
1.2 The Higgs boson phenomenology in the LHC
The Higgs boson is an elementary particle in the SM and the massive scalar boson to give mass
to bosons and fermions of the SM with the so called Englert-Brout-Higgs mechanism [13, 14, 15,
16, 17, 18], which is shown in Section 1.1.2. The Higgs boson was discovered by the ATLAS
[19] and CMS [20] Collaborations in 2012 [21, 22] with a mass of approximately 125 GeV using
the proton-proton (pp) collisions produced by the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) [23]. Since this
discovery, about 30 times more Higgs boson data have been recorded in the ATLAS experiment,
and more precise measurements to test BSM physics theories have been performed. This thesis
focuses on an analysis of VH(H → bb¯) process (Details are in Section 1.2.3). This section reviews
productions and decay modes of the Higgs boson, and shows interest in the VH(H → bb¯) process.
1.2.1 The Higgs boson production in the proton-proton collisions

Feynman diagrams for these modes are shown in Figure 1.6. Since the phase space of the Higgs
production depends on the Higgs mass, the cross-section of the Higgs production changes as shown
in Figure 1.8 (a). Figure 1.8 (b) shows the predicted cross-section, which is calculated with →s =

1.2.2 The Higgs boson decays
The produced Higgs boson immediately decays into other particles. Since the phase space of
the Higgs decay depends on the Higgs mass, the branching ratio of the Higgs decay changes as
shown in Figure 1.9 (a). Figure 1.9 (b) shows the predicted branching ratio, which is calculated
with →s = 13 TeV and the Higgs mass as 125 GeV, and the observed branching ratio measured
with Run2 dataset (Section 3.1) of the ATLAS experiment. The observed branching ratios are
consistent with the predicted branching ratios within their uncertainties, and more evaluation of
the observed result of H → bb¯ decay mode is discussed in Section 1.3.1. The dominant decay
mode is H → bb¯. This mode has a branching ratio of 58.2%[26].
Fig. 1.9: The branching ratio of the Higgs boson decays in each mode. (a) Predicted branching
ratio of each Higgs boson decay mode as a function of the Higgs mass [26]. (b) Observed and
predicted branching ratio of each Higgs boson decay mode in the Run 2 ATLAS experiment [25].
1.2.3 VH (V → lepton, H → bb¯) process
The precise measurement of VH production may probe the BSM physics as discussed in Section
1.1.3. Moreover, as targeting a leptonic decay mode of a vector boson*5 which can be detected
in high momentum resolution, the measurement can provide a sensitive probe for momentum
dependent higher dimensional operators in the EFT as discussed in Section 1.1.4. Considering the
low statistics of the VH production measurement due to the cross-section of the VH production
*5 The branching ratio of leptons: 13 %(Z) 43 %(W), hadrons: 70 %(Z) 67 %(W)[1]

=== PDF PAGE 26 ===
12 1.2 The Higgs boson phenomenology in the LHC
and the branching ratio of the leptonic decaying vector boson, H → bb¯ decay mode with the
largest branching ratio is chosen. The candidate signal event of this VH(H → bb¯) process e.g. the
WH(W → µν, H → bb¯) process in the dataset recorded in the ATLAS experiment (Section 3.1)
is presented in Figure 1.10. Because of handling jets, described in Section 4.3, in the final state,
the VH(H → bb¯) process has many background processes such as V+jets and tt
¯ processes. The
key of precise measurement of the VH(H → bb¯) process is, thus, to reduce and constrain these
backgrounds as much as possible.
Fig. 1.10: ATLAS event display for the candidate signal event of WH(W → µν, H → bb¯) process

shows a track of neutrino.
The results of observed events for different combinations of the Higgs boson production and
decay process with the LHC-ATLAS Run2 full dataset (details are in Section 3.1) are summarized
in Figure 1.11 [25]. The analyses of the VH production modes are summarised with more de￾tails in Appendix B. Looking at the results of the VH production in Figure 1.11, the analysis of
VH(H → bb¯) process is already established [28, 12] and it has the lowest uncertainty thanks to the
largest statistics. Systematic uncertainty determines the precision of the VH(H → bb¯) analysis,
while the other VH analyses need more statistics.
This analysis fixes the mass of Higgs boson(mH) to 125 GeV, because this analysis is not very
sensitive to the mH, and because this analysis focuses on precise measurements of the properties
of the Higgs boson, which can be performed accurately enough by assuming the mH = 125 GeV
as a benchmark.

1.3 Prior measurements for the VH(H → bb¯) process
This section presents two kinds of measurements in the previous VH(H → bb¯) analyses.
1.3.1 Higgs-bottom Yukawa coupling measurement
The Yukawa coupling of a bottom quark and the Higgs boson (Section 1.1.2) is known to be
sensitive to BSM physics. Some BSM theories predict deviations from the SM for the Higgs￾bottom Yukawa coupling for ∼ 3 − 5 % as shown in Figure 1.3.
The measurement of a signal strength as defined in Eq. (1.15) is performed to evaluate the
deviation of the Yukawa coupling of a bottom quark from the SM prediction.
µ = σobserved × BRobserved

with an observed (expected) significance of 6.4(6.3) standard deviations for the background-only
prediction. The uncertainty is not small enough to evaluate the deviation from the SM prediction.
The systematic uncertainty is the dominant uncertainty in the inclusive VH(H → bb¯) analysis,
and the major systematic uncertainties are modelling uncertainties and experimental uncertainties,
which can be reduced by the analysis techniques.
1.3.2 Fiducial differential cross-section measurement
As explained in Section 1.1.4, cross-section of VH production is sensitive to BSM physics
processes, especially in the phase space of high transverse momentum of a vector boson (pV
T).
The advantage to measure cross-section rather than the signal strength is to reduce the contribu￾tion of signal theoretical uncertainty to the measurement. When the signal strength is measured,
several theoretical uncertainties from the simulated SM prediction (Section 9.2.1) are folded into
the measurement. Meanwhile, the differential cross-section measurement only considers the un￾certainty of the fraction of event yields in an analysis phase space derived by MC simulation,
referred to as “acceptance” uncertainty.
The VH(V → lep, H → bb¯) analysis adopts a Simplified Template Cross-section (STXS) frame￾work to measure the fiducial differential cross-section of VH production mode.
Simplified Template Cross-section (STXS) framework [26, 29]:
This framework is developed and established in order to maximise the Higgs signal sensitiv￾ity of the measurements, while at the same time, to minimise the theory dependencies which
are directly folded into the measurements. Phase space of the differential cross-section is
divided by simplified (i.e. ideal) “STXS bins”. The STXS bins are defined with respect to
the following points.
• To minimise acceptance uncertainties; estimated at the boundary of the STXS bins.
• To maximise experimental sensitivity.
• To isolate possible BSM effects from other BSM effects.
Since the optimisation of the STXS bins differs between topologies of the Higgs production
modes, the measurement with the STXS framework is conducted separately for the pro￾duction modes. In the fiducial differential cross-section measurement, the STXS bins are
applied to the phase space based on simulation-based truth particle information, referred to
as “truth level” *6 (Section 3.2.1), so that the results of different experiments in the LHC
*6 Since objects are reconstructed from discrete detected data/sample in a particle physics analysis, generally, the event
categorisation into regions is conducted based on the reconstructed object information; referred to as “reconstructed
level”

=== PDF PAGE 29 ===
Chapter 1. Introduction 15
can be combined.
In the VH(V → lep) production mode, the finest STXS bins are defined with two kinematic
variables, as shown in Figure 1.12: Transverse momentum of a vector boson (pV,t
T ), and number of
jets originated from Initial State Radiation (ISR) in truth level. This binning definition is effective
in terms of separating the contribution of different higher dimension operators, or separating the
signal processes by partons (quarks and gluons) in their initial states. In this analysis, as shown
in Figure 1.12, the cross-sections of WH and ZH are measured separately. The cross-sections of
qq → ZH and gg → ZH processes are measured inclusively because the signal sensitivity with an
available dataset is currently insufficient to distinguish these two processes.
Fig. 1.12: Definition of the phase spaces of the STXS framework for VH production mode [26, 29].
The phase space is divided by two variables; a vector boson transverse momentum and the number
of ISR jets. The measurement is performed separately based on the ZH process or the WH process.
The result of the fiducial differential cross-section measurement in the previous analysis [12] is
shown in Figure 1.13. Due to time constraints, the binning is reduced, and the measurement with
the binning based on only the pV,t
T in truth level is performed to check the deviations especially in
the high pV,t
T bins. All the observed event yields (σ × BR) are consistent with the SM predictions
within their uncertainties. The SMEFT interpretation from this result is summarised in Appendix
C.

1.4 Improved VH(H → bb¯) analysis
The analysis of the VH(H → bb¯) process is re-analysed with improved analysis techniques and
strategy as described in Section 1.4.1. The improved analysis allows us [A.] to improve the preci￾sion of VH(H → bb¯) signal strength measurement, and [B.] to perform fiducial differential cross￾section measurement with finer bins.
1.4.1 Analysis strategy
The main target process of this analysis is the VH(H → bb¯) signal process, but a coherent treat￾ment with the VH(H → cc¯) process is reasonable to perform due to the similarity of the H → bb¯
process and H → cc¯ process*7 in terms of the major backgrounds and the object reconstruc-
*7 H → cc¯ mode has a branching ratio of 2.9%[26]

=== PDF PAGE 31 ===
Chapter 1. Introduction 17
tion such as jets. Therefore, this analysis aims to perform the measurement of VH(H → bb¯) and
VH(H → cc¯) processes simultaneously. It should be noted that the effect of the VH(H → cc¯) pro￾cess is limited since its statistics is very low. This combination can provide significant gains for
the constraint on the background processes for both the VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) processes.
Hereinafter, VH(H → bb¯) is also denoted as VH(bb¯), VH(H → cc¯) as VH(cc¯). As illustrated in
Figure 1.14, the three individual previous analyses [28, 30, 31] are combined, and categorised into
three working points in this analysis: “resolved VH(bb¯) regime” and “boosted VH(bb¯) regime” tar￾geting the VH(H → bb¯) signal process, and “VH(cc¯) regime” targeting the VH(H → cc¯) process.
The different analysis regimes are separated by the dedicated reconstruction and identification
technique of the bottom and charm quark jets (flavour tagging), and separated by the transverse
momentum of the vector boson (pV
T) at 400 GeV. With the flavour tagging, the events in pV
T < 400
GeV are categorised orthogonally into the resolved VH(bb¯) regime and VH(cc¯) regime, as dis￾cussed in Section 5.1.3.1. For the events in pV
T > 400 GeV, the dedicated bottom quark jet tagging
is deployed in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, but the same flavour tagging used in pV
T < 400 GeV
is deployed in the VH(cc¯) regime, as discussed in Section 5.1.3.2, since dedicated charm quark
jet tagging in the boosted regime is not developed enough yet. The analysis techniques in the
resolved VH(bb¯) regime and the VH(cc¯) regime are harmonized as much as possible, based on
the idea that the only difference between these two regimes should be the flavour tagging. The
VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) analysis uses the leptonic decay modes of the vector boson. There are three dif￾ferent analysis channels targeting the number of charged leptons decayed from the vector boson*8;
Z → νν as ”0-lepton” channel, W → (ν as ”1-lepton” channel and Z → (( as ”2-lepton” channel,
where ( refers to electrons or muons. This thesis mainly explains the analysis techniques relating
to the VH(H → bb¯) signal process, including the constraint of the background process from the
VH(cc¯) regime.
1.4.2 Main objectives of this thesis
• Measurement of the signal strength and improvement of the precision:
The significance for the background-only hypothesis is also measured to evaluate signal
sensitivity. The properties of inclusive VH, H → bb¯ process and the separated WH, H → bb¯
and ZH, H → bb¯ processes are measured. This measurement is motivated by the purpose
[A.].
• Measurement of fiducial differential cross-section as functions of pV
T and jet multiplicity
STXS bins:
*8 Branching ration of a vector boson: Z → νν =∼ 20%, W → (ν =∼ 22%, and Z → (( =∼ 7%.

Fig. 1.14: Overview of the strategy of this analysis. Since the H → cc¯ decay mode have similar
backgrounds with the H → bb¯ decay mode, this analysis treats both processes simultaneously
so that a stronger constraint is imposed on the backgrounds in the sensitive analysis regions of
both processes. This analysis separates three channels according to the number of charged leptons

1.4.3 Analysis validation
The diboson processes of VZ(Z → bb¯)(V = W, Z and W → (ν, Z → ((/νν), which are
the background processes in this analysis, have the similar topology as the Higgs signal pro￾cesses VH(H → bb¯), where only the difference is the invariant mass of di-jet(bb¯): mZ = 91 GeV,
mH = 125 GeV. Therefore, the diboson processes VZ(Z → bb¯) can be a substitute for the Higgs
signal processes in this analysis. In order to validate the whole analysis methods, especially the
estimations for the other background processes, the signal strength and significance of the diboson
processes VZ(Z → bb¯) are measured before the measurements for the Higgs signal processes, by
supposing a hypothesis that the signal strengths of diboson processes are consistent with the SM
prediction.

1.5 My contributions in this thesis
I’m one of the leading and main analysers of the international analysis team for the
VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) analysis. I made leading progresses in the development study of the FSR
recovery (Chapter 7), which contributes to both of the analysis purpose [A.] and [B.], and almost
all modelling studies of the diboson background process (Chapter 10), which contributes to the
purpose [A.]. In addition, I contributed to the optimisation of the MVA input variables (Chapter
8) and the fine-tuning study of the final fit (Chapter 11).
Besides the studies of Higgs boson properties in the VH(H → bb¯) analysis, I contributed to the
operation and the development of the ATLAS experiment. In the Run 3 operation (Section 2.1),
I was in charge of the experts for the TGC system and L1 Muon trigger system of the end-cap
region, and contributed to the stable ATLAS operation as described in Section 2.2.5. Moreover, I
achieved successful progress in the development study of a new electronics system of the ATLAS
experiment for HL-LHC, which is described in detail in Appendix A.
1.6 Thesis composition
This thesis summarises a study of the VH(H → bb¯) process, and concludes by discussing the
results of the measurements and discrepancies between experimental observations and the SM
predictions.
Chapter 2 describes the setup of the experiment in this analysis. Chapter 3 describes the dataset
recorded in the experiment and the simulated samples used in this analysis. Chapter 4 describes
the reconstruction, identification and calibration of the objects: Electron, muon, tau, MET and jets.
Chapter 5 describes the event selection and event categorisation adopted in this analysis. Chapter 6
describes four kinds of jet energy corrections for the Higgs-candidate jets applied in this analysis.
Chapter 7 summarises studies of a Final State Radiation (FSR) jet emitted from the H → bb¯
decay for this analysis. Chapter 8 describes the MVA used in this analysis. Chapter 9 describes the
systematic uncertainties of this analysis including the experimental uncertainties and the modelling
uncertainties derived from the simulated samples. Chapter 10 summarises modelling studies for
diboson background processes and the detailed modelling uncertainties of the diboson processes
applied in this analysis. Chapter 11 describes the statistical treatment in the fit between the data
and simulated samples in this analysis. Chapter 12 presents the results and discussion, followed
by Chapter 13 to conclude this thesis.

=== PDF PAGE 34 ===
20
Chapter 2
The LHC-ATLAS experiment
This chapter describes the setup of the experiment in this analysis. Section 2.1 explains an acceler￾ator of protons, and Section 2.2 explains a detector to record the data of proton-proton collisions.
2.1 The Large Hadron Collider (LHC)
The LHC [23] is a particle accelerator at CERN*1 to study particle physics at high energy fron￾tier. The LHC ring lies in tunnel of 27 km in circumference.
The accelerator complex is shown in Figure 2.1. First of all, hydrogen atoms are ionised to
produce protons. These protons are injected into the linear accelerator LINAC2 and accelerated
up to 50 MeV. Then, proton bunches are formed and accelerated by several circular accelerators:
BOOSTER up to 1.4 GeV, Proton Synchrotron (PS) up to 26 GeV, and Super Proton Synchrotron
(SPS) up to 450 GeV. Finally, they are injected and accelerated in the LHC. There are four main
experiments in the LHC which are located at the collision points as shown in Figure 2.1: A Toroidal
LHC Apparatus (ATLAS) [19], Compact Muon Solenoid (CMS) [20], LHC beauty (LHCb), A
Large Ion Collider Experiment (ALICE). The ATLAS and CMS experiments mainly study proton￾proton (pp) collisions.
Figure 2.2 shows the schedule of LHC and the future plan: The LHC accelerator will be up￾graded to increase its instantaneous luminosity referred to as “High-Luminosity LHC (HL-LHC)”.
The VH(H → bb¯) analysis presented in this thesis uses the dataset of full Run2 experiment (2015
- 2018).
*1 EN:European Organization for Nuclear Research, FR:Organisation Europeenne pour la Recherche Nucl ´ eaire ´

2.2 The ATLAS detector
The ATLAS detector is a general purpose detector [19] to measure particles from pp collisions
in the ATLAS experiment. The ATLAS detector covers almost 4π solid angle with symmetric

=== PDF PAGE 37 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-ATLAS experiment 23
cylindrical geometry around the collision point. The ATLAS detector consists of inner track￾ing detectors enclosed by a thin superconducting solenoid magnet, electromagnetic and hadronic
calorimeters, and muon spectrometers integrated with two kinds of large superconducting toroidal
magnets as shown in Figure 2.3. Every sub-detector is structured with detectors separately in the
barrel region and in the end-cap region. The region nearby the beam pipe is called as forward
region.
Fig. 2.3: Cut-away view of the ATLAS detector [38].
These sub-detector components are integrated in hierarchical structure as shown in Figure 2.4 in
order to identify the type of particles from the IP. The inner tracking detectors detect charged parti￾cles, which are bent by the solenoid magnet, and reconstruct tracks of these particles, and measure
their momenta from the curvature. The details are described in Section 2.2.1. The electromag￾netic calorimeters detect mainly photons and electrons, and measure their energy and positions.
The hadronic calorimeters detect hadrons, and measure their energy and positions. The details of
both calorimeters are described in Section 2.2.2. The muon spectrometer detects charged particles
which penetrate all the internal detectors; in general, muons are detected. A muon is bent by the
solenoid and toroidal magnets. The muon spectrometer extrapolates a track of muon from the IP
and measures its momentum from the curvature. The details are described in Section 2.2.3. The
coverage and resolution of each sub-detector are summarised in Table 2.2. In addition, the trig￾ger and data acquisition system is summarised in Section 2.2.4, followed by the ATLAS control

=== PDF PAGE 38 ===
24 2.2 The ATLAS detector
system in Section 2.2.5. All the setups described in this section are for the Run 2 experiment.

of x-y plane (Barrel side) [39].

The ATLAS experiment uses a right-handed coordinate system, whose origin is defined at the
centre of the ATLAS detector. The beam direction defines the z-axis and the x-y plane is defined
as transverse to the beam direction. The positive x-axis points to the centre of the LHC ring, and
the positive y-axis points vertically upward. The azimuthal angle φ and the perpendicular length
from the z-axis R are defined to measure around the z-axis of beam line. The pseudorapidity η is
defined as the high energy limit of the rapidity y = (1/2) ln[(E + pz)/(E − pz)], denoted by,
η = lim
E,pz→∞

where E is the energy, pZ is the longitudinal momentum, and θ is the polar angle from the z-axis.
The distance of two objects in phase space is defined as ∆R = &
∆η2 + ∆φ2. The rapidity y is also
used for analysing massive objects such as jets. Considering the conservation of momentum, the
vector sum of transverse momentum pT = p sin θ in x-y plane should be zero after the collision.
The imbalance of the pT helps to see neutrinos, which are invisible in the ATLAS detector (Details
are in Section 4.5). Transverse energy ET = E sin θ in x-y plane is also defined.
2.2.1 Inner tracking detector (ID)
The inner tracking detector (ID) consists of high-resolution semiconductor pixel and strip detec￾tors in the inner part and straw-tube tracking detectors, and is immersed in a 2 T solenoid magnetic
field. The layout of these detectors is illustrated in Figure 2.5. This sub-detector system aims to
reconstruct tracks of charged particles.
Silicon Pixel detector (Pixel) and Insertable B-Layer (IBL) is the closest sub-detector system
to the IP. They consist of four cylindrical layers in the barrel region and three disks on each side of
the end-cap regions, where the IBL is implemented in the innermost layer and attached on the beam
pipe [40]. The pixel sensors have a size of 50×250 µm2 for the IBL and that of 50×400 µm2 for the
others. They are segmented in φ × z plane. The total number of readout channels is approximately
92 M.
SemiConductor Tracker (SCT) is a semiconductor strip detector and is located outside the
Pixels. The SCT is composed of four cylindrical layers in the barrel region and nine disks on each
side of the end-cap regions. The SCT consists of two 6.4 cm daisy-chained strip sensors with a
pitch of 80 µm. The total number of readout channels is approximately 6.3 M.
Transition Radiation Tracker (TRT) provides a large number of hits, which is typically 36 hits

=== PDF PAGE 40 ===
26 2.2 The ATLAS detector
Fig. 2.5: Cut-away view of the inner tracking detector [38].
per track, with the 4 mm diameter straw tubes covered within |η| < 2.0. The transition radiation
material is made of polypropylene. The TRT provides the hit positions in R × φ (z × φ) plane
in the barrel (end-cap) region with an accuracy of 130 µm per straw. The TRT consists of 73
straw planes in the barrel region and 80 straw planes on each side of the end-cap region. The total
number of readout channels is approximately 351 k. The detection of transition radiation photons
in a Xenon-based gas mixture of the straw tube enhances the electron identification performance.
2.2.2 Calorimeter
The calorimeter is placed outside the ID and consists of an electromagnetic (EM) calorimeter
and a hadronic calorimeter. The EM calorimeter measures the energy of electrons and photons
using electromagnetic showers with fine granularity, and the hadronic calorimeter measures the
energy of hadrons using hadronic showers with enough granularity to satisfy the physics require￾ments for Emiss
T measurement and jet reconstruction. The layout of all the calorimeters is illustrated
in Figure 2.6.
Liquid Argon (LAr) ElectroMagnetic (EM) calorimeter is a sampling calorimeter with a

=== PDF PAGE 41 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-ATLAS experiment 27
Fig. 2.6: Cut-away view of the calorimeter [38].
lead absorber and liquid Argon (LAr) active material. This detector covers |η| < 1.475 in the barrel
region, and 1.375 < |η| < 3.2 in the end-cap region. The lead absorber induces an EM shower
when an electron or photon passes through the detector, and the energy of EM shower is measured
in the LAr detector. The number of ionised particles in the LAr detector is proportional to the total
energy of the EM shower. Total thickness is > 22 radiation length (X0)*3 in the barrel region, and
> 24X0 in the end-cap region. This thickness limits the punch-through of the EM showers into the
hadronic calorimeter. In |η| < 1.8, a presampler detector is installed to correct energy loss before
the calorimeter, and has a finer granularity in the η direction to identify between a signal photon
and a di-photon pair decayed from π0. The total number of readout channels is approximately 110
k in the barrel region and 64 k in the end-cap region.
Tile hadronic barrel calorimeter (Tile) is the ATLAS hadronic calorimeter in the barrel region
covering |η| < 1.7. This is placed outside the LAr EM calorimeter. This calorimeter is composed of
steel as the absorber and scintillating tiles as the active material and segmented with three layers.
The total thickness is approximately 9.7 interaction lengths λ*4 at η = 0. This thickness limits
*3 A distance to reduce energy of an electron by 1/e.
*4 A distance to reduce the number of charged particles in a hadron shower by 1/e.

=== PDF PAGE 42 ===
28 2.2 The ATLAS detector
the punch-through of the hadronic showers into the muon system. The total number of readout
channels is approximately 6 k for barrel detector and 4 k for extended barrel detector.
LAr Hadronic End-cap Calorimeter (HEC) is the ATLAS hadronic calorimeter in the end￾cap region covering 1.5 < |η| < 3.2. This is placed outside the EM calorimeter sharing the same
LAr cryostats, and segmented into two independent wheels per end-cap side. This is a sampling
calorimeter with copper absorber plates for 25 mm and 50 mm, and with LAr active material in
8.5 mm gaps between the plates. The total thickness is approximately 9.7 λ [41]. The total number
of readout channels is approximately 5.6 k.
LAr EM/hadronic Forward Calorimeter (FCal) is placed on both sides of forward regions
covering 3.1 < |η| < 4.9. This consists of three modules per end-cap side: the innermost mod￾ule uses copper for electromagnetic measurement, the other two outer modules use tungsten for
measuring the energy of hadrons. The total thickness is approximately 10 λ. The total number of
readout channels is approximately 3.5 k.
2.2.3 Muon spectrometer
The muon detection system consists of trigger chambers and high-precision tracking chambers
incorporating with the large superconducting air-core toroidal magnets, which are illustrated in
Figure 2.7. In |η| < 1.4, a charged particle, mainly a muon, is bent in magnetic field of 0.2
- 2.5 T imposed by the eight large barrel toroidal magnets in the muon spectrometer area. In
1.6 < |η| < 2.7, a muon is bent in magnetic field of 0.2 - 3.5 T imposed by the eight small end-cap
toroidal magnets per end-cap side in the interval area between the inner muon spectrometer and
outer muon spectrometer. The combination of the barrel and end-cap toroidal magnets provides
magnetic reflection in 1.4 < |η| < 1.6. This system measures the momentum of muons by the
magnetic reflection of muon tracks using the difference from infinite momentum tracks (straight
lines). The trigger chambers are used to trigger the interesting physics events which are described
in Section 2.2.4, thus, the coarse position granularity and fine timing resolution are required for
their designs in order to detect a signal within proton bunch crossing space 25 ns [23]: Resistive
Plate Chambers (RPC) in the barrel region and Thin Gap Chambers (TGC) in the end-cap region.
The high-precision tracking chambers are used for precise muon trajectory reconstruction of the
triggered events: Monitored Drift Tube (MDT) in all the regions and Cathode Strip Chambers
(CSC) in the forward region.

=== PDF PAGE 43 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-ATLAS experiment 29
Fig. 2.7: Cut-away view of the muon spectrometer [38].
Monitored Drift Tube (MDT) consists of six or eight layers of drift tubes covering |η| < 2.7,
while the innermost layer covers |η| < 2.0. The drift tube is 30 mm diameter tube filled with a gas
mixture of Argon and CO2 (93:7) at ∼3 atmospheric pressure. The average position granularity
per tube is approximately 80 µm. The drift time is a maximum of 700 ns. The total number of
readout channels is approximately 354 k.
Cathode Strip Chambers (CSC) use multi-wire proportional chambers (MWPC) covering
2.0 < |η| < 2.7. The plane of CSC is filled with a gas mixture of Argon and CO2 (80:20). The
position granularity per plane is approximately 60 µm. The drift time is a maximum of 40 ns in a
timing resolution of ∼7 ns. The total number of readout channels is approximately 31 k.
Resistive Plate Chambers (RPC) consist of parallel electrode plates filled with gas mixture of
C2H2F4:Iso-C4H10:SF6 (94.7:5:0.3) in a 2mm gas gap. The RPC system covers |η| < 1.05 in the
barrel region. The readout pitch is 23 - 35 mm in the z-φ position. The position granularity is ∼10
mm in the z position, and ∼10 mm in the φ position. The timing resolution is ∼1.5 ns. The total

=== PDF PAGE 44 ===
30 2.2 The ATLAS detector
number of readout channels is approximately 373 k.
Thin Gap Chambers (TGC) use MWPC filled with gas mixture of CO2:n-pentan (55:45). The
TGC system covers 1.05 < |η| < 2.7 (1.05 < |η| < 2.4 is used for trigger) in the end-cap region and
is placed at both the inner and outer sides of the end-cap toroidal magnet. The position granularity
is 2 - 6 mm in the R position, and 2 - 3 mrad in the φ position. The timing resolution is ∼4 ns. The
total number of readout channels is approximately 318 k.
2.2.4 Trigger and data acquisition
The pp bunch crossing rate is 40 MHz. The cross-section of targeted physics events, such as
top quarks, weak bosons, or Higgs bosons, is 6 - 8 times smaller than the total inelastic scattering
cross-section in the pp collision. Therefore, a procedure system to select and record the interesting
physics events is required. This system is called as a trigger and data acquisition (TDAQ) system
[42]. The trigger system is composed of two phases: the First Level trigger (L1 trigger), and the
High Level Trigger (HLT). The overview of the ATLAS TDAQ system is illustrated in Figure 2.8.
L1 trigger is the first stage of the ATLAS trigger system and is performed in a hardware based
electronics exploiting an FPGA or an ASIC for quick decision. The event rate is reduced from 40
MHz to 100 kHz in a fixed latency of 2.5 µs. The L1 trigger makes a judgement with information
from the muon spectrometers (RPC, TGC) and from the calorimeters. In order to realise the quick
judgement, the L1 Muon trigger system calculates the muon pT by the position information in
coarse granularity. The L1 calorimeter trigger system uses a Cluster Processor (CP) for a lepton
and a photon, and uses a Jet/Energy-sum Processor (JEP) for a jet in parallel. At last, the Central
Trigger Processor (CTP) makes the final L1 judgement and issues a L1 Accept (L1A) signal to all
sub-detectors.
High Level Trigger (HLT) is the next stage of the ATLAS trigger system exploiting software
resources. The event rate is reduced from 100 kHz to 1 kHz. The HLT can recover the objects,
which are abandoned in the L1 trigger, by using the information from the whole sub-detectors.
All information of the sub-detectors is first buffered in derandomising memory in their Front￾End (FE) electronics in the ATLAS cavern. When the L1A signal is received, the accepted events
are sent to ReadOut System (ROS) in the counting room, which is placed about 100 m far away
from the cavern, by ReadOut Drivers (ROD) via optical links. After the HLT is performed, the data

=== PDF PAGE 45 ===
Chapter 2. The LHC-ATLAS experiment 31
Fig. 2.8: Overview of the trigger and data acquisition system [42].
is stored in a permanent storage of CERN’s data centre at an average of 1.2 GBytes per second.
2.2.5 Control and operation
The infrastructure is established to operate the ATLAS detector system.
Timing Trigger Control (TTC) system distributes the L1A signal and LHC’s 40.079 MHz
global clock and reset signals to the whole sub-detectors in fixed and synchronised clock phase. A
bunch crossing identification (BCID) is performed on every event in the whole sub-detector system
using the LHC’s 40.079 MHz global clock.
Detector Control System (DCS) controls and monitors the detector parameters, for example,
Low-Voltage electric current, High-Voltage electric current, gas and cooling system, magnetic
field, temperature, and humidity. The DCS also manipulates the communications between the
ATLAS sub-detectors and the other systems, such as the LHC accelerator, the CERN technical

=== PDF PAGE 46 ===
32 2.2 The ATLAS detector
services, the ATLAS magnets, and the detector safety system.
Operation organisation for the ATLAS detector The ATLAS detector is mainly controlled
and monitored at ATLAS Control Room (ACR) 24 hours a day and every day through the operation
period by organising man-power with a shift schedule. When some problems occur in a sub￾detector system, the people in the ACR call to experts of the corresponding sub-detector system,
and solve the problems as soon as possible, so that the ATLAS detector prevents from losing the
collision events delivered by the LHC.

Chapter 3
Data and simulated samples
The ATLAS data and the simulated samples are used for the estimation of background processes
and the prediction of event yields of the VH(H → bb¯) signal process. This chapter describes the
ATLAS data used in the VH(bb¯) analysis in Section 3.1, and the simulated samples in Section 3.2.
3.1 Data
The pp collision data used in this thesis were collected by the ATLAS detector in 2015 - 2018 at
→s = 13 TeV. Cumulative integrated luminosity as a function of data taking period is presented in
Figure 3.1. Total delivered luminosity from the LHC is 156 fb−1, in which 147 fb−1 was recorded
by the ATLAS detector. The luminosity is mainly measured by LUminosity Cherenkov Integrating
Detector (LUCID-2) forward detector [43]. As physics analyses require the recorded data which
pass standard data quality requirements in the ATLAS experiment [44], the actual data used in the
ATLAS physics analyses have the integrated luminosity of 140 ± 1.2 fb−1 [36], which passed the
standard data quality selections. The integrated luminosity of the data for the physics analyses in
2015, 2016, 2017, and 2018 is 3.24, 33.40, 44.63, and 58.79 fb−1, respectively [36].
There are multiple soft pp collisions in bunch crossings in addition to the targeting hard scatter￾ing. These extra interactions are called as “pile-up”. The mean number of interactions is different
in each bunch crossing (,µ-) according to the instantaneous luminosity*1. Figure 3.2 shows distri￾butions of ,µ-.
*1 The number of ,µ- was calculated by the mean value of µ = Lbunch × σinel./ fr [44] in each bunch and in a certain
period, where Lbunch is luminosity per proton bunch, σinel. is the inelastic cross-section (80 mb for 13 TeV pp
collisions), and fr is the LHC revolution frequency.

Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 35
3.2 Simulated samples
The physics events in the pp collisions detected in the ATLAS detector are simulated, and that
simulated events are used to describe the ATLAS data.
3.2.1 Simulation of physics events in the ATLAS experiment
The simulation of the physics events in the ATLAS experiment, generally, consists of the fol￾lowing two simulations: the simulation of physics processes in the pp collision and the pile-up
exploiting Monte Carlo (MC) technique [46], and simulation of hits and readout in the ATLAS
detector. Every simulation and calculation is described in detail in this section. The deliverable,
i.e. simulated sample, is also called as “MC sample”.
Simulation of physics process
Since complex physics phenomena have occurred in the pp collision, the simulation of the
physics processes in the pp collision is performed with the following event generation steps. Fig￾ure 3.3 illustrates the event generation step of tth¯ event as an example. A proton is considered as a
composite particle with partons (quarks and gluons) in high energy collisions. In the collision of
protons, partons from the proton interact with partons from the other proton. Among the parton in￾teractions, a hard scattering interaction induces the targeting physics process. The hard scattering
process can be predicted by factorising the computation into two parts: the perturbatively calcu￾lable part, corresponding to the Matrix Element (ME) using Parton Distribution Function (PDF),
and the perturbatively non-calculable part, corresponding to the Parton Shower (PS) and hadro￾nisation (fragmentation). The separation of two parts is parameterised with a factorisation scale
(µF). The PS and hadronisation are estimated by phenomenological models with parameters tuned
to match observed data. Initial State Radiation (ISR) is radiated from the hard scattering partons,
and Final State Radiation (FSR) is radiated from the decay process of the hard scattering event.
There are interactions with other partons from the same colliding protons as the hard scattering
partons, referred to as “Underlying Event (UE)”, which is modelled using data. The computations
of complex event generation are performed by several MC generators.
Calculation of cross-section
The number of events can be derived from cross-section and integrated luminosity, which is
given as
N = σ
'
Linst.dt, (3.1)

=== PDF PAGE 50 ===
36 3.2 Simulated samples
Fig. 3.3: Illustration of event generation step of tth¯ event in the pp collision [47]. h1, h2 denote the
colliding hadrons (protons). Blue lines represent partons from the colliding hadrons. Initial State
Radiation (ISR) is also shown, which is associated with the hard scattering parton. A noticeable
red blob represents the hard scattering interaction to produce the tth¯ process, and red lines and
blobs illustrate the decay of the top quarks and the Higgs boson. Final State Radiation (FSR) is
also shown in the hard scattering process, which is radiated from the decay process. A noticeable
purple blob denotes the interaction with other partons from the same colliding hadrons as the hard
scattering partons, which is referred to as “Underlying Event (UE)”. Small light green blobs and
dark green marks illustrate the hadronisation. Yellow lines represent the radiation of photons.
where Linst. represents instantaneous luminosity defined in Eq. (2.1). The details of integrated
luminosity used in this thesis are described in Section 3.1. In the calculation of cross-section (σ),
a physics process in the pp collision as discussed above is considered.
Parton Distribution Function (PDF) describes the distribution of momentum carried by a parton
contributing to collisions; defined as a probability fi(xi,µF), where xi is a momentum fraction of
parton i over proton momentum P and µF is a factorization scale. The factorisation scale (µF)
separates the perturbatively calculable part and non-calculable part [48]. The energy scale is pa-

=== PDF PAGE 51 ===
Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 37
rameterised by the µF. The PDF is determined by experimental data of deep-inelastic scattering
(DIS) and related hard scattering data [49]. Thus, the cross-section in the proton-proton collision
for a certain process pp → X can be written as
σpp→X =
%

' 1

dxi
' 1

dxj fi(xi,µF)fj(xj,µF)σij→X(xiP, xjP,µR,µF), (3.2)
where σij is the cross-section of hard scattering process with initial state of partons i and j.
The cross-section of σij→X is calculated mainly by QCD; the precision of calculation is tiered
as Leading Order (LO), Next to Leading Order (NLO), and Next-to-Next Leading Order (NNLO)
in ascending order. Two kinds of QCD scale variations should be considered; factorisation scale
(µF) and renormalisation scale (µR). The strong coupling constant in the ME is subject to quantum
corrections, such as corrections to the quark and gluon self-energies. The renormalisation scale
(µR) removes infinities in the quantum correction. The value of µR varies with energy scale. These
QCD scale variations would be one of the major modelling uncertainties in general.
As shown in Figure 3.4, the proton-proton cross-sections as a function of the centre of mass
energy (parameterised by µF,µR in Eq. (3.2)).
Fig. 3.4: Cross-section of the Higgs production modes as a function of the centre of mass energy
at the LHC [26]. The cross-section is calculated with the Higgs mass as 125 GeV. As the energy
increases, the cross-section also increases.
Simulation of pile-up

=== PDF PAGE 52 ===
38 3.2 Simulated samples
All the simulated samples include the effect of pile-up from multiple interactions in the same
bunch crossing as well as the contributions from neighbouring crossings. This is achieved by
overlaying minimum bias events, simulated using Pythia 8, using A3 tune and interfaced with
NNPDF 2.3 PDFs. In addition, all events of the simulated samples are reweighted to match these
MC samples to the data in terms of the ,µ- in Figure 3.2 [50, 51].
Simulation of hits in the ATLAS detector
Afterwards, the simulation of hits in the ATLAS detector is performed. Both signal and back￾ground simulated processes are passed through the ATLAS detector simulation [52], and digitised
to emulate hits and readout on every sub-detector, based on Geant4 [53]. After that, the objects
such as leptons, photons, and jets are reconstructed with the ATLAS offline reconstruction software
in the identical way to reconstruct the data hits. The “truth” information of simulated processes*2
is also stored in the MC samples so that it can be used in the physics analysis.
3.2.2 Overall set-up of simulated samples
Most of the signal and background processes are simulated using MC samples, except for the
multi-jet background in the 1-lepton channel, and the top-quark background process in the 2-lepton
channel of the resolved VH(H → bb¯) analysis. These exceptional background processes are esti￾mated using data-driven techniques. In order to verify the theoretical modelling of the simulated
samples, besides a nominal MC sample serves as a baseline of a physics process simulation, alter￾native MC samples derived by alternative calculation methods are also prepared for each process,
which is described in detail in Section 9.2.1. This chapter shows only the nominal samples of
all the processes. Table 3.1 lists the matrix element generators, parton showers generators, and
parton distribution function (PDF) releases used for the nominal samples of simulated events. The
cross-sections corresponding to each process are also shown. The cross-sections are computed
from the best theoretical predictions, except for the diboson process of which the cross-section
is derived from the nominal MC generator. The simulated samples, except those generated with
Sherpa , exploit the EvtGen 1.6.0 program [54] to describe properties of the bottom and charm
hadron decays.
The detailed configurations of simulated samples for the signal and background processes are
in Section 3.2.3 and Section 3.2.4 respectively.

3.2.3 Signal process
All qq-initiated production processes are simulated using the Powheg generator with the MiNLO
(Multiscale Improved Next-to-Leading Order) procedure applied [55], interfaced to the Pythia MC
generator (Pythia 8.245 for the quark-induced processes, Pythia 8.307 for the gluon-induced pro￾cesses) [56] for the modelling of parton shower (PS), underlying event (UE) and multiple parton
interactions (MPI), applying the AZNLO tune [57] with the NNPDF3.0NLO PDF [58] for the
matrix elements (ME), and then reweighted to the PDF4LHC15NLO PDF set. The gg-initiated
ZH contribution relies on the Powheg prediction interfaced with Pythia 8 at Leading Order (LO).
Additional electroweak (EW) corrections at NLO are applied to the qq → VH processes as a
function of the transverse momentum of the vector boson [59]. These corrections are applied as

=== PDF PAGE 54 ===
40 3.2 Simulated samples
reweightings of the event yields, and they are computed with Hawk [60, 61].
The signal cross-sections are obtained from the LHC Higgs Working Group prescriptions for
a Higgs boson mass of mH = 125 GeV [26]. The inclusive cross-sections for WH and ZH are
calculated at Next-to-Next-to-Leading Order (NNLO) in QCD [62] and Next-to-Leading Order
(NLO) in EW [59]. The computation of the one-loop amplitudes is performed with the GoSam
automated software [63].
The contribution of the signal process in analysis regions is described in Section 9.2.2, and the
signal prediction is explained in Section 11.1.
3.2.4 Background process
There are five kinds of background processes in this thesis: vector boson + jets (V+jets) pro￾duction, top-pair production, single top production, diboson production, and multi-jet production.
Their contributions in analysis regions and background estimations are described in Section 9.2.3.
3.2.4.1 V+jets
The production of a single vector (V = W or Z) boson in association with jets is one of the main
backgrounds for all the lepton channels considered in this analysis. The Feynman diagrams of the
V+jets process are illustrated in Figure 3.5. The V+jets processes are simulated with Sherpa 2.2.11
[64]. In Sherpa 2.2.11, NLO-accurate Matrix Element (ME) for up to two jets and LO-accurate
ME for between three and five jets are calculated in the five-flavour scheme using the Comix [65]
and OpenLoops [66, 67, 68] libraries. The b- and c-quarks are treated as massless at the ME
level and massive in the Parton Shower (PS). The default Sherpa PS [69] and hadronisation based
on Catani–Seymour dipole factorisation and cluster-hadronisation model [70] is used. The built￾in Hessian NNPDF3.0NNLO PDF set [58] is used. The default Underlying Event (UE) tune of
Sherpa is used. The samples are generated using a dedicated set of tuned parameters developed by
the Sherpa authors. The NLO matrix elements for a given jet multiplicity are matched to the PS
using a variant of the MC@NLO algorithm [71]. Different jet multiplicities are then merged into
an inclusive sample using an improved CKKW matching procedure [72, 73], which is extended to
NLO accuracy using the MEPS@NLO prescription [74]. The merging cut parameter Qcut, which
is a criterion to allow jets initiated from the ME partons when Q > Qcut, is set to 20 GeV. In
addition, flavour filtering is used in the sample generation to ensure sufficient statistics for events
with b-, c-jets. The cross-section is calculated at NNLO QCD accuracy [75].
In addition, virtual loop NLO electroweak (EW) corrections are available for Sherpa 2.2.11,
with three different NLO QCD and NLO EW combination schemes [76, 77]. The additive

=== PDF PAGE 55 ===
Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 41
scheme shows the smallest deviation from NLO QCD-only prediction and is thus chosen as the
Sherpa 2.2.11 EW-corrected new nominal. The multiplicative and exponentiated schemes have
larger deviations and are used as sources of modelling uncertainties.
Fig. 3.5: Feynman diagram of V+jets processes. (a) shows the W+jets process and (b) shows the
Z+jets process.
3.2.4.2 Top-pair
The nominal MC sample for the tt
¯process is generated with the Powheg generator using an NLO
calculation for the ME [78, 79] and interfaced to Pythia 8.230 [80] with NNPDF3.0NLO PDFs
and the A14 set [81] of parameters for the PS, hadronisation, and UE description. In addition,
filtering at the generator level is applied during production to enhance statistics. The top-pair
cross-sections are computed assuming a top quark mass of 172.5 GeV [1] at NLO+NLL(the next￾to-leading logarithmic) accuracy. The Feynman diagrams of the top-pair process are illustrated in
Figure 3.6.
Fig. 3.6: Feynman diagram of top-pair process.
In the 2-lepton channel, a data-driven method is used to estimate the total top-quark background
(tt
¯ + single-top), since the top-quark dominant (top control) region with enough statistics can be

=== PDF PAGE 56 ===
42 3.2 Simulated samples
found when e and µ lepton pair events are selected and the other event selections in the signal
sensitive region in the 2-lepton (Section 5.1) are applied. More details about the 2-lepton top
control region are available in Section 5.2.
3.2.4.3 Single-top
Single-top processes are generated for the different channels (s-channel, t-channel, Wt-channel)
using the Powheg MC generator for the hard scattering prediction with NNPDF3.0NLO PDFs,
interfaced with Pythia 8.230 using the A14 tune for the simulation of PS, UE and MPI. The Feyn￾man diagrams of the single-top processes are illustrated in Figure 3.7. The cross-sections for s
and t channel production are calculated with the MCFM program [82] at NNLO in QCD using a
top-quark mass of 172.5 GeV [1]. The fiducial Wt channel production cross-section is calculated
with approximate NNLO accuracy from NNLL soft-gluon resummation [83, 84]. In the case of
Wt production, some overlap with tt
¯ production occurs. At NLO in QCD, these processes have
overlapping diagrams, and it is no longer possible to keep them formally apart. The diagram sub￾traction (DS) scheme [85] is used to handle the overlap with tt
¯pair production by removing doubly
resonant diagrams from the Wt sample, i.e. where both top quarks are on-shell. A comparison with
the alternative diagram removal (DR) scheme is used to assess systematic uncertainties. The DS
scheme showed better agreement of MC with the data in the boosted VH(H → bb¯) regime and is
thus used as the nominal sample.
Fig. 3.7: Feynman diagram of single-top processes. (a) shows the s-channel process, (b) shows the
t-channel process, and (c) shows the Wt-channel process.
3.2.4.4 Diboson
The diboson background consists of three production processes: WW, WZ, and ZZ events. The
nominal simulation of these processes relies on the Sherpa 2.2.11 MC generator for the qq-initiated
and on Sherpa 2.2.2 for the gg-initiated processes. In both cases, the NNPDF3.0NNLO PDF set
is used for both the ME calculation and the PS. The default UE tune of Sherpa is used. For the

=== PDF PAGE 57 ===
Chapter 3. Data and simulated samples 43
virtual loop NLO EW corrections, the additive scheme is adopted as a new nominal sample. The
generator used for the nominal samples provides the diboson cross-sections at the NLO accuracy
in QCD. The Feynman diagrams of the diboson processes are illustrated in Figure 3.8. The details
of the diboson modelling study are summarised in Chapter 10.
Fig. 3.8: Feynman diagram of diboson process. Only the qq-initiated processes are shown.
3.2.4.5 Multi-jet
Multi-jet events can contribute to the background due to muons or electrons from heavy-flavour
hadron decays, or jets faking leptons. A small fraction passes the lepton isolation requirements, but
the production cross-section is large*3. Thus, the contribution of the multi-jet should be considered.
Using MC simulations to achieve good modelling of this background is very difficult, mostly
because the fraction of events that pass the selection criteria is very limited, and the available
simulations do not ensure enough statistics in this phase space. Therefore, data-driven template
methods are used to extract the multi-jet contributions as discussed in Section 9.2.3. The multi-jet
templates are only considered in 1-lepton in the resolved VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) regimes,
since their contributions are found to be negligible in the other regions.
*3 It’s almost eight orders of magnitude larger than the Higgs boson cross-section.

=== PDF PAGE 58 ===
44
Chapter 4
Objects reconstruction and identifi￾cation
This analysis uses the reconstructed objects: charged tracks, primary vertex, electrons, muons,
missing transverse momentum and jets. This chapter describes the reconstruction and identifica￾tion of these objects in Section 4.1 - 4.3 and 4.5, and the identification of b-, c-jets in Section 4.4.
Overlap removals between different objects are described in Section 4.6.
4.1 Track and primary vertex
A charged particle passes through and deposits hits in the inner tracking detectors. A cluster
is reconstructed with the bunch of hits in the pixel, and a tracklet is made by the hits in the SCT.
The cluster and the tracklet are fitted and assembled to a charged track. After that, the charged
track is extrapolated to the hits in the TRT in another fit. In the procedure of fitting the track, bad
reconstructed tracks are rejected [86]. The tracks reconstructed with inner tracking detectors are
referred to as ID tracks.
Interaction vertices are reconstructed with at least two of these tracks [87], called as a “primary
vertex” (PV). A PV with the highest sum of transverse momentum of its tracks is selected as the
PV corresponding to the hard scattering event, among ∼60 PVs in the same pp collision event.
Electrons, muons, and jets should be originated from the selected PV to suppress the background
objects from pile-up collisions. Relative position between a track and the selected PV is defined
as follows:
• d0: minimum distance of a track to the selected PV in the x-y plane
• z0: minimum distance of a track to the selected PV along the z-axis

=== PDF PAGE 59 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identification 45
The value of d0 is evaluated by the standard deviation of the d0, referred to as ”d0/σd0 ”. The z0 is
usually evaluated by |z0 sin θ| in order to make this variable independent of the polar angle.
4.2 Leptons
Electrons are reconstructed and selected based on requirements for the properties of ID tracks
and the energy deposit matched to the corresponding track in the EM calorimeters. The selection
criteria for electrons are defined in Tables 4.1, which differ depending on the requirements of the
analysis lepton channels. After the reconstruction, prompt electrons from hard scattering vertex
are identified and isolated from electrons from semi-leptonic decays of hadronic sources or jet￾faking electrons (“non-prompt electrons”). The candidate electrons are identified and isolated
with likelihood discrimination, of which this analysis adopts “Loose quality” or “Tight quality”
identification (ID) working points, and “Loose quality” or a tighter quality of “HighPtCaloOnly”*1
isolation working points [88]. After the identification and isolation, the efficiency of candidate
electrons is scaled to match the data in the calibration with the Z → ee and J/ψ → ee events
[88]. The scale factors are calculated from the ratio of the efficiency measured between the MC
simulation and the data. Different selection levels are required depending on the situation in the
0-, 1-, 2-lepton channels. In VH-Loose level, the criteria are defined to keep as many electrons
from the signal process as possible. The “Loose” isolation is applied to reduce jet-faking electrons,
and that working point keep 99 % efficiency for prompt electrons from hard scattering vertex [88].
In ZH-Signal level for the 2-lepton channel, tighter criteria for the lepton pT is applied. In WH￾Signal level for the 1-lepton channel, a much tighter selection is applied to suppress the multi-jet
background. The tighter calorimeter-based isolation is applied with 95 % signal efficiency [88].
Table 4.1: Electron selection requirements
Electron selection level pT η ID [88] d0/σd0 |z0 sin θ| Isolation [88]
VH-Loose >7 GeV |η| < 2.47 Loose quality < 5 < 0.5 mm Loose quality
ZH-Signal >27 GeV |η| < 2.47 Loose quality < 5 < 0.5 mm Loose quality
WH-Signal >27 GeV |η| < 2.47 Tight quality < 5 < 0.5 mm HighPtCaloOnly
Muons are reconstructed and selected with the information from the inner tracking detectors
and the muon spectrometers. The selection criteria for muons are defined in Table 4.2, which
differ depending on the requirements of the analysis lepton channels. After the reconstruction,
*1 This electron isolation uses only the variables of EM calorimeters

46 4.3 Jets
prompt muons from hard scattering vertex are identified and isolated from muons from semi￾leptonic decays of hadronic sources or jet-faking muons (“non-prompt muons”). The candidate
muons are identified and isolated with likelihood discrimination, of which this analysis adopts
“Loose quality” or “Medium quality” identification (ID) working points, and “Loose quality” or
a tighter quality of “HighPtTrackOnly”*2 isolation working points [89]. After the identification
and isolation, the efficiency of candidate muons is scaled to match the data in the calibration with
the Z → µµ and J/ψ → µµ events [90, 89]. The scale factors are calculated from the ratio
of the efficiency measured between the MC simulation and the data. Different selection levels
are required depending on the situation in the 0-, 1-, 2-lepton channels. In VH-Loose level, the
criteria are defined to keep as many muons from the signal process as possible, and to reject pile￾up events and cosmic muons. The “Loose quality” of ID working point selects a default muon,
and a “CalTagged” muon. The CalTagged muon is reconstructed from an ID track with an energy
deposit in the calorimeter as a minimum ionising particle in |η| < 0.1 where is a gap of the muon
spectrometers [89]. The “Loose quality” of the muon isolation reduces non-prompt muons, then
the isolation realises 99 % efficiency for the signal muons [89]. In ZH-Signal level, the same
tighter criterion is added as the electrons above. In WH-Signal level for the 1-lepton channel, a
much tighter selection is applied to suppress the multi-jet background. The CalTagged muon is not
included in the Medium quality muon ID working point. A tighter track based isolation is applied
with 95 % signal efficiency [89].
