4.3 Jets
A hadron containing quarks and gluons is observable, while a quark or gluon itself is unable to
be observed as a single particle because of the confinement effect of QCD. In the fragmentation
process, a bunch of hadrons can be observed, and it inherits the kinematic information of a quark
or gluon. Then, the bunch of hadrons is reconstructed as a cluster in the calorimeter and as tracks
*2 This muon isolation uses only the variables of the inner tracking detectors

=== PDF PAGE 61 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identification 47
in the ID detectors. This object is called “jet”.
Three different jets are used in this analysis: small-R jet for the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯)
regimes, large-R jet and Variable-R(VR) track jet for the boosted VH(bb¯) regime.
Small-R jet
Small-R jets are reconstructed from a particle-flow (PFlow) algorithm [91], which combines
energy deposits in the calorimeter and tracks in the inner tracking detector. The PFlow objects are
clustered as a jet exploiting anti-kt algorithm [92] with a distance parameter of R = 0.4. The anti-kt
algorithm forms clusters of soft (low momentum) constituents first to detach the soft constituents
from hard constituents, which helps to assess soft jets such as Initial State Radiation (ISR) jets.
Once the jets are reconstructed, the jets need to be adjusted according to several effects: detector
effects such as the difference between the EM and hadronic calorimeters, the energy leakage of the
jet in the calorimeter, and reconstruction effects such as energy deposited outside the jet cone, and
the jets from pileup events. Jets are adjusted by applying a jet energy scale (JES) [93, 94]. The
derivation and calibration of JES are conducted in the following steps:
1. PV correction: The jet axis is corrected to point toward the selected PV in the corre￾sponding event, while it originally points to the detector centre. The four-vector of jet is
recalculated. The jet energy is not changed.
2. Pile-up correction: The energy deposits by the pile-up contribution are subtracted from
the measured energy deposits in the calorimeters. The corresponding energy to be sub￾tracted is calculated by the transverse energy density, which is measured in the pile-up
events beforehand.
3. MC-based calibration: The JES is also derived from the jet energy response in MC￾based calibration. The jet energy response R is computed by comparing the reconstructed
jet energy Ereco. and the “truth” jet energy Etruth in MC simulation of di-jet events, given
as R = Ereco./Etruth, as functions of jet energy and jet η. Here, the “truth” jet is formed
with “truth” particles in the MC simulation by the PFlow algorithm, and the “truth” jets are
geometrically matched to the corresponding reconstructed jet within ∆R = 0.3.
4. Global Sequential Calibration (GSC): The jet energy response varies depending on a
jet-initiating particle, e.g. the flavour of quark or gluon. To distinguish the calibration of
the jet energy response between them, additional kinematic variables that can characterise
these jets are used, such as the fraction of the measured jet pT from the tracks associated
with the jet.
5. “In-situ” calibration: The final calibration is applied to the data to account for the

=== PDF PAGE 62 ===
48 4.3 Jets
possible differences between the reconstructed jets in the data and the “truth” jets in the
MC samples, referred to as “in-situ” calibration. These differences are caused by imperfect
simulations of the detector and the physics processes. The pT ratio is measured between jets
and well-calibrated reference objects, e.g. Z bosons, photons, and low pT jets, in both the
data and MC samples, to extract the correction as functions of jet pT and η. This calibration
consists of three steps. Firstly, the energy of jets in the forward region (0.8 < |η| < 4.5)
is corrected based on the well-measured jets in the central region (|η| < 1.4) using di-jet
events, referred to as “η inter-calibration”. Secondly, by balancing the pT of jets and the
reference objects, the measurements of Z+jets (Z → (() and γ+jets events provide the
calibration in |η| < 0.8, in 20 < p
jet
T < 400 GeV and in 40 < p
jet
T < 950 GeV, respectively.
Finally, a single high-pT jet is calibrated by using a pT balance with the referenced low-pT
jet in multi-jet events with high-pT jets up to 2.4 TeV.
Moreover, following the JES calibration, the jet energy resolution (JER) σpT /pT is calibrated by
the “in-situ” technique with the data and di-jet simulated samples [95]. In addition to the standard
jet calibrations, several jet energy corrections are applied to improve the invariant mass of two
b-,c-jets, exploiting the topological features in the H → bb¯/cc¯ decay processes, as described in
Chapter 6.
Jets are classified as “signal” or “forward” jets with the selection requirements in Table 4.3.
As the tracking information is necessary for flavour tagging, as described in Section 4.4, only the
signal jets are used in the Higgs boson reconstruction. Both the signal and forward jets are used in
the counting of jet multiplicity for event categorisation in Section 5.2.
In order to suppress jets from pileup events, the information of the selected hardest primary
vertex, jet pT, and track pT is used with a multivariate likelihood, which is called Jet Vertex Tagger
(JVT) [96]. The JVT is applied for jets with |η| < 2.5 and pT < 60 GeV. The JVT achieves ∼ 90 %
efficiency with ∼ 1 % fake rate of pileup events. Because of the lack of tracking information, for
the forward jet with pT < 120 GeV, a forward Jet Vertex Tagger (fJVT) [97] is applied.
Jets have a possibility to come from calorimeter noise or from beam-induced background. These
jets can be suppressed by cut-based selection in Jet Cleaning [98] method: “Tight” quality realises
rejection of non-collision background with an efficiency of selecting jets from proton-proton col￾lisions above 95 % (99.5 %) for pT > 20 (100) GeV.
Unless there is an explicit statement about the term “jet”, a “jet” refers to a Small-R jet.
Large-R jet

=== PDF PAGE 63 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identification 49
Large-R jets are formed with clusters detected in the calorimeter exploiting the anti-kt algorithm
with a radius parameter of R = 1.0.
The large-R jets pass through several simulation-based calibration algorithms [99]. First, the
Jet Energy Scale (JES) is corrected to the particle-level scale using matched truth-jet collections.
Afterwards, any offset in η is corrected in the same way. Both corrections are applied as a function
of the reconstructed jet energy and η. Since the jet mass is especially sensitive to wide-angled
soft radiation rather than the total jet energy, the Jet Mass Scale (JMS) is explicitly calibrated
in a last step using simulated di-jet events [99]. For further validation and estimation on the
uncertainties associated with the calibration procedure, JES and JMS are also calibrated with data
by the “in-situ” technique. This “in-situ” calibration results in better agreement between the data
and simulated samples. Jet Energy Resolution (JER) and Jet Mass Resolution (JMR) are also
calibrated by the “in-situ” technique in di-jet events.
The Large-R jets are used in the reconstruction of the Higgs boson in the boosted VH(H → bb¯)
regime.
Variable-R(VR) track jet
Tracks from the inner tracking detector with pT > 0.5 GeV and |η| < 2.5 form the inputs to
VR track jet clusters. Then, the VR track jets are formed by clustering tracks with the anti-kt
algorithm. In an iterative reconstruction algorithm, a variable R as a function of jet pT is defined
as R(pT) = ρ/pT, where ρ is a parameter to decide the effective jet size according to the track
VR jet pT [100]. R(pT) has an upper limit (Rmax) and lower limit (Rmin). The VR track jets are
reconstructed with parameters {ρ = 30 GeV; Rmax = 0.4; Rmin = 0.02}, which have been optimized
for b-tagging efficiency in the boosted VH(H → bb¯) regime [101]. The selection requirement of
the VR track jet is shown in Table 4.4.
The VR track jets are used in MVA final discrimination, flavour tagging of jets, and the event
categorisation to set a top enriched control region in the boosted VH(H → bb¯) regime. Since the
momentum of VR track jet is not used in Higgs boson reconstruction, no additional uncertainty of
their momentum nor energy is considered in this analysis. The uncertainties of flavour tagging are
expected to cover most effects of the uncertainties from the track jet reconstruction.
4.4 Flavour tagging of jets
There are the b or c quark initiated jets (b-,c-jets) from the Higgs boson in the signal processes,
meanwhile there are also jets initiated from the other quarks or gluons. The quark flavour identifi-

4.4.1 Identification of several jets
The b- and c-jets have a unique feature to have a secondary vertex (SV) and an impact parameter
(d0) to show the distance of the hard scattering primary vertex (PV) and the extrapolated point
from the SV. The transverse distance of the hard scattering PV and SV varies flavour-by-flavour
according to their lifetime: a b-quark has a relatively long lifetime for 1.5 ps (flying 5 mm in the
ID), a c-quark (forming D-meson) has a short lifetime for 0.2 - 1.0 ps. Especially, since ∼ 90 % of
b-quark decays into c-quark due to the CKM matrix |Vcb|
2 . |Vub|
2, b-jet contains a c-hadron at a
few mm far from the SV, which results in that the b-jet has multiple vertices. Figure 4.1 illustrates
these unique features.
The identification of b- or c-jets is performed based on the associated tracks reconstructed in
the ID and primary vertex reconstruction. The final flavour discriminant is based on the DL1 algo￾rithms, a deep feed-forward neural network, using low-level tagger information including impact￾parameter based algorithms (IP2D/IP3D), secondary vertex SV1, multi-vertex decay-chain fitter
(JetFitter), recurrent neural network track-based tagger (RNNIP) as well as the jet kinematic in-

=== PDF PAGE 65 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identification 51
Fig. 4.1: Several unique features of the b-,c-jets. An impact parameter (d0) shows the distance of
the hard scattering PV and the extrapolated point from the SV.
formation as the input variables, referred to as ”DL1r” tagger [102, 103]. In the previous analysis
[12, 31], the final flavour discriminant is performed with multi-variant analysis with the same input
variables, referred to as “MV2c10”. Figure 4.2 shows the c-jet and light-flavour jet rejection as
a function of the b-tagging efficiency. Comparing the background rejection performance at 70 %
b-tagging efficiency between the previous MV2c10 tagger and the current DL1r tagger, especially
the c-jet rejection improved ∼ 200 %.
In the simulation samples, the jet truth flavour is labelled using a match to weakly decaying b/c￾hadron with pT ≥ 5 GeV within a cone of size ∆R = 0.3. Once a single b-hadron is assigned to a
jet, it is excluded from the list for further associations. Thus, overlap tagging on one reconstructed
jet won’t happen. If the jet cannot be labelled as neither b nor c, it will be labelled as a light jet.
The b-,c-jets are referred to as “heavy flavour” jets, and the jets originated from other quarks
such as strange quark are referred to as “light flavour” jets.
4.4.2 Tagging scheme
Because the c-tagging performance is not good enough to apply in the boosted regime (pV
T > 400
GeV), application schemes differ between the resolved (resolved VH(bb¯),VH(cc¯)) regimes, and the
boosted (VH(bb¯)) regimes.

=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
52 4.4 Flavour tagging of jets
Fig. 4.2: Background rejection of b-tagging comparing between MV2c10 and DL1r (blue line)

4.4.2.1 Scheme in resolved regime
PFlow small-R signal jets are used for b- or c-tagging. To harmonise the flavour tagging process
in the resolved VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) regimes orthogonally, a PseudoContinuous Flavour
Tagging (PCFT) scheme is defined and optimised for c-tagging working points besides the standard
b-tagging working points. The PCFT scheme has 2-dimensional working points with 2 b-tagging
bins and 2 c-tagging bins and one untagged bin for the light jets as denoted in Figure 4.3. These
working points are defined as cuts based on the b-tagging DL1r-based score(DL1rb,(4.1)) or c￾tagging DL1r-based score (DL1rc,(4.2)) as shown in Table 4.5. The values of the DL1r-based
score in Table 4.5 are decided based on the flavour tagging efficiency: 60 %(Tight), 70 %(Loose)

=== PDF PAGE 67 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identification 53
for b-tagging, and 20 %(Tight,CT), 40 %(Loose,CL) for c-tagging.
DL1rb = ln { pb
fc pc + (1 − fc)pu
)
(fc = 0.018), (4.1)
DL1rc = ln { pc
fb pb + (1 − fb)pu
)
(fb = 0.3), (4.2)
where pb, pc, pu are the probabilities of b-, c-, light flavour jets produced by the DL1r training,
and fb, fc is a weight to apply the probabilities of b-, c-jets respectively. fb = 0.3 is fixed for the
b-tagging in the ATLAS experiment, and fc = 0.018 is optimized for this analysis.
As a cross-check, the tagging efficiencies of b-,c-, and light flavour jets are measured in the
different working points with tt
¯simulated samples by Powheg +Pythia 8, which are presented in
Table 4.6. These PCFT bins are used in the event selection in Section 5.1 and the input variables
of final discriminant in Chapter 8.
Fig. 4.3: A PseudoContinuous Flavour Tagging (PCFT) scheme. PCFT bins are separated based on
the flavour tagging efficiencies. Two bins are prepared for both b-tagging and c-tagging working
points.

(PseudoContinuous b-Tagging, PCBT) is defined with b-tagging DL1r-based score. In the PCBT
scheme, four working points are defined as cuts based on the b-tagging score (4.1). The working

4.4.3 Calibration
All five PCFT bins or all five PCBT bins are calibrated simultaneously to get a coherent heavy
flavour tagging scale factors and uncertainties.
All the scale factors are derived to correct the efficiency of different parton-shower simulation
samples to the data using the standard flavour tagging method [102, 104]. The calibrations for
the b- and c-jets are performed with di- and semi-leptonic tt
¯ events, and the calibrations of the
other tagged jets are performed with the Z+jets events because Z+jets have a significant amount

=== PDF PAGE 70 ===
56 4.4 Flavour tagging of jets
4.4.4 Maximising the statistics after flavour tagging selection
In a certain flavour tagging working point, the corresponding flavour tagged events are directly
selected, while the other flavour tagged events are not used. Because of this strong suppression of
the other flavour tagged event, the statistical uncertainty of the applicable events becomes large,
which is problematic in the training of the final discriminant in Chapter 8 and of the neural network
based shape uncertainty estimation in Section 9.2.1, and in the analysis phase spaces with low
statistics such as c-tagged, non-tagged bins, or the boosted VH(bb¯) regime.
In order to increase the statistics of available MC samples, a dedicated method is introduced,
referred to as “Truth tagging”. In the method, the tagging requirement is not applied to the MC
samples. Instead of the requirement, events are weighted by the probabilities (+tag ·+non−tag) of every
jet as given in (4.3). The tagging efficiencies (+) in the probabilities are derived by a Graphical
Neural Network (GNN) training approach [106] with the input training variables, denoted as x
(e.g. 4-momentum tracks information of jets, secondary vertices of jets). The tagging efficiencies
are also parameterised as a function of a set of variables θ (e.g. pT and η of the jets).
w =
%
C
i
+tag(Ti, x, θ) · +non−tag(T¯i, x, θ) (4.3)
where,
+tag(Ti, x, θ) =
*
j′Ti
+j(x|θ) (4.4)
+non−tag(T¯i, x, θ) =
*
j′T¯i
(1 − +j(x|θ)) (4.5)
Here, j denotes one of the reconstructed jets in the event. The set of tagged jets can be defined as
Ti, while the set of non-tagged jets can be defined as T¯i. C stands for all kinds of jet flavours. The
training models are prepared for each signal or background process separately to catch the topo￾logical features of the process. For validation, the distributions of MVA input variables and final
discriminant score is used. The distributions applying the tagging requirement and that applying
the Truth tagging method are almost consistent within their statistical uncertainties.
In the measurement of the signal strength and differential cross-section, the Truth tagging is
applied to the V+jets, s-,t-channel of single top quark samples in inclusive regime, and to the tt
¯
and Wt single top quark samples in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime. The Truth tagging is not applied
to signal samples and diboson samples, whose signal strengths are measured, so that the Truth
tagging doesn’t give some bias on the final results.

=== PDF PAGE 71 ===
Chapter 4. Objects reconstruction and identification 57
4.5 Missing Transverse Momentum (MET)
A missing transverse momentum (MET, Emiss
T ) plays a key role in the 0-lepton channel and the
1-lepton channel in this analysis, since a Z boson decays to two neutrinos in 0-lepton and a W
boson decays to one lepton and one neutrino.
The MET is reconstructed as the negative vector sum of the transverse momenta of the recon￾structed objects: electrons, muons, τhads, jets, and a “soft-term” [107]. The “soft-term” is derived
as the vector sum of the pT of well-qualified ID tracks which are not associated with a lepton nor
jet. Since the well-qualified ID tracks are associated with the PV, the “soft-term” is less sensitive
to the effects of pileup events.
Since the signal process of the 0-lepton channel has only two neutrinos decayed from a Z boson
in its final state, the transverse momentum of a Z boson is equivalent to Emiss
T . In the 1-lepton
channel, a W boson of the signal process is reconstructed with a pT of lepton and a MET. In
addition, the longitudinal momentum of a neutrino can be obtained by constraining the lepton and
neutrino system with the W boson mass.
4.6 Overlap between different objects in the same event
Because the object reconstructions are performed at the same time, the same response in the
detector may be treated in two different objects. The VH-loose electrons, VH-loose muons, τ
lepton, small-R jets, and large-R jets are considered. The overlap between the different objects is
removed as follows:
• tau-electron: If ∆R(τ, e) < 0.2, the τ lepton is removed.
• tau-muon: If ∆R(τ,µ) < 0.2, the τ lepton is removed. If the τ lepton has pT > 50 GeV and
the muon is a CaloTagged muon, then the τ lepton is not removed.
• electron-muon: If a default muon shares an ID track with an electron, the electron is
removed. If a CaloTagged muon shares an ID track with an electron, the muon is removed.
• muon-small-R jet: If a jet and a muon are close (Satisfying a condition {A. ∞ (B. ∈ (C. ∞
D.))}), then the jet is removed.
A. ∆R(jet,µ) < 0.2
B. the jet has less than three associated tracks with pT > 500 MeV
C. the pT ratio of the muon and jet is larger than 0.5
D. the ratio of the muon pT to the sum of pT of tracks with pT > 500 MeV associated with
the jet is larger than 0.7
For any surviving jets, if ∆R(jet,µ) < min(0.4, 0.04 + 10 GeV/pµ
T), the muon is only con-

=== PDF PAGE 72 ===
58 4.6 Overlap between different objects in the same event
sidered in muon-in-jet correction in Section 6.2, but not used in event selection or categori￾sation.
• tau-small-R jet: If ∆R(τ, jet) < 0.2, the jet is removed.
• electron-large-R jet: If ∆R(e, large−R jet) < 1.2, the fat jet is removed.
The small-R jets overlapped with a large-R jet are not removed, but only when the small-R jet
not overlapped with the Higgs-candidate in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, ∆R(large-R, small-R)>1,
the small-R jets are considered for the event categorisation.

=== PDF PAGE 73 ===
59
Chapter 5
Event selection and categorisation
This chapter describes event selection requirements in Section 5.1, and event categorisations in
Section 5.2.
5.1 Event selection
This analysis uses the events collected by the trigger as described in Section 5.1.1. After that,
this analysis selects events characterised by a vector boson and a Higgs boson. The criteria to
enhance events having a vector boson are described in Section 5.1.2 with their different event
topologies. The conditions to identify the Higgs candidate with pairs of either b- or c- jets are
described in Section 5.1.3. Table 5.2 and Table 5.3 in the end of this section summarise all the
event selection requirements in different regimes and lepton channels.
5.1.1 Trigger selection
According to the data taken period and information of the HLT and L1 system as explained
in Section 2.2.4, the several kinds of triggers are prepared. The trigger used in this analysis is
described in this section and summarised in Table 5.1.
As discussed in Section 1.2.3, this analysis uses a vector boson signature decaying into leptons
to get high signal sensitivity for the VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) processes. Since triggering the events with
leptons from a vector boson is the effective way to obtain the targeted physics events of this anal￾ysis, triggers by the missing transverse momentum (Emiss
T ), electrons, and muons are used. The
same trigger selections can be adopted to collect both the targeted VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) events. In the
events with muons from a vector boson (µ sub-channel) in the 1-, 2-lepton channel, the events col￾lected by the µ trigger and the Emiss
T trigger are overlapped. The Emiss
T trigger uses the observables

T trigger is used for selecting the events with µ leptons.

acceptance in both the 1-, 2-lepton channels.
For the 0-lepton channel, events are selected using the lowest Emiss
T triggers with online thresh￾olds of 70 GeV for the data recorded in 2015, of 90 and 110 GeV for the data recorded in 2016,
and of 110 GeV for the data recorded in 2017 and 2018.
For the 1-lepton channel, single electron events are required to be triggered by at least one of
the lowest single-electron triggers in the single e sub-channel. Events in the single µ sub-channel
are triggered using the same Emiss
T trigger for pV
T ≥ 150 GeV events as used in the 0-lepton channel
to obtain high trigger efficiency. For lower pV
T < 150 GeV events, the lowest single-muon triggers
are used.
For the 2-lepton channel, the triggers are similar to the ones in the 1-lepton channel, except in
the µ sub-channel, where the pV
T threshold for switching between muon and Emiss
T triggers is 250
GeV. This is because the single muon trigger efficiency is higher in the 2-lepton channel than in
the 1-lepton channel.
For a eµ control region, which is described in Section 5.2.2, the single electron triggers are
required first. Afterwards, the single muon triggers are required to the selected events. More
details on the triggers used in the Run2 experiment are summarised in Table E.1 in Appendix E.

=== PDF PAGE 75 ===
Chapter 5. Event selection and categorisation 61
5.1.2 Vector boson selection
5.1.2.1 0-lepton channel
Since the ZH, Z → νν, H → bb¯/cc¯ process is the target of this channel, the transverse mo￾mentum of two neutrinos is equivalent to Emiss
T , There is a signal process where ZH is in the
production, with Z decaying into neutrinos and the Higgs decaying into bb¯ (cc¯), resulting in a final
state containing zero VH-loose leptons, as defined in Section 4.2.
The Emiss
T is required to be greater than 150 GeV, with respect to the Emiss
T trigger thresholds (see
Section 5.1.1), where the trigger efficiency achieves about 90%. Trigger scale factors of 0.9 to 1.0
are applied in the low Emiss
T region to correct for the residual turn-on effects.
Furthermore, a scalar sum of the pT (S T) is required to be greater than 120 GeV for the 2-jet
region, 150 GeV for the 3 or more jet region. The regions classified based on the number of jets
are defined in Section 5.2.2. This criterion is designed to remove phase space that is associated
with uncertainties due to a non-trivial dependence of the trigger efficiency on the jet activity and
is also inherited from the previous analysis [28, 31].
5.1.2.2 1-lepton channel
Since the one lepton final state of the W → (ν process is considered, events with exactly one
WH-signal lepton and no additional VH-loose lepton, as defined in Section 4.2, are selected. The
pV
T is reconstructed as the vector sum of the Emiss
T and the lepton transverse momentum in pV
T > 75
GeV.
5.1.2.3 2-lepton channel
Since ee or µµ final states in the Z → (( process are considered, the Z boson can be reconstructed
as a system of two VH-loose leptons, as defined in Section 4.2. The two leptons are required to
have the same flavour, and at least one of them needs to satisfy the ZH-signal lepton requirements.
The leading electron needs to satisfy pT > 25 GeV, and the leading muon needs to satisfy pT >
27 GeV with respect to the trigger thresholds. For di-muon events, the two muons are further
required to be of opposite charge; the requirement is not applied to di-electron events due to a
higher rate of charge mis-identification. Furthermore, the invariant mass of the di-lepton system
must be consistent with the Z-boson mass: 81 < m(ee, µµ) < 101 GeV in the resolved VH(bb¯)
and VH(cc¯) regimes, 66 < m(ee, µµ) < 116 GeV in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime. This requirement
suppresses backgrounds having a non-resonant lepton-pair, such as tt
¯ and multi-jet processes.

=== PDF PAGE 76 ===
62 5.1 Event selection
5.1.3 Higgs-candidate selection
The Higgs boson is reconstructed with two reconstructed jets that pass jet flavour tagging in
Section 4.4. The two jet system for the Higgs boson reconstruction is called as “Higgs-candidate”;
the rest of the jets are called as “non-Higgs-candidate” jets. According to the flavour tagging
scheme, different selection requirements are applied to the resolved (VH(bb¯),VH(cc¯)) regimes and
the boosted regime.
5.1.3.1 Resolved regime
Events with multiple jets (Njet ≥ 2) are accepted to reconstruct the H → bb¯ process with two
small-R jets (pT > 20 GeV).
The Higgs-candidate jets are identified with two signal jets defined in Table 4.3, and tagged
by the PCFT scheme. Based on the PCFT bins (Section 4.4.2.1), the resolved VH(bb¯) regime
and VH(cc¯) regime are orthogonally defined, as shown in Figure 5.1. Events with exact two b￾tagged jets are categorised into the resolved VH(bb¯) regime (BB-tag region), while events with at
least one c-tight tagged jets are categorised into the VH(cc¯) regime (CTC, CTN-tag regions). The
two heavy flavour tagged*1 signal jets are referred to as the Higgs-candidate jets. Exceptionally,
events with only one c-loose tagged jet, and events with one b-tagged jet and one c-tight tagged
jet are categorised into the background dominant control regions, which are discussed in Section
5.2.2. The VH(H → bb¯) events distribute in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, while the VH(H → cc¯)
events distribute in the VH(cc¯) regime. Since this thesis focuses on the property measurements
of VH(H → bb¯) signal process, the VH(H → bb¯) signal prediction in the BB-tag region and the
background estimation in all the tagged regions are considered in this thesis as shown in Figure 5.1.
Here, the details of the background estimation, which provide large impacts on the VH(H → bb¯)
signal prediction, are described in Section 9.2.3.
The rest of jets including the forward jets are treated as the non-Higgs-candidate jets, typically
from the initial state radiation (ISR) processes.
The two Higgs-candidate jets are sorted by their pT, where the leading jet is denoted as j1 or
b1 and the sub-leading jet is denoted as j2 or b2. The non-Higgs-candidate jets are also sorted by
their pT.
The leading Higgs-candidate jet is required to have pT > 45 GeV, while the other is required to
have pT > 20 GeV. Due to the known issue of the mis-modelling of gluon splitting in V+jets at
low Higgs mass region, the invariant mass of the two Higgs-candidate jets (denoted as mbb, mcc) is
required to satisfy mbb,cc > 50 GeV.
*1 In the CTN region, two signal jets are chosen; c-tight tagged jet and pV
T leading non-tagged jet.

Fig. 5.1: Analysis regions defined by the flavour tagging (PCFT) requirements for the Higgs￾candidates. The green region is used for the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, while the blue regions are
used for the VH(cc¯) regime. The orange regions are used for the top enriched control region
(BCT-tag Top CR). The VH(H → bb¯) signal process is predicted in the SR in the resolved VH(bb¯)
regime (BB-tag). The important background events and their dedicated CRs (defined in Section
5.2.2) for the VH(H → bb¯) signal prediction are also shown in the explanation texts.
5.1.3.2 Boosted regime
In the boosted regime, the dedicated b-jet tagging (PCBT) is deployed in the boosted VH(bb¯)
regime, but the same flavour tagging used in pV
T < 400 GeV (PCFT) is deployed in the VH(cc¯)
regime in pV
T > 400 GeV since dedicated charm quark jet tagging in the boosted regime is not
developed enough yet. This thesis focuses on the VH(H → bb¯) signal prediction in the boosted
VH(bb¯) regime.
The VR track jets are b-tagged or untagged with the 85 % WP in the PCBT scheme. The large￾R jet with the highest pT is used for the Higgs-candidate reconstruction. The three VR track-jets
with the highest pT are required, and the large-R jet is required to have exactly two b-tagged VR
track jets inside the large-R jet’s cone. The reconstructed mass of the Higgs-candidate large-R jet
(denoted as mj) needs to have mj > 50 GeV.
Because different flavour tagging frameworks are deployed in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime
(PCBT) and the VH(cc¯) regime in pV
T > 400 GeV (PCFT), there are overlap events between the
boosted VH(bb¯) regime and VH(cc¯) regime. The overlap of VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) is
negligible as described in Appendix D.

=== PDF PAGE 78 ===
64 5.2 Event categorisation
5.1.4 Additional background suppression
Multi-jet suppression in 0-lepton:
Multi-jet background due to mismeasured jets in the calorimeters is suppressed by the following
selection requirements:
• For resolved VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯), |∆Φ(j1, j2)| < 140◦,
• |∆Φ(Emiss
T , Hcand )| > 120◦,
• |∆Φ(Emiss
T , closest jets)|; In resolved VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) regime, > 20◦ in 2-jet
region, > 30◦ in 3-jet region; In boosted VH(H → bb¯) regime, > 30◦,
where j1 and j2 are the Higgs-candidate jets in resolved VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) regime,
and ∆Φ(Emiss
T , closest jets) is the azimuthal angle between the Emiss
T vector and the closest jet. In the
resolved VH(H → bb¯) and VH(H → cc¯) regime, three leading signal jets are considered, while in
the boosted VH(H → bb¯) regime, only small-R jets with pT > 70 GeV outside the Higgs-candidate
large-R jet are considered.
The requirement is applied because the kinematic parameter tends to concentrate on small values
of |∆Φ(Emiss
T , closest jets)| in the case of multi-jet events. With this selection, the remaining fraction
of multi-jet contamination is as small as O(1) % of the total background, and the multi-jet process
is negligible in the 0-lepton channel.
Multi-jet suppression in 1-lepton:
To suppress multi-jet backgrounds, Emiss
T is required to be larger than 30 GeV in the electron
sub-channel of resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes, Emiss
T > 50 GeV for the boosted VH(bb¯)
regime. The mW
T , as defined in Eq. (8.1), is required to be larger than 20 GeV in 75 < pW
T < 150
GeV. As the Emiss
T trigger is also used in the 1-lepton’s µ sub-channel, the same S T*2 cut in the
0-lepton channel is applied.
5.2 Event categorisation

The reconstructed events are categorised into regions based on the truth-based defined bins in the
Simplified Template Cross-Section (STXS) framework for the fiducial differential cross-section
measurement, which is introduced in Section 1.3.2. Thus, the analysis region is divided by the

5.2.2 Resolved regimes
In the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, two pV
T regions are defined as 150 < pV
T < 250 GeV and
250 < pV
T < 400 GeV in the 0-lepton channel, and three pV
T regions are defined as 75 < pV
T < 150
GeV, 150 < pV
T < 250 GeV, and 250 < pV
T < 400 GeV in the 1-,2-lepton channels. The analysis
region is also divided by the number of inclusive (signal+forward) jets, where the non-Higgs￾candidate jets with pT > 30 GeV is counted, referred to as “Njet region”. Since all the jets are
available in pT > 20 GeV in Section 4.3, to avoid confusion between the counting of all jets and
the Njet region, a term “N-jet event” is introduced for an event with N jets. For example, when there

=== PDF PAGE 81 ===
Chapter 5. Event selection and categorisation 67
is an event with two Higgs-candidate (b-)jets and one non-Higgs-candidate jet in 20 < pT < 30
GeV, this event is called as “3-jet event”, while this event is categorised into “2-jet region”. In the
0-lepton channel, three Njet regions are defined as 2-jet, 3-jet, and 4-jet regions. In the 1-lepton
channel, two Njet regions are defined as 2-jet and 3-jet regions. In the 2-lepton channel, three Njet
regions are defined as 2-jet, 3-jet, and Njet≥ 4 (denoted as ”> 4-jet”) regions.
Signal Region (SR) and ∆R Control Region (CR):
The signal regions require the events to be categorised in the BB-, CTC-, and CTN-regions of
Figure 5.1. The targeted signal events (VH(H → bb¯) process) in this thesis are predicted in the
signal region of the BB-tag region. As shown in Figure 5.2, since the signal events are concen￾trating in the middle of the 2-D histograms as functions of the opening angle (∆R) between the
Higgs-candidate jets (∆R(j1, j2)) and pV
T, the signal pure region and background dominant regions
are defined as follows according to these observables. Because the Higgs-candidate jets are de￾cayed from a resonant Higgs boson in the signal events, the invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate
jets is reconstructed with a peak of the Higgs mass (∼ 125 GeV), and the ∆R(j1, j2) has a peak as
a function of pV
T. Therefore, the combination of these observables approximates the invariant mass
of two Higgs-candidate jets, and these properties can help to distinguish the signal events from
the background events. Figure I.2 shows the distribution of invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate
jets for all signal and background events, and Figure 5.3 shows the distribution of ∆R(j1, j2) for
all signal and background events. Historically, since the invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate
jets is used as a final discriminant variable in the fit, and since the SR-CR cut with ∆R(j1, j2) and
pV
T is effective to describe the background contributions as a function of pV
T, this SR and ∆R CR
definitions are exploited. Besides the Higgs boson, a resonant diboson decay (Z → bb¯) also has
the same property with its invariant mass (Z =∼ 91 GeV).
A High ∆R Control Region (High-∆R CR) is designed with the cut function in Table 5.4 to
constrain the V+jets and top quark background events. The cut functions are defined to allow 95
%(85 %) of the signal events in the 2-jet(> 3-jet) region stay below this cut function. In order
to discard the mis-modelling issue by MC simulation in High-∆R CRs and to keep the High-∆R
CR kinematically closer to the signal region, an upper cut at ∆R = π is applied. This cut discards
∼40 % of events in High-∆R CRs and has a negligible impact on the signal region. The High-
∆R CRs are enriched with the top quark background events in the 1-lepton channel, with Z+jets
background events in the 2-lepton channel, and both events in the 0-lepton channel.
A Low ∆R Control Region (Low-∆R CR) is designed with the cut function in Table 5.4, to model
the W+jets process with bb¯ jets in the 1-lepton channel in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime. The cut

=== PDF PAGE 82 ===
68 5.2 Event categorisation
functions are defined to allow 90 % of the diboson events stay in the signal region above this cut
function, for the validation analysis that uses the diboson VZ(Z → bb¯) events as the signal events,
which is explained in Section 1.4.3.
The signal region (SR) is defined between these two cut functions, or below the High-∆R CR
cut function, to predict the VH signal events (VH(H → bb¯)) or to predict the VZ signal events
(VZ(Z → bb¯)). The High-∆R CR cut functions and Low-∆R CR cut functions are superimposed
in signal distributions in Figure 5.2.

V+light flavour Control Region (CR):
The V+light flavour Control Region (V+lf CR) is the inclusive CTN-tag region. The V+lf CR
consists of events with one of the Higgs candidate jets being a loose c-tagged jet, and one being
a non-tagged jet. This CR is set in all three lepton channels. In the 0-lepton channel, both of
W+light and Z+light flavour jet events exist. In the 1-lepton channel, this CR is composed of ∼60
% of W+light flavour jet events. In the 2-lepton channel, it is composed of more than 70 % of
Z+light flavour jet events.

In the 2-lepton channel, a high purity tt
¯ control region is defined by selecting SR events with
different flavoured leptons (eµ or µe). This treatment eliminates most of the backgrounds and the
signal events, which are all expected to contain the same flavour lepton pairs from a Z boson decay.
The eµ CR in resolved VH(bb¯) 2-lepton channel is used for deriving the top quark background
template of BDT final discriminant distributions for the corresponding SRs.
BCT-tag Top Control Region (CR) in 0-,1-lepton channels:
The top background components with b+other mixed flavour labelled jets at the truth level, de￾noted as Top(bc) and Top(bl), enter into the SRs in both the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes

=== PDF PAGE 84 ===
70 5.2 Event categorisation
due to flavour mis-tagging, which is not negligible. As discussed in Section 5.1.3.1, the Top CR
region can be defined by selecting events with at least one tight c-tag and at least one b-tag (BCT￾tag region). Since SRs in the 0-, 1-lepton channels use tight c-veto and b-veto in resolved VH(bb¯)
and VH(cc¯), respectively, the Top CR is completely orthogonal to SRs in the resolved VH(bb¯) and
VH(cc¯) regimes. In this top CR, the Higgs-candidate is constructed from the pT leading b-tagged
jet and the pT leading tight c-tagged jet. As shown in Figure 5.4 (a), when the b-jet and c-jet
decayed from the same top quark are used in the Higgs-candidate reconstruction, this process has
the same the kinematic feature such as mj1, j2 and ∆R as the signal process, which directly affects
the signal sensitivity in the SRs.
(a) TopCR in the resolved regime (b) TopCR in the boosted regime

shows how the b-tag and c-tight tag control region can catch the tt
¯→ bc component in the resolved
regime. (b) shows how the top enriched control region in the boosted regime can catch the tt

process with the Higgs-candidate large-R jet(left) and b-tagged VR track jet(right).
Hereinafter, because this thesis focuses on measuring the Higgs boson properties of the
VH(H → bb¯) signal process, the analysis methods in the SRs of BB-region and in the correspond￾ing CR regions are mentioned. The targeted SRs and CRs in this thesis are summarised in Figure
5.1 and the dedicated CRs for different background processes are summarised in Table 9.3.

reconstructed analysis, cross-section of each truth STXS bin is measured with the signal simulated
samples that preserve the truth information of their applicable STXS bin as discussed in Section

Chapter 5. Event selection and categorisation 73
11.2. According to this truth bin information, the events in a truth STXS bin are migrated from
several reconstructed regions. This migration is caused by the object reconstruction and event cat￾egorisation effect such as the resolution, identification efficiency, or counting of the lepton and jets.
As a result of this event migration, the statistical uncertainties of the differential cross-section in
STXS bins increase, since the statistics of signal events categorised in a certain STXS bin would be
smaller in a corresponding analysis region. The changes of the statistical uncertainty can be eval￾uated with the differential cross-sections normalised with the SM predictions in different STXS
bins as shown in Figure 7.10. The event migration can be evaluated by a correlation between the
reconstructed regions and the truth STXS bins as shown in Figure 7.7 or a correlation between
each truth STXS bins as shown in Figure 7.12. Here, while the less events migrate between differ￾ent two bins/regions, the correlation of these two bins/regions decreases, which can be said as “the
correlation is improved”.
In order to improve the correlation, mainly two kinds of treatments are performed. Firstly, the
pT requirement for counting the number of non-Higgs-candidate reconstructed jets is set to pT >30
GeV to align the truth ISR jet definition in the STXS framework. Secondly, a Final State Radiation
(FSR) jet is identified and removed from the counting of non-Higgs-candidate jets to align the truth
ISR jet definition. This FSR jet treatment is described in detail in Chapter 7.

=== PDF PAGE 88 ===
74
Chapter 6
Energy correction of jets originating
from Higgs decays
The overview of jet energy correction is provided in Section 6.1, followed by descriptions of four
correction methods in the following subsections. Section 6.6 describes the performance of the four
corrections on the Higgs mass resolution.
6.1 Overview of dedicated jet energy correction
The dedicated jet energy corrections for the Higgs-candidate jets are applied considering the
topological information of the VH(H → bb¯) signal process*1 to improve the Higgs mass resolu￾tion and signal sensitivity on top of the standard jet energy correction. There are four dedicated
corrections: Muon-in-jet correction, pT dependent correction (PtReco), Kinematic Fit (KF), and
FSR recovery. These corrections are performed after the object reconstruction, event selection,
and event categorisation.
6.2 Muon-in-jet correction
Muon-in-jet correction calibrates the energy of the Higgs-candidate jets that decay semi￾leptonically into muons in ∼ 20 % probability, as the energy of muon is not fully ab￾sorbed by the calorimeter. In the resolved regime, if a muon is identified with ∆R(j,µ) <
min(0.4, 0.04 + 10 GeV/pµ
T), where j denotes a Higgs-candidate jet, the muon 4-momentum is
added to the Higgs-candidate jet. O(1) GeV energy loss of a muon in the calorimeters calculated
*1 The energy corrections in the VH(cc¯) regime are configured considering the VH(H → cc¯) process.

=== PDF PAGE 89 ===
Chapter 6. Energy correction of jets originating from Higgs decays 75
according to the path length is subtracted, and therefore there is no double counting of the muon
energy deposit in the calorimeters expected. In the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, a muon inside
the Higgs-candidate large-R jet cone is identified, then the muon 4-momentum is added to the
Higgs-candidate large-R jet.
The muon-in-jet correction is applied to all the events. Systematic uncertainty on the momentum
correction is considered to be negligible in this correction.
6.3 pT dependent correction
A pT dependent correction, denoted as “PtReco”, aims to correct the missing energy due to
neutrinos produced in the b-jets with semi-leptonic decays. The PtReco is applied in the resolved
VH(bb¯) regime in all the lepton channels. The correction factor is derived from the ratio between
muon-in-jet corrected b-tagged jets, and generator-based truth jets which include truth muons and
neutrinos. The b-jets with muons and without muons are considered separately. There is a ∼10 %
difference on the correction factor between b-jets with and without muons. The correction factor
is derived in the nominal model and applied to all systematic variations. Systematic uncertainty on
the correction factor is considered to be negligible in this correction.
6.4 Kinematic Fit (KF)
Kinematic Fit (KF) is applied in the 2-lepton channel with at most one non-Higgs-candidate jet
(pT > 20 GeV) after the muon-in-jet correction. The four-momentum of a Z boson decaying into
an electron or a muon pair is measured with high precision, allowing a constraint on momentum
balance in the transverse plane to be applied, thereby improving jet energy resolution. The resolu￾tion of lepton is typically 1 % level, resolution of jet is typically 10 % level, and no physical Emiss
T
is expected in the 2-lepton channel.
In the KF, a negative log likelihood is minimised to obtain the fit value

where the index i denotes all the objects except for the Higgs-candidate jets including leptons, jets,
and MET soft term, index j denotes all flavour-tagged small-R jets, and index k denotes all objects.
The first term is the resolution term for the final state objects such as electrons, muons, light
flavour jets and MET. The energy responses of these objects are treated as Gaussian distributions.
The MET resolution term is considered to comprehend the ISR effect in ggZH. σi is the energy or

=== PDF PAGE 90 ===
76 6.5 FSR recovery
momentum resolution of each object.
To better model the energy response for the Higgs-candidate jets, instead of Gaussian distributions,
a transfer function of the probability density function of the ratio between reconstructed jet pT and
generator-based truth jet pT are used in the likelihood, which is presented as the second term. The
transfer function is measured in 8 pT regions, 2 η regions and with or without muon decay inde￾pendently measured from the signal simulated sample. The transfer functions are updated in the
resolved VH(bb¯) regime, and are first derived in the VH(H → cc¯) regime. The transfer functions
are only available for small-R jets, and the Gaussian distribution is treated as the energy response
for large-R jets.
The third and fourth terms are the balance constraint on the x and y direction, respectively. The
Gaussian constraint of x or y direction is obtained from the ZH signal simulated sample, where
σbal is expected to be 12 GeV for the 2 jets region and 15 GeV for the 3 jets region.
The fifth term is the Z boson mass constraint which is assumed to follow a Breit-Wigner distribu￾tion, where mZ and ΓZ are the Z boson mass and width, respectively. The m(( is calculated with
the fitted lepton’s momentum.
No additional uncertainty is applied in the KF correction.
6.5 FSR recovery
Non-Higgs-candidate jets may come from a Final State Radiation (FSR) of the Higgs-candidate
jets. To identify jets from the FSR, a continuous cut on the sum of ∆R between a non-Higgs￾candidate jet and each of two Higgs-candidate jets (∆Rj, j
1
Higgs
+ ∆Rj, j
2
Higgs ) as a function of pV
T, is
defined as discussed in Section 7.2. A non-Higgs candidate jet below the cut function is considered
as the FSR jets, and is merged to the Higgs candidate system. This correction is referred to as “FSR
recovery”. The FSR recovery is applied in the 2-lepton channel for the resolved regimes for the
first time to improve the signal mj1, j2 resolution by ∼ 4 %, and the MVA based signal sensitivity by
∼ 1 %. The FSR recovery is not applied to the 0-,1-lepton channel, since the tt
¯ background events
get the peak of mj1, j2 around the Higgs mass (∼125 GeV) when the FSR recovery is applied, and
it degrades the signal sensitivity. Additional systematic uncertainty for the FSR recovery is not
applied since they are comprehended in other systematic uncertainties.
More details on the FSR recovery are described in Chapter 7.

79
Chapter 7
FSR recovery
This chapter summarises the studies of the FSR recovery. The overall strategy of the FSR re￾covery is described in Section 7.1, the cut-based optimal identification of FSR jets is explained
in Section 7.2, and the performance studies are summarised in Section 7.3 in terms of the mass
resolution, signal sensitivity, the statistical uncertainties of the fiducial differential cross-section
measurement based on the STXS framework. The application of the FSR recovery into the main
VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) analysis is described in Section 7.4.
7.1 Strategy of FSR recovery
In case an event contains jets that do not originate from a Higgs boson, several possible sources
for these jets can be considered as follows.
• Gluon radiation from either or both b-jets decayed from Higgs boson, known as Final State
Radiation (FSR)
• Gluon or quark radiation from initial state partons in the Higgs boson production, known as
Initial State Radiation (ISR)
• Other radiation from Pile-up
Figure 7.1 illustrates the VH(H → bb¯) signal events containing an ISR jet and an FSR jet. At
the stage of event reconstruction, the types of jets are treated without classification, called as
“additional jets”, which is equivalent with the term of “non-Higgs-candidate jets” used in the other
chapter.
In the resolved regimes (pV
T < 400 GeV), Final State Radiation (FSR) causes a loss of the
reconstructed invariant mass of the Higgs, since additional jets are typically not included in the

=== PDF PAGE 94 ===
80 7.1 Strategy of FSR recovery
H
B1
B2
FSR Jet
V
ISR Jet
Fig. 7.1: A VH(H → bb¯) process with an FSR jet and an ISR jet.
reconstruction of the Higgs system. Figure 7.2 shows a histogram of invariant mass with two b-jets
and a leading additional jet (mbb j), where a leading additional jet is labelled as FSR / ISR / pile-up
jet using truth information of the signal simulation sample. When the FSR jet is correctly identified
as a decay product of the Higgs boson, the invariant mass is accurately calculated, resulting in a
peak at the Higgs boson mass of around 125 GeV as shown in the FSR labelled histogram of Figure
7.2, along with an improvement in mass resolution.
In addition, as discussed in Section 5.2.4, the event migration across the reconstructed regions
degrades the statistical uncertainties of cross-section measurement in different STXS bins. The
STXS bins are classified based on the number of truth ISR jets in pV
T
,truth
< 400 GeV as defined
in Section 1.3.2, meanwhile the analysis regions are simply classified based on the number of
reconstructed jets including additional jets in pV
T < 400 GeV. If the FSR jets can be removed from
the ISR jet counting, the fiducial differential cross-section measurement for the number of ISR jets
would be improved.
In summary, the correct FSR jet identification derives the following improvement:
• Correction of the four momentum of the Higgs system, and improvement of the resolution
of the mass reconstruction.
• Improvement of the correlation between the truth and reconstructed categorisation in the
STXS binning.
In the VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) analysis, an identification of the FSR jets is performed toward all the
additional jets for pT > 20 GeV, exploiting a cut function as described in Section 7.2. The iden￾tified FSR jet and the corresponding closest Higgs-candidate jet are summed in vectors, and the
corresponding Higgs-candidate jet is updated. After merging the jets, the events with the identified
FSR jets in pT > 30 GeV are re-categorised to the smaller number of jet region. This treatment is
referred to as “FSR recovery”.

channels. The FSR recovery is applied to several N-jet events as shown in Table 7.1.
The order of corrections to apply the FSR recovery between the other three jet corrections
(Chapter 6) is studied. As the result, no significant performance change is found whenever the
FSR recovery is applied, which is described in detail in Appendix G.2. For the simplicity of im￾plementation and the theoretical reason from the KF, the FSR recovery is applied at the last of the
jet energy corrections.

7.2 FSR identification
The FSR recovery should be applied to the jets that have FSR-like characteristics based on their
topological features. The criteria for identifying FSR jets are optimised using all additional jets:
the signal jets in pT > 20 GeV and the forward jets in pT > 30 GeV (See Table 4.3). As the QCD
colour exchange between the two b-jets tends to generate the FSR [109], most significant topolog￾ical differences for different types of additional jets are shown in Figure 7.3. These plots show the
2D ∆η-∆φ relative distribution between additional jet and each b-jet using the VH(H → bb¯) signal
simulated samples, where leading b-jets (b1) is located in a fixed position. The b1 is always fixed
at (0,0) position, and then the plane is rotated to keep the sub-leading b-jets (b2) at π/4 direction.
A remarkable feature observed in these plots is that FSR jets tend to concentrate between the two
b-jets, and this characteristic can be utilised to distinguish them from the other types of additional
jets.
Considering this feature and good resolution of pV
T as proxy of pH
T , a cut function with variables
of ∆Rb1, j+∆Rb2, j and pV
T is introduced to distinguish the FSR jets and other types of additional jets.

sample, where distributions of the events with FSR jet (red), ISR jet (blue), and pile-up jet (green)
are labelled with truth information stored in the MC sample. The cut function is intended to be
drawn at the boundary between the FSR jet region and the other types of additional jet regions. The
optimisation of the FSR cut function is conducted using all events containing three jets (Figure 7.4
(a)) in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime. The signal and background nominal
MC samples are used for the optimisation of the cut function. The significance of the mbb signal

7.3.1 Higgs mass resolution

to estimate the mass peak (mean[GeV]) and mass resolution (σ[GeV]). Figure 7.5 (a) shows the
distribution of events with only FSR identified jets in the 3-jet events before the event migration.

7.3.2 Purity
The purity of truth FSR jet identification is defined by Eq. (7.3) and is evaluated based on the
truth information from the signal MC sample. According to the event yields of truth FSR events,
the overall purity of all pV
T regions is 82%, while the purity for individual pV
T interval regions of
75 < pV
T < 150, 150, pV
T < 250, and 250 < pV
T < 400 GeV are 80%, 86%, 80%, respectively.

=== PDF PAGE 100 ===
86 7.3 Performance of FSR recovery
Therefore, the FSR identification can collect the correct FSR with high purity.
Purity = Events with FSR labelled jets
Events with FSR identified jet. (7.3)
Thanks to this accurate FSR identification, the events in the ∼ 125 GeV peak of mbb j distribution
are collected and migrated to the less Njet region as shown in Figure 7.9(d).
7.3.3 Signal event migration
When there are FSR identified jets in the additional jets in pT > 30 GeV, a signal event with
these jets is re-categorised into the smaller number of jets (Njet) region. This signal event migration
is illustrated with Figure 7.6, and the impact of this migration is shown in Figure 7.7.
Figure 7.6 shows the signal event migration across the analysis Njet regions, where the signal
events are labelled with the number of truth ISR jets as defined by the STXS bins. By applying the
FSR recovery, the signal events move to the 2-jet region. The problematic events are the events
with FSR jets (Ntruth
IS R jets=0) but categorised in ≥3-jet region as shown in Figure 7.6 (a). These events
are migrated to the 2-jet region more than the events with ISR jets (Ntruth
IS R jets ≥ 1) that are migrated
due to mis-identification of ISR jets by the FSR recovery. This result proves that the FSR recovery
can identify the correct FSR jets. The total event yields are changed between Figure 7.6 (a) and
(b). This is because the FSR recovery collects the FSR-identified jets from up to the 4-jet event,
while the analysis uses only the 2-, 3-jet region in the 1-lepton channel. The FSR recovery brings
4-jet events with FSR-identified jets into the analysis regions in the 1-lepton channel. The similar
plots in the different lepton channels are shown in Figure G.11 in Appendix G.3.
Figure 7.7 shows the correlation matrix between the truth STXS bin and the reconstructed anal￾ysis region in the 2-lepton channel. In Figures 7.7 (a) and (b), fraction of event yields in the
different analysis regions is calculated relative to inclusive event yields in the same lepton channel
in the same STXS bin. Figures 7.7 (a) and (b) show the case before and after the event migration
is conducted by the FSR recovery, respectively. Figure 7.7 (c) shows the differences of the rela￾tive fraction values between these two cases. The correlation between the STXS truth bin and the
reconstructed analysis region improves. For example, for the component in the STXS bin with 0
ISR jet in the 2-jet region, the relative fraction increases. The changes of the relative fraction in all
the different lepton channels have the same trends as shown in Figure G.12 in Appendix G.3.

7.3.4 Impact on signal sensitivity
This analysis exploits BDTVH as a final discriminant comparing the Higgs mass (mbb) and other
kinematic observables, and its definition is explained in detail in Chapter 8,
The BDTVH learns the trends of several kinds of kinematic variables as the BDT input variables.
The FSR recovery affects the input variables used for the BDT training, where mbb has the most
significant impact. Therefore, the re-training of BDTVH is demanded after the FSR recovery.
To evaluate the performance of signal sensitivity, a BDTVH-based significance is defined by the
following equation
S =
)
Σi[2(si + bi)ln(1 +
si
bi
)]. (7.4)
Table 7.2 shows the comparison of BDTVH-based significance before and after the FSR recovery in
the different regions and different lepton channels. The BDTVH after the FSR recovery is trained
with the same configuration. The BDTVH significance is improved by ∼ 0.5 % in the 2-lepton
channel, but is decreased by ∼ 0.3 %, ∼ 1.9 % in the 0-, 1-lepton channels, respectively. The
significance in the 2-jet region tends to be increased except in the 1-lepton channel, while the
significance in the multiple jet region tends to be decreased. This is because the yields of signal
events increase in the 2-jet region, while decreases in the multiple (> 3) jet regions as shown in

7.3.5 Impact on precision of fiducial differential cross-section measurement
Here, the effect of FSR recovery on the measurement of fiducial differential cross-section based
on the STXS framework is discussed. The phase space is divided into 15 STXS bins (15 Param￾eter of Interests (15POIs)) using a combination of pV
T
,truth and the number of the ISR jets at the
truth level. The Asimov dataset is used to assess uncertainties in the fit as described in Section
11.3. Figure 7.10 shows the results of the fiducial differential cross-section measurement of 15
POIs before and after applying the FSR recovery. These measurements consider all the statistical
and systematic uncertainties estimated for the final results*2. Figure 7.10 (a) is the result before
the FSR recovery, and Figure 7.10 (b) is the result after the FSR recovery in all lepton channels.
The effect in the 0-, 2-lepton channels is shown in the ZH STXS bins, while the effect in the
1-lepton channel is shown in the WH STXS bins. These plots show the expectation of signal
strength measurement with errors in the different STXS bins (POIs). In all STXS bins, the statis￾tical uncertainty is dominant in the total uncertainty of the cross-section measurement except for
the ‘WH, 75 < pV
T
,truth
< 150’ STXS bin.
Table 7.3 summarises the relative changes of the total, statistical, and systematic uncertainties
between the results before and after the FSR recovery in the different STXS bins in the Figure
7.10. In the STXS bins split by the number of truth ISR jets, the statistical uncertainties decrease
by a maximum of 8 %. The correlation of truth STXS bins and reconstructed analysis region is
improved as discussed in Section 7.3.3. As discussed in Section 11.2, the POI is assigned to each
STXS bin in the fiducial differential cross-section measurement. When more events of the same
STXS bin are collected in the same analysis region as shown in Figure 7.11, the corresponding
POI is determined with more statistics. Therefore, the improvement of the correlation results in
the improvement of the statistical uncertainty in the fiducial differential cross-section measurement
as explained in Section 5.2.4.
In the STXS bins of pV
T
,truth
> 400 GeV, the difference is not observed since the FSR recovery
is not applied in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime. In all the STXS bins except the ‘WH, 75 < pV
T
,truth
<
150’ STXS bin, the changes of systematic uncertainties don’t largely affect the total uncertainties,
since the statistical uncertainties are dominant. As the results, the total uncertainty improves by a
maximum of ∼ 9 % by applying the FSR recovery in all lepton channels.
Figure 7.12 shows the correlation matrix between the STXS bins (POIs) in the measurements
before (a) and after (b) the FSR recovery. In these correlation matrix plots, the statistical uncer￾tainty is only considered. The correlation between the STXS bins is calculated as discussed in
*2 The acceptance uncertainty (Section 9.2.1) in the 2-lepton channel is estimated after the event migration of the FSR
recovery, but those in the 0-, 1-lepton channel are estimated before the event migration.

7.4 Optimal application to the final results of this analysis
The FSR recovery was found to reduce the statistical uncertainty of the fiducial differential
cross-section measurement by up to ∼ 8 % across all lepton channels. Concerning the significance,
it improves by ∼ 0.5 % in the 2-lepton channel, but leads to a decline in the 0- and 1-lepton
channels. In the 2-lepton channel, the FSR recovery can improve the precision of the fiducial
differential cross-section measurement without degrading the signal sensitivity by migrating the
FSR-like events. Therefore, in the final results of the VH(bb¯, cc¯) analysis, the FSR recovery is
applied into the 2-lepton channel.

=== PDF PAGE 111 ===
97
Chapter 8
Multivariate discriminants
This chapter describes a strategy of event discrimination by MVA in Section 8.1, input variables
of MVA in Section 8.2, MVA training setup in Section 8.3, MVA evaluation in Section 8.4.
8.1 Strategy of discrimination in SR and Low-∆R CR
Having an invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate jets at the head of the list, there are several
kinematic variables to verify some differences between the signal and background processes. In
order to increase the signal purity in the SR and the Low-∆R CR, this analysis uses MultiVariate
Analysis (MVA). By training a set of well-chosen input variables, the multivariate likelihood learns
the difference between signal and background events and produce a one dimensional discriminant.
This analysis uses the Boosted Decision Tree (BDT) method provided by the TMVA package
of ROOT [110]. The BDT produces a score distributing in the range of [-1,1], referred to as
”BDT (output) score”, where the low score represents background-like events and the high score
represents signal-like events.
This analysis utilises three different types of BDT models: 51 “BDTVH”, 52 “BDTCRLow”, and 53
“BDTVZ”. The training configurations of these three BDT models are the same, except the training
samples as described in Section 8.3.
51 To maximise the signal sensitivity and to measure the Higgs properties with high precision,
BDTVH is used to construct a final discriminant variable to distinguish the VH signal events
from the other background events in the SRs.
52 To have better constraint on the W+jets in the SRs of the resolved VH(bb¯) regime in the
1-lepton channel, the BDTCRLow is used to discriminate the W+jets events from tt
¯ events in
the Low-∆R CR as discussed in Section 9.2.3.

=== PDF PAGE 112 ===
98 8.2 Input variables
53 BDTVZ is trained to discriminate diboson VZ(Z → bb¯/Z → cc¯) events from other back￾ground events. As discussed in Section 1.4.3, since these diboson processes share nearly
the same topology with the VH(H → bb¯/H → cc¯) signal processes, BDTVZ is used
as the replacement of the BDTVH to measure the signal strength and significance of the
VZ(Z → bb¯/Z → cc¯) processes before the VH property measurements for the validation of
this analysis.
8.2 Input variables
Table 8.1 summarises all the BDT input variables. The three BDT models (BDTVH, BDTCRLow,
BDTVZ) use the same set of input variables.
Variables in the resolved regimes: *1 Here, all the input variables used in the resolved regimes
are explained.
• pV
T: transverse energy of the vector boson. In the 0-lepton channel, it is equivalent to the
missing transverse energy (Emiss
T ); in the 1-lepton channel, it is the vector sum of Emiss
T and
the lepton pT; in the 2-lepton channel, it is the vector sum of the 2 charged lepton pT.
• pj1
T and pj2
T : transverse momenta of the Higgs candidate jets. j1 refers to the jet with higher
pT.
• mj1 j2 : invariant mass of the Higgs candidate system.
• ∆R(j1, j2): the angular distance between the two Higgs-candidate jets*2.
• mj1 j2 j3 : the invariant mass of two Higgs-candidate jets and the remaining jet with the highest
pT. When there are only 2 jets in an event, mj1 j2 j3 = mj1 j2 .
• ∆φ(V0 , H0cand): azimuthal distance between the reconstructed vector boson and Higgs boson
candidates.
• binDL1r(j1), binDL1r(j2): the variable shows the PCFT-bin to which each Higgs-candidate jet
belongs; the untagged, the loose (70 % WP) and the tight (60 % WP) b-tagged, and the loose
and the tight c-tagged bins. In the MVA, the bin numbers for two of the Higgs-candidate
jets are used.
• +
i!1,2
pji
T : pT sum of non-Higgs-candidate jets that have pT > 20 GeV.
0-lepton channel specific variables:
*1 The usage of input variables is harmonized between the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes.
*2 The angular distance ∆R(i, j) between two particles i and j (or objects such as jets) is defined as ∆R(i, j) = &
(∆φ(i, j))2 + (∆η(i, j))2 with ∆φ(i, j) = φi − φj the azimuthal and ∆η(i, j) = ηi − ηj the pseudorapidity distances.

102 8.2 Input variables
regime due to the topological similarity. For example, the invariant mass of two Higgs candidate

large-R jet.

leading large-R jet, which requires ∆R(small-R jet, large-R jet) > 1.0.

• Colour: a variable designed to exploit the difference in colour-flow between gluon splittings
and decay from QCD singlet states, which is described below.
• binDL1r(j1,trk), binDL1r(j2,trk): corresponds to the PCBT bins to which the b-tagged VR track
jet belongs: the 85 %, the 77 %, the 70 % and the 60 % b-tagging efficiency bins.

the W-boson.

• ∆y(V0 , H0cand): the same definition as for the resolved case.

8.3 Training setup
The signal and background events that pass the event selection requirements in Section 5.1 and
the jet energy corrections in Chapter 6 are used for the BDT training. Furthermore, the truth
tagging (Section 4.4.4) is applied to produce the training samples to enhance the training statistics.
The nominal MC samples are used in the BDT training.
In the BDTVH, a signal sample is the VH(H → bb¯) events in the VH(bb¯) regimes*3, and back-
*3 VH(H → cc¯) signal sample is not included in the training, and it’s the signal sample in the training in the VH(cc¯)

=== PDF PAGE 118 ===
104 8.3 Training setup
ground samples are the V+jets, diboson, and top quark processes, including the top quark MC
samples in the 2-lepton channel. In the BDTCRLow, the W+jets with 2 b-tagged events in the Low-
∆R CR are used as a signal sample, and the tt
¯ events are used as a background sample. In the
BDTVZ, the diboson VZ(Z → bb¯) events are used as a signal sample in the VH(bb¯) regimes*4, and
the other background events and VH events are used as a background sample.
The BDT training is performed in the inclusive phase space of the SR and ∆R CR to enhance
the training statistics and ensure the stability of the training*5. The BDT models are separately
trained in each number of jet (Njet) region in the resolved regimes and dedicated pV
T categories as
follows: 75 < pV
T < 150 GeV*6, pV
T > 150 GeV in the resolved regimes, and pV
T > 400 in the
boosted regime.
The hyperparameters are optimised per training region to maximise the BDT-based significance
calculated by Eq. (8.6), but avoid overtraining. The gradient boost method is used in the resolved
VH(bb¯) regime. It is known to be good at capturing effects outside the bulk of the distributions,
and to give improvement to the BDT performance. AdaBoost is an optimal method for low sig￾nal statistics such as the training in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime. In these two VH(bb¯) regimes,
the BDTVH, BDTCRLow, and BDTVZ in each channel and region share the same hyperparameter
settings. For more details, Table 8.2 summarises the all hyperparameters in the VH(bb¯) regimes.

This analysis adopts k = 2, and the samples are divided according to even-odd of the MC event

8.4 MVA evaluation
By using the MVA, signal and background events can be successfully distinguished in all three
cases: BDTVH, BDTCRLow, BDTVZ. The BDT output distributions (post-fit) are shown in: Section
12.1.2 for the BDTVH, Figure 9.5 for the BDTCRLow, and Appendix I.4 for the BDTVZ.
The separation power in the BDT output distribution is evaluated with Eq. (8.6) in binned
distributions, referred to as ”Asimov Significance”.
S =
)
Σi[2(si + bi)ln(1 +
si
bi
)], (8.6)
where si and bi are the number of signal and background events contained in the i
th bin of a binned
BDT score histogram. The significance represents the signal excess over the background-only
hypothesis, where the unit is the standard deviations (σ). The Asimov significance is used as an
important criterion to adopt new attempts in the BDT training.
The significance can also be calculated with the mbb binned distribution, which has the best
separation power of the signal and background processes in the kinematic variables used in this
analysis. The comparison of the separation power between the BDT output distribution and the
mbb distribution is summarised with the significance values in Table 8.3. By introducing the BDT
score as the final discriminant variable, the Asimov significance improves ∼ 60 % for the BDTVH
and ∼ 20 % for the BDTVZ from the mbb distribution. Therefore, the MVA method improves the
separation power compared to the usage of the mbb distribution as the final discriminant variable.
As an overtraining cross-check, the ROC curves and the Asimov significance of the training and
testing samples are compared to each other, and the Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) test is performed
with BDT output distributions to make sure the BDT trained model has similar separation power
in both samples, as shown in Figure 8.3.

Chapter 9
Systematic uncertainties
This chapter describes experimental uncertainties in Section 9.1, and the modelling uncertainties
from the MC simulations in Section 9.2. The breakdown of the systematic uncertainties and their
impact on the signal strength measurement for the inclusive VH(H → bb¯) process is shown in
Table 12.4.
9.1 Experimental uncertainties
Experimental uncertainty is estimated during the calibration procedure of the object reconstruc￾tions and flavour tagging as discussed in Chapter 4, from the detector performance study, and from
the trigger efficiency study. In this thesis, the uncertainties of flavour tagging performance, the jet
energy scale (JES) calibration, and the jet energy resolution (JER) calibration have large impacts
on the property measurements of the VH(H → bb¯) process among the experimental uncertainties.
9.1.1 Jet reconstruction
The calibrations of reconstructed jets are described in Section 4.3.
For the small-R jet, the uncertainties are estimated for the calibration of the JES [93], JER
[93], and Jet Vertex Tagger (JVT) [113]. Figure 9.1 shows the uncertainties of JES, JER for the
small-R jet. The uncertainty size of JES varies 1-5 %, and that of JER varies 0.5-1.5 %. The
dominant uncertainties in the JES (Figure 9.1 (a)) are the systematic uncertainty of the in-situ
calibration such as modelling uncertainty of MC samples, and the variation comes from the jet
flavour response between quark-initiated jet and gluon-initiated jet. The dominant uncertainty in
the JER (Figure 9.1 (b)) is the systematic uncertainty of the in-situ calibration.
For the large-R jet, the uncertainty of JES, JER, Jet Mass Scale (JMS), and Jet Mass Resolution

=== PDF PAGE 122 ===
108 9.1 Experimental uncertainties
(JMR) are considered in this thesis, where they are derived for the in-situ calibration [99]. The
uncertainty size of JES varies 1-2 %, and that of JMS varies 2-10 %, while the precision of ratio
of the JER/JMR in data and simulation varies 10-15 % [99]. The contribution of the uncertainty
of large-R jet is smaller than that of the small-R jet in the measurement of signal strength of the
inclusive VH(H → bb¯) process.
For the VR track jet, the uncertainty is not considered in this analysis, since the momentum of
track is not used in the Higgs candidate reconstruction and the uncertainty of flavour tagging are
expected to cover the most effects of the uncertainties from the VR track jet reconstruction.
Fig. 9.1: Uncertainties of JES and JER for small-R jet (anti-kt, R = 0.4). The jet is reconstructed
with PFlow algorithm and calibrated with the JES. (a) shows the fractional JES uncertainty as a
function of jet pT at η = 0. (b) shows the absolute uncertainty of JER as a function of jet pT at
η = 0.2. The total uncertainty (blue filled shape) is derived as the quadrature sum of all sources
listed in the legend.
9.1.2 Flavour tagging
The calibration of scale factor in the flavour tagging is described in Section 4.4. The flavour
tagging efficiency is calibrated for b-jet, c-jet, and light jet, which are labelled in truth level. The
uncertainties of the scale factors, as shown in Figure 4.4, are considered separately for the differ￾ent jet flavour, and are treated as uncorrelated between the small-R jets and VR track jets. The
uncertainty is decomposed into several orthogonal systematic sources: 45 for b-jet, 20 for c-jet,
and 36 for light jet. The major uncertainties come from the difference of the parton-shower MC
simulations and the statistics of the data used in the calibration.

=== PDF PAGE 123 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 109
9.1.3 Lepton reconstruction
The uncertainties estimated during the calibration of the candidate electrons [88] and the candi￾date muons [89], which assess all the uncertainties of reconstruction, identification, and isolation,
are considered, in addition to the uncertainties from their trigger efficiencies.
9.1.4 Emiss
T reconstruction
The uncertainties of the calibration of reconstructed leptons and jets are propagated into the
uncertainty of the reconstructed Emiss
T . The uncertainties of the energy scale and resolution of the
soft term in the Emiss
T reconstruction are also considered [107].
9.1.5 Other sources of experimental uncertainty
There are other experimental uncertainties that are considered in this thesis. For the electron and
muon trigger, uncertainties of their trigger scale factor (SF) are considered. For the met trigger,
the uncertainty for trigger efficiency and SF between MC samples and the data is evaluated from
the W+jets, Z+jets, and tt
¯ events, which is inherited from the previous analysis [28]. Luminosity
is measured 140 ± 1.2fb−1 (0.83 %) [36] by LUCID-2 Cherenkov detector, which is located in the
forward region outside the ATLAS detector. The distribution of the mean number of interactions
per crossing (pile-up ,µ-) in MC samples is corrected with factor 1.0/1.09 to match the distribution
in the data as discussed in Section 3.2.1. The uncertainty of the correction factor is estimated with
measured inelastic cross-sections [114] by varying the factor between 1.0/1.0 and 1.0/1.18.
9.2 Modelling uncertainties
Modelling (theoretical) uncertainty is derived from the comparison between different theoret￾ical models in the MC simulations. In this thesis, the uncertainties of the VH signal process,
V+jets background processes, and diboson background process have large impacts on the property
measurements of the VH(H → bb¯) process among the modelling uncertainties.
9.2.1 General strategy to derive modelling uncertainty
The uncertainties for the modelling are studied for both the background estimation and sig￾nal prediction. For the background estimation, both of the data and MC samples in background
dominant regions (CRs) are used to estimate and constrain the background process in the signal
sensitive regions (SRs) in the VH(bb¯) regimes. The definitions of CRs and SRs are described in
Section 5.2, and the estimations and modelling studies of the different background processes are

=== PDF PAGE 124 ===
110 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
discussed in Section 9.2.3. For the signal prediction, signal MC samples and the estimated back￾ground samples are utilized to explain the data in the SR in the VH(bb¯) regimes and signal MC
samples are used to extract the VH(H → bb¯) signal event yields from the data. The modelling
study of the VH(H → bb¯) signal process is discussed in Section 9.2.2. The signal prediction and
background estimation are performed in profile likelihood fit, whose methodology is discussed in
Chapter 11.
In pV
T < 400 GeV, the modelling uncertainties of the VH(H → bb¯) process and the VH(H → cc¯)
process is estimated coherently, with sharing the same modelling uncertainties of their background
processes. Since the boosted VH(bb¯) regime adopts different analysis technique from the resolved
VH(bb¯) regime and the VH(cc¯) regime, the modelling in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime is considered
separately from these two regimes. The modelling of events in pV
T > 400 GeV in the VH(cc¯)
regime is studied by being included in pV
T > 250 GeV region in the VH(cc¯) regime.
Apart from the nominal model that is explained in Section 3.2.2, alternative models are pre￾pared to ensure the accuracy of the physics process simulation. The modelling uncertainties are
derived by comparing the nominal model from the corresponding alternative models. The alter￾native models come from the alternative sets of parameters in the nominal MC generator, referred
to as “internal scale variation”, and from the alternative MC generators. The MC samples and the
internal scale variations for the different signal and background processes are summarised in Table
9.1. The alternative models are determined to disentangle different sources as much as possible.
The alternative MC samples are used to assess the impacts of Matrix Element (ME) or Parton
Shower (PS) computation variations. The internal scale variations are generally used to assess the
impacts of the renormalisation (µR) scale, the factorisation (µF) scale, Parton Distribution Function
(PDF), and strong coupling constant (αs) variations. Additional internal scale variations such as
NLO electroweak (EW) corrections are also considered depending on the physics processes.
The modelling uncertainties are derived separately for every physics process, and they are fac￾torised into three kinds of effects: normalisation, relative acceptance, and shapes of distributions
of fit variables defined in Section 11.2.
Normalisation
Overall event yield in every MC sample of a certain physics process is normalised to the same
cross-section times branching ratio predicted by the SM. When the cross-section and branching
ratio are predicted from theoretical studies or other experiments, the event yield of a certain process
is normalised to that cross-section, and its uncertainty is applied as the normalisation uncertainty
for that process. This treatment is conducted for the VH signal process and top quark background

=== PDF PAGE 125 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 111
Table 9.1: Summary of nominal and alternative theoretical models for different physics processes.
The nominal MC generators, internal scale variations of the nominal generators, and alternative
MC generators used to assess signal and background modelling uncertainties are listed.
