processes. For the other background processes, the cross-section and branching ratio simulated
by the nominal MC generators is used for the normalisation. The overall acceptance uncertainty,
which is derived from the variations of event yield in the inclusive analysis phase space due to
the different theoretical calculation models, is applied as the normalisation uncertainty. The value
of overall acceptance uncertainty is the sum in quadrature of uncertainties from all the available
alternative models, given by Eq. (9.2).
In the fit, a normalisation factor is introduced to modify the event yield of the background pro￾cesses. The normalisation factors of the major background (V+jets and top quark processes) are
left to float in the fit to match the data, referred to as “floating normalisation”, while the normalisa￾tion factors of minor background processes are fixed at the MC predictions in the fit. To estimate
a certain background contribution in the Signal Regions (SRs), where several physics processes
exist in the same region, the floating normalisation is set across the SRs and the dedicated Control
Regions (CRs) for that background process. The floating normalisation is evaluated in the CRs
with high purity of that background process, and propagated to the SRs. The same extrapolation
by the floating normalisation is performed across the lepton channels: from the 1- or 2-lepton
channel to the 0-lepton channel. The floating normalisation is split by the pV
T regions and the num￾ber of jet regions as much as the data has enough statistics, since there are trends of the floating
normalisation between these regions as discussed in Section 12.1.2. The statistical uncertainty of
floating normalisation is considered in the fit instead of the modelling uncertainty. The detailed
usage of the floating normalisation for every background process is described in the background
modelling parts below.

=== PDF PAGE 126 ===
112 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
Relative acceptance
There is a possible migration of the events from one region to another region caused by the vari￾ations of theoretical models. Especially, in the case of extrapolation across several regions, this
migration causes huge impact on the uncertainty of modification of event yields in the extrapolated
region. To evaluate this migration, relative acceptance between the two regions under the relation
of extrapolation is derived. The simplest way to implement the relative acceptance uncertainty
in the fit is to calculate the ratio of acceptance of the extrapolated region over the extrapolating
region, and to apply the value of its uncertainty to the extrapolating region. To assess the differ￾ence between the nominal and alternative models, the value of relative acceptance uncertainty is
calculated as a double-ratio given by Eq. (9.1).
Acceptance[RegionB(alternative MC)]
Acceptance[RegionB(nominal MC)] 2
Acceptance[RegionA(alternative MC)]
Acceptance[RegionA(nominal MC)] , (9.1)
where region A (RegionA) is the region with the highest purity of a certain background process,
and region B (RegionB) is the extrapolated region. The direction of extrapolation (A → B) depends
on the contribution of each background process: when there is little difference in the purity of a
physics process between both regions, the region with more event yield is assigned to the region
A. In case of extrapolation across three regions A, B, and C, in order of purity, the value is first
computed from the highest purity region to the two less purity regions i.e. A → [B+C], and then
computed between these two less purity regions i.e. B → C.
The value of relative acceptance uncertainty applied in the fit is the sum in quadrature of uncer￾tainties from all the available alternative models, given by Eq. (9.2). In this sum of uncertainties,
the uncertainty from the same kind of scale variation, e.g. QCD scale with six variations, is defined
as the maximum shift in the envelope of these variations.
Total acceptance =
"
Σi(Acceptance uncvari
)2, (9.2)
where the “Acceptance uncvari
” is an acceptance uncertainty due to the variation from the nominal
model to an alternative model vari.
Shapes of fit variables
Shape uncertainty quantifies the effects on the probability density function (p.d.f.) of variables
used in the fit by comparing the available theoretical models. There are two ways to evaluate
shape uncertainty: comparing two models with a reweighting technique, or comparing two models
directly.

=== PDF PAGE 127 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 113
When comparing nominal and alternative MC samples which have a large difference on their
statistics, event weights are applied to the shape of nominal MC sample to reproduce the shape
of alternative MC sample, which is referred to as “reweight”, and then the shape uncertainty is
estimated by comparing between the reweighted shape and the default shape of nominal MC sam￾ple. The details on the reweighting technique are described in Section 10.4.1. The event weight
is estimated by Calibrated Likelihood Ratio Estimator (CARL) framework [115]. More details on
the CARL are described in Section 10.4.3.
When comparing nominal and alternative MC samples which have small difference on their
statistics, and when comparing nominal scale and internal systematic scales in the same nominal
MC sample, the shape uncertainty is estimated by comparing two models directly.
The shapes of alternative models in each analysis region are normalised to that of the nominal
model to avoid any double counting with the normalisation and relative acceptance uncertainties.
9.2.2 Signal modelling
The signal prediction and the modelling study of the VH(H → bb¯) signal process are performed
based on the MC samples. The VH(H → bb¯) signal process contributes in the SRs in the resolved
and boosted VH(bb¯) regimes. The fractions of event yield of the signal MC samples in the different
lepton channels are shown in Figure 9.2, where the regions are defined in Section 5.2. The WH
signal events dominate in the 1-lepton channel, and also they contribute to the 0-lepton channel
due to mis-reconstruction of a lepton. The ZH signal events with Z → (( decay dominate in the
2-lepton channel, and ZH signal events with Z → νν decay dominate in the 0-lepton channel,
while the ZH signal events in the 1-lepton channel are negligible. The gg-initiated ZH, H → bb¯
signal processes are considered in 0-lepton and 2-lepton channels. These gg-initiated processes
make a larger contribution in multiple jet regions.
Major systematic sources for the modelling of signal MC samples are outlined as follows:
• QCD scale This systematic source assesses the variation of the range of perturbative QCD
computation and non-perturbative computation by changing the renormalisation scale (µR)
and the factorisation scale (µF) in the nominal MC sample of Powheg + Pythia 8, which
are implemented as internal weights. The QCD scales vary with seven points:
[µR/µnominal

F ] = [0.5, 0.5][0.5, 1][1, 0.5][1, 1][1, 2][2, 1][2, 2]. (9.3)
The scale [1,1] is the nominal scale, hence, there are six variations.
• Parton Distribution Function (PDF) + αs The uncertainties of Parton Distribution Func￾tion (PDF) and the strong coupling constant αs is evaluated using the PDF4LHC15 30 set

• PS/UE/hadronisation (Powheg + Herwig 7) To assess the computation variations of Par￾ton Shower (PS), Underlying Event (UE), and hadronisation, the alternative MC generator

The normalisation, relative acceptance uncertainties are derived, which are optimised to the
fiducial differential cross-section measurement in the STXS framework, and shape uncertainty is
also considered in the fit. The summary of the VH signal uncertainty is shown in Table 9.2.
Normalisation uncertainty The normalisation uncertainty in the inclusive phase space is
derived from the uncertainties of cross-section and branching ratio. The inclusive cross-section
uncertainties for WH, qq¯ → ZH, gg → ZH production modes due to QCD scale variations are
0.7%, 0.6%, and 25%, respectively [26]. The branching ratio uncertainty for the H → bb¯ decay is
1.61% [26].
STXS uncertainty The fiducial differential cross-section of the VH signal process is mea￾sured in the STXS framework as discussed in Section 1.3.2. The phase space of measurement
“STXS bin” is defined in the framework, which is split by the transverse momentum of a vector
boson in truth level (pV
T
,truth) and the number of ISR jets at the truth level. Due to the systematic
variations in the signal MC models, the event migration across the STXS bins has happened. Gen￾erally, in this analysis, the relative acceptance uncertainty due to the event migration is evaluated
by the double-ratio of Eq. (9.1). However, this method allows to apply the relative acceptance
uncertainty into only the extrapolated region but not into the extrapolating region, i.e. this method
under-estimates the acceptance uncertainty in the extrapolating region, although the modification
of cross-section in all STXS bins should be performed by the relative acceptance uncertainty. In
the fiducial differential cross-section measurement, the uncertainty of event yields in each STXS
bin, referred to as “STXS uncertainty” [117], is considered by alternative method to take account
of the cross-section modification for each STXS bin. This methodology can also be applied to the
relative acceptance uncertainty of the background processes for the event migration between pV
T
regions or between the number of jet regions in the reconstructed level, since ideally the relative
acceptance uncertainty should be applied to all these regions. For the uncertainty estimation with
high granularity, the diboson production adopts the same methodology for the relative acceptance
uncertainty of pV
T and the number of jet regions in terms of QCD scale variations as discussed in

=== PDF PAGE 130 ===
116 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
Section 10.3.
The QCD scale variation is the largest uncertainty among the systematic sources. To evaluate the
STXS uncertainty by the QCD scale variation, Stewart-Tackmann (ST) method [118] is exploited.
In the ST method, the decrease of event yield in one region and the increase of event yield in
another region in the event migration are both considered in the calculation of uncertainty values,
where the 6 QCD scale variations are enveloped. The effects on the migration between pV

=== PDF PAGE 131 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 117
tainty. Since the impact of event migration due to these systematic sources is relatively small in
the fit, the STXS uncertainty is simply computed by the relative changes of the cross-section in
each STXS bin separately, apart from the ST method.
In the fiducial differential cross-section measurement in the STXS framework, the STXS uncer￾tainties are divided into two kinds: the relative acceptance uncertainty across the STXS bins, and
the normalisation uncertainty to modify the cross-section of each STXS bin. In the cross-section
measurement, the measured cross-section is not normalised by the SM predicted cross-section, so
the normalisation uncertainty of the VH signal models is not included into the uncertainty of mea￾sured cross-section. Therefore, the normalisation STXS uncertainty is not included in the profile
likelihood fit as discussed in Section 11.1.1, and only considered in the SM predictions with which
the measured cross-sections are compared as shown in Table 12.3, while the relative acceptance
STXS uncertainty is considered in the measured cross-section. Since the gg-initiated ZH signal
process are simulated at Leading Order (LO), and they provide large contribution in the multiple
jet regions as shown in Figure 9.2, the uncertainties of the SM predictions in the ≥ 1 truth ISR jet
STXS bin are larger than those of the other STXS bins.
Other uncertainty In the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, the High/Low-∆R CRs are defined based
on the ∆R(bb) variable, where is not defined in the STXS bins. Hence, relative acceptance uncer￾tainties between SRs and ∆R CRs are estimated at the reconstructed level. Here, the QCD scale
variations are only considered in this SR-CR acceptance due to their large impacts on the fit.
The shape uncertainties of QCD scale variations, and of difference between Pythia 8 and
Herwig 7 are considered. The shapes of alternative models in each STXS bin are normalised
to that of the nominal model to avoid any double counting with the normalisation and STXS un￾certainties.
9.2.3 Background modellings
V+jets(Z/W+jets), tt
¯, single top-quark, and diboson productions are modelled based on their
MC samples, while top-quark production in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime
and multi-jet production in the 1-lepton channel are modelled with the data-driven technique. Fig￾ure 9.4 shows the fraction of different background events in the different SRs and CRs in each
lepton channel of the resolved or boosted regimes, where the regions are defined in Section 5.2. In

VH(bb¯) regimes. W+jets and Z+jets are considered separately. The contribution in the 2-lepton
channel is almost dominantly composed of the Z+jets events, while that in the 1-lepton channel
consists of almost the W+jets events. The contributions of both Z/W+jets events are present in the
0-lepton channel, where the Z+jets events are more dominant, and the W+jets events are entered
due to the failure of lepton reconstruction by mis-identification or outside of detector acceptance.
Their MC events are categorised based on the flavours of two Higgs-candidate jets at the truth
level, referred to as “flavour composition”: “V+hf ” (heavy flavours) for V+bb and V+cc,“V+mf ”
(mixed flavours) for V + bc, V + bl, and V + cl, and “V + lf ” for V+jets with both light-flavour
jets. The V + bb events contribute 85% to 95% of the V+jets background in the resolved VH(bb¯)
regime, while the fraction decreases to ∼ 50% in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime due to the loose
flavour tagging requirement*1. Therefore, in the SRs of resolved and boosted VH(bb¯) regime, the
V + hf is the dominant V+jets background, while the V + mf and V + lf provide small but not
negligible contributions. The V +hf events are dominant in the High-∆R CR (0-,2-lepton channel)
and the Low-∆R CR (1-lepton channel) of the resolved VH(bb¯) regime. The V + mf events are
dominant in the High-∆R CRs of the CTCL-tag and CTN-tag regions in the VH(cc¯) regime. The
*1 ∼ 15% for the V + cc events.

=== PDF PAGE 135 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 121
V +lf events are dominant in the V +light CR in the VH(cc¯) regime. The fractions of three flavour
compositions are shown in Figure 9.4.
The normalisation factors of the V+jets events are free to float in the fit to the data, as explained
in Section 9.2.1. In the overall analysis regions, the float normalisation factors move independently
in different jet multiplicity and pV
T interval regions, but the same factors are set across the lepton
channels. With this setup, the W+jets components in SRs and CRs in the 0-lepton channel are
constrained and extrapolated from the corresponding components in the 1-lepton channel, and
the Z+jets components in SRs and CRs in the 0-lepton channel are constrained and extrapolated
from the corresponding components in the 2-lepton channel. In the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯)
regimes, they are also split by three flavour compositions, but the same factors are set across these
two regimes. With this setup, The V + hf component is constrained by the High/Low-∆R CRs in
the resolved VH(bb¯) regime (BB-tag region), the V+mf component is constrained by the High-∆R
CRs of the CTN-tag regions in the VH(cc¯) regime, and the V + lf component is constrained by the
V+light CRs of the CLN-tag regions in the VH(cc¯) regime, where the dedicated CRs are defined in
Section 5.2.1. In the High-∆R CR (BB-tag, CTN-tag) and V+light CR in the 1-,2-lepton channel,
the pV
T shape distributions are used in the fit to tackle a pV
T mis-modelling of the V+jets’s nominal
MC sample (Sherpa 2.2.11) [77], which is explained in the systematic sources below. In the Low-
∆R CR of the 1-lepton channel in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, the constraints of the W + hf
component are significantly improved by adopting the MVA (“BDTCRLow”) as defined in Section
8.1. Figure 9.5 shows the BDTCRLow post-fit distributions in Low-∆R CR, which discriminates
the W+jets events from other events such as top quark backgrounds. Compared to a fit with
only a single bin in the Low-∆R CR, the statistical uncertainty of the W + hf float normalisation
decreases by a factor of two (by ∼ 30%) in the 75 < pV
T < 150 GeV (150 < pV
T < 250 GeV) region.
Considering the constraint in the 0-lepton channel from other lepton channels, in the High-∆R CR
(BB-tag, CTN-tag) and V+light CR in the 0-lepton channel, only the event yield in each analysis
region is used in the fit. In the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, the float normalisation factors are set
inclusively in the V + hf and V + mf events, while dedicated normalisation uncertainty is derived
and applied for the V + lf events due to its small contribution.
The systematic sources of the V+jets are outlined as follows:
• QCD scale variations by factors of 0.5 to 2.0, implemented in internal weights of the nom￾inal MC model (Sherpa 2.2.11).
• PDF+αs variations, implemented in internal weights of the nominal MC model
(Sherpa 2.2.11).
• Different schemes of NLO electroweak (EW) correction to the ME computation at NLO

Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 123
Top quark process modelling in the 0,1-lepton channels
Top quark process is one of the major backgrounds in the SRs of the resolved and boosted
VH(bb¯) regimes*2 in the 0-,1-lepton channels. As at least one W boson in the top quark pro￾cess decays leptonically, the events enter into the 0- and 1-lepton channel, where the events in
the 0-lepton channel are present due to the failure of lepton reconstruction by mis-identification
or outside of detector acceptance. Its contribution in this analysis increases as the number of jet
region increases, and decreases as pV
T increases. The top quark background consists of tt
¯ pro￾duction and single top (t-channel, s-channel, and Wt-channel) productions. The major top quark
backgrounds are stemmed from tt
¯ production and single top Wt-channel production, which are
treated inclusively to minimise the impact of mutual interference between these two productions
given that they have the very similar final state topology in this analysis phase space. The relative
fraction of the Wt-channel production varies from 5% to 15 % as pV
T increases in the resolved
and boosted VH(bb¯) regime. The single top t-channel production can be observed mainly in the
1-lepton channel in the resolved and boosted VH(bb¯) regimes, where its relative fraction of the top
quark background is ∼ 10 % in the low pV
T SRs. The single top s-channel production is negligible
in all the analysis regions. The modelling of tt
¯+ single top Wt-channel and single top t/s-channels
are considered separately.
The tt
¯ and single top Wt-channel backgrounds are categorised according to the flavour com￾position of two Higgs-candidate jets: “Top(bb)” for the top quark process containing two b-jets,
“Top(bq)” for the top quark process containing a b-jet and either a c- or light flavour jet from
the W boson decay, and “Top(qq)” for the top quark process containing only c- or light flavour
jets from the W boson decay. In the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, the Top(bb) component is a lead￾ing background and the Top(bq) component is a sub-leading background among all the top quark
backgrounds in the SRs. Here, the dedicated CRs for each of Top(bb) and Top(bq) components
play an important role for modelling both components in the same SRs, where the dedicated CRs
are defined in Section 5.2.1. The Top(bb) component provides a dominant or large contribution in
the High-∆R CRs of both the lepton channels, since it has a feature of a large angle of ∆R between
the b-tagged Higgs-candidate jets. The Top(bq) component provides the dominant contribution in
the Top CRs in the BCT-tag region, where its relative fraction in top quark backgrounds is up to
70%. In the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, the Top(bb) and Top(bq) components are sub-leading back￾grounds in the SRs, and dominant backgrounds in the Top CRs. The Top(bq) component in the
boosted VH(bb¯) regime is a major top quark background, where its relative fraction in top quark
backgrounds is up to 85% in both the SRs and Top CRs, since the large-R jet naturally captures
*2 The top quark process in the VH(cc¯) regime (except BCT-tag region) is suppressed thanks to a veto on b-tagged jets.

=== PDF PAGE 138 ===
124 9.2 Modelling uncertainties
the full hadronic decay of a top quark. The Top(qq) component is negligible in the resolved and
boosted VH(bb¯) regime*3.
In the resolved VHbb regime, the Top(bb) component and the combined Top(bq) and Top(qq)
component, referred to as “Top(bq/qq)”, are treated separately because the different dedicated
CRs are used for each. The fractions of the Top(bb), Top(bq/qq), and single top t/s-channel
components are shown in Figure 9.4 (a)(b). In the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, all the Top(bb),
Top(bq), Top(qq) components are combined, referred to as “Top(boosted)”. The fractions of the
Top(boosted) and single top t/s-channel components are shown in Figure 9.4 (d).
The nominal MC sample of the top quark background is Powheg + Pythia 8. In the modelling
study and the estimation of systematic uncertainty of the top quark background, the systematic
sources come from two alternative MC samples, MadGraph5 aMC@NLO + Pythia 8 for ME
generator variation, Powheg + Herwig 7 for PS generator variation, and the internal parameter
variation for the ISR and FSR in the alternative MC sample (MadGraph5 aMC@NLO + Pythia 8).
tt
¯ and single top Wt-channel modelling in the 0,1-lepton channels
The normalisation factors of the tt
¯ and single top Wt-channel events are free to float in the fit to
the data, as explained in Section 9.2.1. The float normalisation factors move independently in
different jet multiplicity and pV
T interval regions. They are also split by Top(bb) and Top(bq/qq)
components, but the same factors are set across both the 0-,1-lepton channels and across the re￾solved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes. The Top(bb) component in the SR in the resolved VH(bb¯)
regime is constrained from the High-∆R CR in the same regime, while the Top(bq/qq) component
in the SR in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime is constrained by the corresponding component in the
BCT-tag Top CR. In the case of the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, the same float normalisation factors
are set for the inclusive Top(boosted) component. The Top(boosted) component in the SR in the
boosted VH(bb¯) regime is constrained from the Top CR in the same regime. All the top quark
components in the 0-lepton channel are constrained by the 1-lepton channel. In the High-∆R CR
of the 0-lepton channel, only the event yield in each analysis region is used in the fit to constrain
the Top(bb) component in SRs, because the component in the 0-lepton channel is constrained from
the 1-lepton channel. On the other hand, in the High-∆R CR of the 1-lepton channel, the pV
T shape
distributions are used in the fit to prioritise the V+jets modelling as discussed above. Since the
Top(bq) and Top(boosted) component tends to be reconstructed with the b quark and hadronic
W boson decayed from the same top quark as explained in Figure 5.4, the invariant mass of two
Higgs-candidate jets (mbc) of Top(bq) component tends to be around the Higgs mass (≈125 GeV)
*3 This component mainly contributes to the VH(cc¯) regime, and constrained from BCT-tag region similar to the
Top(bq) component.

in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime and Top(boosted) component to be the top mass (172.5 GeV) in
the boosted VH(bb¯) regime*4. Therefore, usage of mbc/mJ as the fit variable is effective to isolate
the contribution of mainly Top(bq) component in the Top CRs of both the BCT-tag region and the
boosted VH(bb¯) regime. Figure 9.6 (a)(b) show the post-fit distributions of Top CRs using mbc/mJ
in the BCT-tag region, and in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, respectively.

Top quark process modelling in the 2-lepton channel
Top quark events, mainly di-leptonic tt
¯ events, contribute in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved
VH(bb¯) regimes. The relative fractions of top quark backgrounds are shown in Figure 9.4 (c),
labelled as “Top(2L)”. For the modelling of top quark process, the data-driven approach is used to
estimate the top quark background in the SR, and the Top eµ CR as defined in Section 5.2.2 is ex￾ploited. Due to the very high purity of top quark background in the Top eµ CRs (the fraction varies
99.5 % - 99.9 %), the distributions of data in the Top eµ CRs can be used for the distribution of top
quark background in the SR with scale factors estimated by the MC simulation. The normalisation
uncertainty comes from these scale factors. In the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, the tt
¯ contribution is
negligible thanks to the cut on pV
T ≥ 400 GeV and the Z boson mass selection requirement.
Dibson modelling
The diboson production consists of five processes: qq-initiated WZ, ZZ, WW, and gg-initiated
ggWW, ggZZ. Because of the b-tagging event selection, the major diboson processes are WZ(W →
leptons, Z → bb¯), and ZZ(Z → leptons, Z → bb¯) in the resolved and boosted VH(bb¯). These
processes contribute primarily into the SRs as shown in Figure 9.4, since it has the same topol￾ogy as the VH signal production. They are exploited as the signal events in the VZ signal
strength measurement to validate the estimation of other background processes before the VH
signal strength measurements, as discussed in Section 1.4.3. In the resolved VH(bb¯) regime,
WZ(Z → bb¯), ZZ(Z → bb¯) processes contribute to ∼ 90% of the whole diboson production events,
while the ratio of these processes decreases to ∼ 60% in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime due to the
loose tagging requirement. The WZ(Z → bb¯) process distributes in the 0- and 1-lepton channels,
and the ZZ(Z → bb¯) process distributes in the 0- and 2-lepton channels. The WZ(Z → bb¯) events
in the 1-lepton channel and the ZZ(Z → bb¯) events in the 2-lepton channel can be observed domi￾nantly, so these events extrapolate the corresponding diboson events in the 0-lepton channel. Since
the diboson production is a minor background and it doesn’t have any dedicated CRs as opposed
to the major background processes above, the normalisation factor of the diboson process in the
VH signal fit is fixed to the SM prediction. The studies of the diboson modelling are discussed in

=== PDF PAGE 141 ===
Chapter 9. Systematic uncertainties 127
Chapter 10.
Multi-jet modelling
The multi-jet background in the 1-lepton channel cannot be accurately estimated through MC
simulation due to practical limitations in generating sufficient events for the analysis phase space.
Therefore, a data-driven approach has been adopted to estimate the impact of this background. This
approach extracts the MVA final discriminant shape of the multi-jet background in the SR from
an orthogonal Control Region enriched with multi-jet events, referred to as “mJCR”. The mJCR is
defined with the same event selection requirements as the SR except for the lepton isolation cut.
Since the isolation cut for the lepton suppresses the multi-jet events, the mJCR can be obtained by
requiring the inversion of the isolation cut. Because there are also the contributions of top (tt
¯,Wt)
and V+jets processes in the mJCR, the nominal multi-jet shape is formed by subtracting the MC
samples of these processes from the data. The uncertainty comes from the shape variations in the
several isolation cases or in the several lepton trigger cases, and the normalisation variations of the
MC samples.

=== PDF PAGE 143 ===
129
Chapter 10
Improvement of systematic study for
diboson modelling
This chapter summarises the systematic study for diboson modelling. Section 10.1 shows the
motivation of the diboson modelling study and strategy for the diboson modelling. Section 10.2
outlines the alternative models for the diboson process, and Section 10.3 summarises the deviation
of normalisation, relative acceptance, and shape uncertainty. Particularly, a systematic study for
the shape uncertainty is improved by introducing a high performance estimation method; this is
discussed in Section 10.4.
10.1 Diboson modelling motivation and strategy
In the previous analysis, the modelling uncertainty of diboson process resulted in the second￾largest impact on the measurement of signal strength for the VH(H → bb¯) signal process among
the modelling uncertainties of the background processes [12, 28], as shown in Table K.1. Besides,
in the validation analysis to measure signal strength of the VZ(Z → bb¯) process, the modelling un￾certainty of diboson process gives the largest impact on the measurement among all the modelling
uncertainties. In this thesis, the modelling study of the diboson production is motivated by the
following two points.
A. Update all the modelling uncertainties The phase space of this analysis is slightly
changed from the previous analyses [12, 28], e.g. 75 < pV
T < 150 GeV interval region
in the 1-lepton channel, and 4-jet region in the 0-lepton channel are added, besides > 3-jet
region in the 2-lepton channel is split into 3-jet and > 4-jet regions. Moreover, the nom￾inal MC sample is upgraded from Sherpa 2.2.1 to Sherpa 2.2.11 by tuning the generator

=== PDF PAGE 144 ===
130 10.1 Diboson modelling motivation and strategy
parameters to adjust the bottom quark production and hadronisation properties to the world
average values [77, 121], and by increasing the number of sample events that results in the
decrease of statistical uncertainty of MC sample by ∼ 50%. Therefore, all the modelling
uncertainties of the diboson process need to be re-estimated for this analysis.
B. Study the modelling uncertainty with high performance method In the previous analy￾ses, the modelling uncertainties of the diboson process are estimated by a bare minimal es￾timation method, which results in under-estimation or over-estimation of the uncertainties.
Especially, this problem is serious*1 in the estimation of shape uncertainty from the alterna￾tive MC samples, as discussed in Section 10.4. Because the diboson modelling uncertainty
gives a large impact on the signal strength measurements of both the VH(H → bb¯) and
VZ(Z → bb¯) processes, the modelling uncertainty should be estimated properly by higher
performance estimation method than the previous method.
The diboson process consists of five production modes: qq-initiated WW, WZ, ZZ, and gg￾initiated ggWW, ggZZ. In this analysis, the following semi-leptonic decay processes contribute
to the property measurement of the VH(H → bb¯) process: ZZ(Z → leptons, Z → hadrons),
WZ(W → leptons, Z → hadrons), ZW(Z → leptons, W → hadrons), and WW(W →
leptons, W → hadrons). Flavour composition of hadrons decayed from a Z/W boson is defined
by the MC samples at the truth level. The flavour composition of hadronic Z decay consists of
Z → bb¯ (Zbb), Z → cc¯ (Zcc), Z → light-flavour included jets (Zbkg), while the hadronic W decay
(Whad) doesn’t contain b-jets (W → cl, W → ll, where l denotes light-flavour jets here). Among
these semi-leptonic diboson processes, WZbb and ZZbb are the major processes in the resolved
and boosted VH(bb¯) regimes, and they concentrate in the SRs due to the topological similarity
with the VH(H → bb¯) signal process. The VZcc, VZbkg, VWhad provide large contribution in the
VH(cc¯) regime, and small contribution in both the VH(bb¯) regimes. For the gg-initiated processes,
only the ggZZbb process contribute less than 10% in the event fraction of diboson processes in the
resolved VH(bb¯) regime, while the other gg-initiated processes are negligible. The detailed event
fractions of the different diboson processes in each region/regime are summarised in Appendix
H.1.
Because the diboson production is a minor background and it doesn’t have any dedicated CRs
as opposed to the other background processes, such as V+jets and top quark productions, the
normalisation factor of the diboson process in the VH signal fit is fixed to the SM prediction in the
VH(H → bb¯) property measurements.
*1 The estimation of relative acceptance uncertainty for pV
T , jet multiplicity regions has also this problem, as discussed
in Section 10.3.2.

=== PDF PAGE 145 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 131
For the validation of this analysis, the WZbb and (qq/gg)ZZbb processes are exploited as the
signal events in the VZ(Z → bb¯) signal strength measurement, which is discussed in Section 1.4.3.
Meanwhile, the other diboson processes are treated as background processes as ever. Hence, the
BDTVZ distributions of WZbb and ZZbb processes have a peak around ∼ 1 BDT score, while
those of the other processes have a peak around ∼−1 BDT score. The distributions of BDTVZ
for different diboson processes are shown in Appendix H.2. The same modelling uncertainties
are applied in both the signal strength measurement of VH(H → bb¯) process and VZ(Z → bb¯)
process.
10.2 Alternative models
The systematic sources for the modelling of qq-initiated diboson processes are outlined as fol￾lows:
• QCD scale This systematic source assesses the variations of the perturbative QCD com￾putation by changing the renormalisation scale (µR) and the factorisation scale (µF) in the
nominal MC sample of Sherpa 2.2.11, which are implemented as internal weights. The
QCD scales have six variations as shown in Eq. (9.3)
• Parton Distribution Function (PDF) + αs The uncertainties of Parton Distribution Func￾tion (PDF) and the strong coupling constant αs is evaluated using the PDF4LHC15 30 set
and following the “PDF4LHC recommendations for LHC Run II” [116]. A two-sided PDF
variation and a two-sided αs variation are available as internal weights of the nominal MC
sample (Sherpa 2.2.11), which is prepared by the “LHAPDF” tool [122].
• NLO electroweak (EW) correction The default Matrix Element (ME) computation of
Sherpa 2.2.11 is conducted at next-to-leading (NLO) accuracy in QCD up to two extra par￾tons. An additional correction at NLO accuracy in electroweak (EW) is applied in the ME
computation for an accurate description of vector bosons [76, 77], which takes an impor￾tant role in the BSM search by probing high-pV
T phase space [123]. By applying the EW
correction, cross-section modification in the high-pV
T region is conducted. The NLO EW
correction is calculated by OPENLOOPS [66] and implemented in Sherpa 2.2.11 in addi￾tion to the NLO QCD terms according to three schemes: “additive scheme”, “multiplicative
scheme”, and “exponentiated scheme”. Since the corrected pV
T shape by the additive scheme
is the closest to the default NLO QCD shape, the additive scheme is adopted as the nominal
model, while the other two schemes are treated as systematic variations of the EW correc￾tion relative to the additive scheme. The cross-section computed in Sherpa 2.2.11 at NLO

=== PDF PAGE 146 ===
132 10.2 Alternative models
QCD accuracy is exploited for normalising the diboson events, but the NLO EW correction
is adopted in the nominal model, which results in the decrease of its cross-section. By ap￾plying the EW correction, the cross-section decreases ∼ 3% and the event yields decrease
by ZZ:∼ 7.4%, WZ:∼ 3.8%, and WW:∼ 4.6%. Since this decrease is small enough against
the normalisation uncertainty below, the modification of diboson event yields under the
normalisation uncertainty in the fit would consider the effects of the EW correction. The
detailed implementations and effects on the final results are discussed in Appendix H.3.1.
• ME + PS/UE/hadronisation (Powheg + Pythia 8) To assess the computation variations
of ME, Parton Shower (PS), Underlying Event (UE), and hadronisation, the alternative MC
generator of Powheg ME generator, interfaced to the Pythia 8 PS/UE generator, is used
as the replacement of the nominal MC sample (Sherpa 2.2.11). The CT10 NLO set [124]
is used for the PDF of the hard scattering process, while the CTEQ6L1 PDF set [125] is
used for the PS/UE configured by the AZNLO tune. The EvtGen v1.2.0 program is used
for properties of the hadronisation of bottom and charm quarks. The Powheg + Pythia 8
events are normalised to the cross-sections computed by this alternative MC generator at
NLO QCD accuracy.
• Fragmentation function of Sherpa (Sherpa 2.2.1) Besides adjusting the simulation pa￾rameters of the bottom quark decay process in the change of Sherpa version, its fragmen￾tation function is also changed [77]. This change results in a shift of the peak of invariant
mass of two b-jets toward higher mass in the VZbb processes between Sherpa 2.2.1 and
Sherpa 2.2.11. This shift is also observed in the truth level. However, the modelling of frag￾mentation function is not well-calibrated with the data. Hence, in this analysis, Sherpa 2.2.1
is used as the fragmentation systematic variation in the Sherpa generator. The Sherpa 2.2.1
events are normalised to the cross-sections computed by this alternative MC generator at
NLO QCD accuracy. The detailed differences of Sherpa version and its effects on this thesis
are discussed in Appendix H.3.2.
Since the cross-section of gg-initiated processes are small comparing to the qq-initiated pro￾cesses, the systematic sources of the gg-initiated diboson processes have a small impact on this
analysis. Hence, the systematic uncertainty derived from diboson analyses in the ATLAS experi￾ment [126, 127] is applied to the gg-initiated diboson processes. Because the relative acceptance
uncertainty and shape uncertainty must be derived in the dedicated phase space of this analysis,
only the normalisation uncertainty of gg-initiated diboson process is considered in this analysis.

=== PDF PAGE 147 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 133
10.3 Derivation of modelling uncertainty
According to the modelling strategy as discussed in Section 9.2.1, the normalisation, relative
acceptance, and shape uncertainties for the diboson process are derived.
10.3.1 Diboson normalisation uncertainty
For the diboson process, normalisation uncertainty is dominated by total acceptance uncer￾tainty in the inclusive analysis phase space. Normalisation uncertainty is derived separately for
ZZ, WZ, WW, and ggVV productions. For ZZ and WZ productions, the total acceptance uncer￾tainties of the VZbb process in the resolved and boosted VH(bb¯) regimes are applied to all ZZ,
WZ productions, and the values in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime are shared in the VH(cc¯) regime
because the VZ(bb¯) process is dominant in the VH(bb¯) regime. For WW production, the total
acceptance uncertainties derived in the VH(cc¯) regime are used in all the regimes, because it’s
dominated in the VH(cc¯) regime. For ggVV production, the normalisation uncertainty that is used
in other ATLAS diboson analyses [126, 127] is used. All the normalisation uncertainties are com￾puted by enveloping every kind of systematic sources with a quadrature-sum calculation by Eq.
(9.2). The derived values of normalisation uncertainties are shown in Appendix H.4.1.
10.3.2 Diboson relative acceptance uncertainty
Because the diboson modelling is estimated separately for ZZbb/ZZcc, WZbb/WZcc, ZZbkg,
WZbkg, ZWhad, and WWhad processes, the relative acceptance uncertainties across the analysis
categories are derived separately based on these different diboson processes. The uncertainties
of VZbb process are derived in the VH(bb¯) regimes, since they are dominant among the diboson
processes in the SRs of the VH(bb¯) regimes. Since Z → bb¯ and Z → cc¯ decays in the diboson pro￾cesses have the same topology and their differences only come from the jet flavour tagging, VZbb
and VZcc processes in all three regimes share the same uncertainties derived in the corresponding
VH(bb¯) regimes for the VZbb process*2. For the other diboson processes in the resolved VH(bb¯)
and VH(cc¯) regimes, their uncertainties are derived in the VH(cc¯) regime because these processes
are rather dominant there, and their uncertainties are also applied in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime.
For the other diboson processes in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime, the uncertainties are derived and
applied in the same regime.
The relative uncertainties across the following analysis categories are considered. Generally, the
*2 The same trends for the uncertainty values of VZcc derived in the VH(cc¯) regime is observed. This decision is
motivated by harmonisation between the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes.

=== PDF PAGE 148 ===
134 10.3 Derivation of modelling uncertainty
value of relative acceptance uncertainties of each systematic variation is calculated by Eq. (9.1),
except for the uncertainties between pV
T regions and between the jet-multiplicity regions due to
the QCD scale variations. The relative acceptance uncertainties used for the fit are derived by
enveloping every kind of systematic sources with quadrature-sum calculation by Eq. (9.2).
• Lepton channels Because the WZbb/WZcc, WZbkg, and WWhad events are dominant in
the 1-lepton channels and the ZZbb/ZZcc, ZZbkg, and ZWhad events are dominant in the 2-
lepton channels, these events extrapolate the corresponding diboson events in the 0-lepton
channel. Hence, the relative acceptance uncertainties from the 1- to 0-lepton channel, or
from the 2- to 0-lepton channel for each diboson process are considered.
• SR and ∆R CR The diboson process is distributed across the SRs and ∆R CRs. The SRs
in the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes have more diboson events than the High-∆R
CRs and Low-∆R CRs, so the extrapolation uncertainties from SR to ∆R CR are applied.
In the 1-lepton channel, the Low-∆R CR is extrapolated from SR, and the High-∆R CR is
extrapolated from an inclusive region of SR and Low-∆R CR. The values are calculated in
the different diboson processes and the different lepton channels.
• Jet multiplicity (Njet) regions This uncertainty is extrapolated from a low number of
jets (Njet) region and applied to a high Njet region. Only the Njet uncertainties in the re￾solved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes are considered. The values are calculated in different
processes and different lepton channels. The QCD scale variation is not considered in the
uncertainties of the WZbb/cc and ZZbb/cc processes, since they are estimated separately
as discussed below.
• pV
T interval regions The 150 < pV
T < 250 GeV interval region is always selected as
the high purity region and extrapolating to other pV
T regions in the resolved VH(bb¯) and
VH(cc¯) regimes. The pV
T > 600 GeV interval region is extrapolated from the 400 < pV
T <
600 GeV interval region in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime. The values are calculated in the
different diboson processes and the different lepton channels. The QCD scale variation is
not considered in the uncertainties of the WZbb/cc and ZZbb/cc processes, since they are
estimated separately as discussed below.
• pV
T ⊗ Njet regions with ST method for QCD scale variation The relative acceptance un￾certainties between pV
T ⊗ Njet region for the WZbb/cc and ZZbb/cc processes due to QCD
scale variations are considered by the same methodology as the STXS uncertainty, which is
discussed in Section 9.2.2, since the QCD scale variations may cause large yield migration
across these regions. These uncertainties are computed with “Steward-Tackmann” (ST)
method. This is the first time to exploit the ST method in the background process in this

=== PDF PAGE 149 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 135
analysis. This ST method [118] helps to evaluate the approximate yield migration uncer￾tainty, which is usually under-estimated with the default method Eq. (9.1), and it proves
its full potential when enveloping the several variations from the same source to evaluate
each migration uncertainty; Enveloping 6 variations of QCD scale in this case. The values
for pV
T migration uncertainties are computed for different diboson processes in the resolved
VH(bb¯) regime. The values for Njet migration uncertainties are computed in each pV
T inter￾val region for different diboson processes in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime.
The detailed calculation and the derived values of relative acceptance uncertainties are shown in
Appendix H.4.1.
10.3.3 Diboson shape uncertainty
The shape uncertainties of the fit variables, which are explained in Section 11.2, for different
systematic sources, as listed below, are estimated separately. The PDF+αs variation is negligible.
The shapes of alternative models in each analysis region are normalised to that of the nominal
model to avoid any double counting with the normalisation and relative acceptance uncertainties.
The shape plots of the final discriminant variables (BDTVH and BDTVZ) for the different systematic
sources are shown in Appendix H.4.2.
• QCD scale variation This variation is evaluated by internal weights of the nominal MC
sample Sherpa 2.2.11. The variation of renormalisation scale (µR) and factorisation scale
(µF) by a factor 2 to the nominal scales, which is evaluated in the ME only due to shape
fluctuation, are considered for the shape uncertainty. It is applied to all the SRs and ∆R
CRs, and decorrelated by the boundary of pV
T = 400 GeV.
• NLO Electroweak (EW) correction variation This variation is evaluated by internal
weights of the nominal MC sample Sherpa 2.2.11. The multiplicative scheme of the EW
correction is considered as shape uncertainty relative to the additive scheme that is adopted
as the nominal EW correction, because it’s the largest one of the two available alternative
combination schemes. It is applied to all the SRs and ∆R CRs, and decorrelated by the
boundary of pV
T = 400 GeV.
• ME, PS/UE/hadronisation variation This variation is evaluated by the comparison of
Powheg + Pythia 8 and Sherpa 2.2.11. Since the shape of Powheg + Pythia 8 is not
corrected at NLO EW, it is compared with that of Sherpa 2.2.11 without the additional
NLO EW correction. To estimate the shape uncertainty of the fit variables by suppressing
the shape fluctuations of Powheg + Pythia 8, the shape of Powheg + Pythia 8 is reproduced

=== PDF PAGE 150 ===
136 10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty between MC generators
by reweighting the shape of Sherpa 2.2.11 exploiting the CARL method as described in
Section 10.4.3. The shape uncertainties are applied in the inclusive SR and ∆R CR. They
are decorrelated in the different diboson productions and in the different lepton channels:
ZZ in the 0-,2-lepton channels, and WZ in the 0-,1-lepton channels in the resolved and
boosted VH(bb¯) regimes*3. They are also decorrelated by the boundary of pV
T = 400 GeV.
In the fit, the shape uncertainty is applied to the nominal shape of Sherpa 2.2.11 with NLO
EW correction.
• Fragmentation function variation This variation is evaluated by the comparison of
Sherpa 2.2.1 and Sherpa 2.2.11. Since the shape of Sherpa 2.2.1 is not corrected at NLO
EW, it is compared with that of Sherpa 2.2.11 without the additional NLO EW correction.
Similarly to the estimation of shape uncertainty from Powheg + Pythia 8, the shape un￾certainty from Sherpa 2.2.1 is estimated by a reweighting technique exploiting the CARL
method. The shape uncertainties are decorrelated in the different diboson productions and
in the different lepton channels: ZZ in the 0-,2-lepton channels, and WZ in the 0-,1-lepton
channels in the resolved and boosted VH(bb¯) regimes*2. They are also decorrelated by the
boundary of pV
T = 400 GeV. In the fit, the shape uncertainty is applied to the nominal shape
of Sherpa 2.2.11 with NLO EW correction.
10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty
between MC generators
As explained in Section 9.2.1 and Section 10.3.3, the shape variations of fit variables between
nominal and alternative MC samples are estimated by reweighting technique. In this section, to
examine the estimation method of the shape uncertainty for an alternative MC sample, the ZZ
diboson production mode in the 2-lepton channel of the resolved VH(bb¯) regime is focused, and
the variation of Sherpa 2.2.11 and Powheg + Pythia 8 is used.
10.4.1 Improvement of reweighting technique
For the modelling study of the physics process, the MC sample is used for sampling the probabil￾ity density functions (p.d.f.) f(x) of the analysis variable, where x = {x1, x2, ...} are the kinematic
variables (e.g. mbb, pV
T). Here, the p.d.f.s vary between the nominal MC sample f(x | y = 0) and
the alternative MC sample f(x | y = 1), and this is considered as the shape uncertainty. By learning
*3 In the VH(cc¯) regime, to assess the shape uncertainty of Zcc, Zbkg, Whad too, ZZ in the 0-,2-lepton channels, WZ
in the three lepton channels, and WW in the 0-,1-lepton channels are decorrelated.

=== PDF PAGE 151 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 137
these kinematic variables as BDT inputs, the BDT final discriminant scores (BDTVH, BDTVZ) are
defined as discussed in Chapter 8. As described in Section 11.2, not only the kinematic variables,
the BDT final discriminant scores (BDTVH, BDTVZ) are also used as the fit variables, which are
considered as the shape uncertainty in the modelling. For example, Figure 10.1 (a) shows the
shape uncertainty of BDTVH histogram between the nominal MC generator of Sherpa 2.2.11 and
the alternative MC generator of Powheg + Pythia 8, after normalising both histograms with their
event yields in a certain region. The bottom panel of Figure 10.1 (a) is a ratio of the normalised
alternative MC model relative to the normalised nominal MC model, referred to as “shape-shift”,
and shape-shift is applied as the nuisance parameter of shape uncertainty in the fit, which is defined
in Section 11.1.1.
Due to limited computational resources, it’s impractical to prepare the MC samples of equal
size (unweighted statistics) for all the MC models in a physics analysis. Thus, the size of alter￾native MC sample is generally less than half of the size of nominal MC sample, e.g. the size of
Sherpa 2.2.11 is ∼ 16 times larger than that of Powheg + Pythia 8. This results in the limitation
of statistical precision of the shape variation in each bin of a histogram in the fit, which causes
the fit stability issue. Moreover, this results in the large statistical fluctuation of shape as shown
in Figure 10.1 (a), which causes over-estimation of the modelling uncertainty in the property mea￾surements. Especially, the large statistical fluctuation of BDTVH ∼ 1 shape of the background
process provides large impact on the VH(H → bb¯) signal strength measurements.
To tackle the issues above, it’s necessary to have a strategy to approximate the shape of alter￾native model by suppressing its statistical fluctuation. As the effective strategy, additional event
weight w(x) is estimated event by event, and applied to the nominal MC sample to reproduce the
shape of the alternative MC sample so that the shape variation would be evaluated with the same
statistics, which is referred to as “reweight”, given by
f(x | y = 0) → w(x) · f(x | y = 0) ∼ f(x | y = 1). (10.1)
Figure 10.1 (b) illustrates the strategy of the reweighting technique. This reweighting technique
is a well-established method in particle physics that is used in the estimation of shape uncertainty
[115].
To get the p.d.f.s of the reweighted nominal MC model (blue line) closer to that of the alternative
MC sample (red line), the ideal weight w(x) should be estimated as the ratio
w(x) = f(x | y = 1)
f(x | y = 0), (10.2)
where w(x) and f(x) are multi-dimensional probability distribution functions. When applying the
event weight w(x) to the analysis variables of nominal MC sample f(x | y = 0), the weight w(x)

=== PDF PAGE 152 ===
138 10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty between MC generators
must be converted to one-dimensional p.d.f. Here, the w(x) cannot be derived by the ratio of the
BDT output (one-dimensional p.d.f.) of nominal and alternative MC samples since eventually
the statistical fluctuation is included in the ratio, so the w(x) should be estimated by some input
kinematic variables x. Then the w(x) corresponding to the events in bins with low statistics bins
(e.g. BDTVH ∼ 1) can be parameterised by the input variables x in bins with enough statistics.
However, since there are numerous dimensions for the BDT input variables x and the x have
correlation with each other, the derivation of the ratio Eq. (10.2) is non-trivial in the estimation of
w(x) with the limited size of samples in the one-dimensional p.d.f. format.
In the previous analysis, because of time constraints, the event weights w(x) of the diboson
events are parameterised by only one mbb kinematic variable, whose methodology is discussed in
Section 10.4.2.
To reweight the shape of BDT final discriminant score more accurately, it’s demanded to con￾sider the shape variations of all the BDT input variables and to parameterise the event weights w(x)
with all the BDT input variables, called as “multi-dimensional parameterisation”. The accurate es￾timation and implementation of the event weight w(x) is difficult with the limited statistics of MC
samples. Here, since the BDT input variables have multi-dimensional correlation, a likelihood
function (multi-dimensional probability density function) is the most effective tool that can learn
all the variations of input variables and their correlations and that can provide one-dimensional
output, In this analysis, the event weights w(x) are derived by multi-dimensional parameterisation
using a deep neural network (NN) technique*4 in CARL framework [115] as discussed in Sec￾tion 10.4.3. This new reweighting method strongly satisfies the motivation [B.] of the diboson
modelling study explained in Section 10.1.
10.4.2 mbb-based reweighting
The simplest way to derive the event weight w(x) is to calculate the ratio of Eq. (10.2) by the
p.d.f. of the most effective kinematic variable for considering the shape variations of analysis
variables including the BDT final discriminant scores. Because the mbb is the most discriminant
kinematic variable in the BDT input variables, the effect of mbb shape variation is most propagated
to the shape variation of the BDT final discriminant scores. Therefore, in this reweighting method,
the mbb is chosen to parameterise the event weight w(xmbb ) in one dimension to approximate the
w(x): w(xmbb ) → w(x).
*4 BDT-based reweighting method was available as the multi-dimensional parameterisation in the previous analyses.
The CARL is preferably implemented in the analysis framework, and has more accuracy (e.g. for learning pV
T
shape).

=== PDF PAGE 153 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 139

In the reweighting of the analysis variables such as the BDT final discriminant score, the weight
value is calculated from the weight function w(xmbb ) with a mbb value in an event, and the weight
value is multiplied by the total event weight in the same event of the mbb.
10.4.3 Calibrated Likelihood Ratio Estimator (CARL) reweighting
Mechanism and training model
The Calibrated Likelihood Ratio Estimator (CARL) [115] is a framework to estimate the weight
w(x) based on the deep neural network (NN). The NN is reconstructed from a Multi-Layer Per￾ceptron (MLP), and is trained with kinematic variables including all the BDT input variables x to

=== PDF PAGE 155 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 141
distinguish between the nominal events (y = 0) and alternative events (y = 1) as a binary classifi￾cation model. The output of the trained NN D(x) for an event with all the BDT input variables x is
given as one-dimensional continuous score y, where the output score of the MLP is calculated by
the Softmax function to derive the score between 0 to 1. As the number of events N in the training
increases, the output score D(x) approximates to the probability that the event with x is classified
as the alternative MC model P(y = 1 | x), given by Eq. (10.5).
D(x) N−→∞ −−−−−→ P(y = 1 | x). (10.5)
Hence, the output D(x) can be used as an estimator for the probability P(y = 1 | x), which is
the advantage of using the CARL method in the estimation of the weight w(x) under the multi￾dimensional parameterisation.
The p.d.f. f(x | y = 0) of the nominal MC events and f(x | y = 1) of the alternative MC events
can be expressed with the probability as a function of x: P(x),
f(x | y = 0) = P(x | y = 0), (10.6)
f(x | y = 1) = P(x | y = 1). (10.7)
The ratio from Eq. (10.2) can then be rewritten as
w(x) = f(x | y = 0)
f(x | y = 1) = P(x | y = 0)
P(x | y = 1). (10.8)
Using the Bayes theorem, Eq. (10.8) can be turned into
w(x) = P(y = 0 | x)
P(y = 1 | x)
P(y = 1)
P(y = 0). (10.9)
Since both the nominal and alternative MC events are normalised to the same value, the terms
P(y = 1) and P(y = 0) are canceled out. Using unitarity, the ratio can be expressed as
w(x) = 1 − P(y = 1 | x)
P(y = 1 | x) . (10.10)
An estimator w∗(x) for w(x) can be then computed directly from the trained NN output as
w∗
(x) = 1 − D(x)
D(x) , (10.11)
since w∗(x) −→ w(x) because of Eq. (10.5). This estimator w∗(x) for the ratio is used in the event
reweighting as described in Section 10.4.1.
Training configuration
The training in the CARL framework exploits “carl-torch” [128], which is the NN package
based on PyTorch. The setup in the NN is listed as follows.

=== PDF PAGE 156 ===
142 10.4 Improvement of systematic study for shape uncertainty between MC generators
• MLP: 3 layers with the number of neurons equivalent to the number of input kinematic
variables
• Activation function in the MLP: LeRU function in hidden modes, Softmax function in out￾put
• Loss function: categorical cross-entropy
• The number of training iterations (epoch): 300 times
The epoch number is optimised to maximise the reweighting performance such as closure dis￾cussed in Section 10.4.4.
The training in the CARL is done for each diboson production separately in each lepton channel
and in each regime, while the training is done inclusively in pV
T, the number of jet*5, and SR/∆RCR
regions. The input variables in the CARL training are the BDT input variables and auxiliary
variables (the number of jets, truth flavour composition). The sample used in the CARL training is
produced by using the truth tagging method as described in Section 4.4.4 to increase the number
of events N for better traing performance, while the diboson sample in the fit is produced by using
the direct tagging method to get rid of bias from the truth tagging. 75% of sample events are used
for the training, while 25% of events are used as the test sample for the validation.
Training validation
The training performance is confirmed with the ROC curve. The trained NN is validated with
the transition of loss function as a function of the number of epochs, and with the closure between
the shape of reweighted nominal MC events and that of alternative MC events as discussed in
Section 10.4.4.
10.4.4 Performance comparison between CARL and mbb-based reweighting
Evaluating and quantifying the closure
The goal of reweighting technique is to make the p.d.f. of the reweighted nominal MC sample
as close as possible to the p.d.f. of the alternative MC sample. The closure between the reweighted
nominal histogram and the alternative histogram is the most important evaluation metric of the
reweighting performance. The closure is assessed visually by residuals of each bin in the his￾togram, and is also quantified by the χ2 test.
The residual represents the deviation of the entry of reweighted nominal histogram from the
*5 If the reweighting performance is not qualified for the fit, e.g. the closure of reweighted nominal and alternative
samples is bad, the pV
T , or the number of jet region is split in the training.

=== PDF PAGE 157 ===
Chapter 10. Improvement of systematic study for diboson modelling 143
entry of alternative histogram in each bin, which is defined as
residual i = w · Nom i − Alt i
&
σ(w · Nom i)2 + σ(Alt i)2
, (10.12)
where Nom is a histogram of the nominal MC sample, Alt is a histogram of the alternative MC
sample, i represents the i th bin in the histogram, w is the event weight estimated in the reweighting
technique, and σ(X) is a standard deviation (statistical error) of entry from a histogram X. As the
criteria of good closure, the residual in all bins should be within ±3σ, since the deviation over 3σ
would be treated as a different shape from the original shape of alternative MC sample in the fit,
while the deviation within 1σ is the best closure.
The χ2 value for two histograms is defined as:
χ2 = 1
AN
%r
i=1
(Nai − Ani)
2
ai + ni
, (10.13)
where ni and ai are entries in the i-th bin of histograms of nominal and alternative MC samples,
respectively, N = +
i ni, and A = +
i ai. The χ2 value follows approximately the χ2
(r−1) distribution.
For two identical histograms, χ2 is zero. For two different histograms but having almost the same
distribution, χ2
(r−1)/(r − 1) is expected to be around 1.0, where (r − 1) is sometimes referred to as
the number of degrees of freedom (n.d.f.). The χ2/n.d.f. value is reported in the closure plots as
below. The χ2/n.d.f. value closer to ≈ 1.0 shows the better closure.
Closure of BDT input variables
To verify whether the derived event weight considers the shape variations of the BDT input
variables, the p.d.f. of all the BDT input variables are reweighted. Besides the mbb, the invariant
mass of two leptons m(( is one of the BDT input variables that have a large variation between the
nominal (Sherpa 2.2.11) and alternative (Powheg +Pythia 8) MC sample, as shown in the shape￾shift plot of Figure 10.3. Therefore, for here, the shape variation of m(( should be considered to
estimate the event weight w(x).
Figure 10.4 and 10.5 show the closure of mbb and m((, respectively, in comparison between the
case of applying mbb-based weight w(xmbb ) and applying CARL weight w∗(x). Comparing the case
of CARL weight from the case of mbb-based weight, the residuals of each bin get closer to 0σ,
and the χ2 score decreases toward 1 by ∼ 23% for mbb, and ∼ 47% for m((. With these results, the
shape variation of m(( is properly considered in the CARL weight w∗(x). For the mbb, the closure
is better in the CARL weight, which may be caused by the correlation of the BDT input variables.
Besides the closure of mbb and m((, the closures of other BDT input variables, except the pV
T shape

Uncertainty impact on the fit
As the results of the higher performance approximation of the shape of alternative MC sample
exploiting the CARL reweighting method, the shape uncertainty, which is equivalent to the shape￾shift, can be estimated with a more proper systematic shape in a smooth form. Figure 10.8 and
10.9 show the shape-shift of BDTVH and BDTVZ for the fit, respectively, in comparison between
the case of applying mbb-based weight w(xmbb ) and applying CARL weight w∗(x). By apply￾ing the CARL weight rather than the mbb-based weight, the shape-shift of the BDTVH decreases,
which would result in the decrease of impact of the shape uncertainty on the VH(H → bb¯) signal
strength measurement. In the case of the BDTVZ, the shape-shift of the BDTVZ increases, which
would result in the increase of impact of the shape uncertainty on the VZ(Z → bb¯) signal strength
measurement.
Here, to evaluate the uncertainty as discussed in Section 11.3, the signal strength measurements
for both VH(H → bb¯) and VZ(Z → bb¯) processes with the Asimov dataset are conducted in the
dedicated region: in the 2-lepton channel in resolved VH(bb¯) regime. The expected changes on the
overall diboson modelling uncertainty (and shape uncertainty against Powheg + Pythia 8) can be
observed in the breakdown of uncertainties in the signal strength measurements as shown in Table

=== PDF PAGE 163 ===

10.1, where the breakdown is derived in the same way as shown in Section 12.1.2. By applying the
CARL reweighting method, the overall diboson modelling uncertainty decreases by 1.96% in the
VH(H → bb¯) signal strength measurement, and it increases by 2.07% in the VZ(Z → bb¯) signal
strength measurement. These fit results are expected since the shape variation of BDTVH is shrunk
and the shape variation of BDTVZ is expanded by applying the CARL reweighting method rather
than applying the mbb-based reweighting method, and this is already known very well as discussed
above that the CARL reweighted shape approximates the shape alternative MC sample in higher
performance. As the effective performance of the CARL reweighting method is confirmed in the
estimation of shape uncertainty between the nominal and alternative MC samples, this method is
adopted in the final results as explained in Section 9.2.1 and Section 10.3.3.

Chapter 11
Statistical description of data
This chapter describes the usage of profile likelihood fit in this analysis in Section 11.1, the fit
model and method used in the fit in Section 11.2, and validation of the fit in Section 11.3.
11.1 General description of profile likelihood fit
In this analysis, a profile likelihood fit is exploited to explain the data with the simulated samples
considering statistical and systematic uncertainties, and to perform the background estimation and
the signal prediction. A likelihood function used in the profile likelihood fit is defined in Section
11.1.1. The evaluation of the statistical quantities from the best fit likelihood, such as derivation
of the significance over the background-only hypothesis, is discussed in Section 11.1.2.
11.1.1 Definition of likelihood function
The likelihood function consists of three kinds of terms.
As the first term, Poissonian probability is introduced to compare the data event yields and
expected event yields in all the bins.
L(0µ) =
*
i′bins
Pois(Ni|0µsi + bi) =
*
i′bins
(0µsi + bi)
Ni
Ni!
e−(0µsi+bi)
, (11.1)
where Ni is the number of measured data event yields in i
th bin, si is the signal event yields expected
by the MC simulation within the SM prediction, and bi is the background event yields expected by
the MC simulation and the data-driven method. For the signal prediction, a Parameter of Interest
(POI) is defined for different signal processes, where its configuration for this analysis is explained
in Section 11.2. µ is the signal strength, which is defined as Eq. (1.15), and one µ is measured in

=== PDF PAGE 166 ===
152 11.1 General description of profile likelihood fit
the corresponding POI. A set of all the POIs (0µ) is measured simultaneously in the fit.
In order to consider the systematic uncertainty in the fit, nuisance parameters (NP) are imple￾mented according to the systematic uncertainties given in Section 9.2. A set of NPs with their
predefined variations is denoted as 0θ. The predefined variation sizes are derived from the cal￾ibration processes for the experimental uncertainties, and the comparison between nominal and
alternative models of the MC simulations for the theoretical (modelling) uncertainties. These NPs
modify the expected signal and background event yields while the Poisson term (11.1) performs
the fit: {si, bi}→{si(0θ), bi(0θ)}. The NPs of the same systematic source are correlated across the re￾gions as many as possible to provide the effect of the same systematic source into the fit with fewer
parameters for the fit convergence. The NPs of all the experimental uncertainties are fully corre￾lated in all the analysis categories. On the other hand, the NPs of some theoretical uncertainties
are decorrelated between the categories. As the NPs are decorrelated in finer regions, the agree￾ment of the data and the MC simulations improves, since the modifications of the expected event
yields in the different fine regions can be performed. The de-correlation should be used as long as
the systematic effects are well understood. For example, when the effects of the NPs of the same
systematic source are known to be different in the different regions, these NPs are decorrelated
between certain regions. Moreover, when the source of systematic uncertainty is considered by
different ways in the different regimes or lepton channels, the NPs of that systematic uncertainty
are decorrelated between the regimes and lepton channels in the fit.
As the second term, Gaussian constraint term as Eq. (11.2) is introduced to express the variation
size of the NPs (0θ).
LConstraint =
*

. (11.2)
The nominal value of an NP (θ) is 0, and the predefined variation size of an NP corresponds to the
1 standard deviation (σθ) of Eq. (11.2). A NP can be more constrained during the fit due to some
correlations with other fit parameters and so on. This causes a decrease of the variation size of an
NP.
The normalisation factors (NFs) are also introduced as the fit parameter to estimate the floating
normalisation of the top and V+jets background across the SRs and CRs, The NFs are left uncon￾strained in the fit and are defined purely from data in the CRs. A set of NFs is denoted as 0τ, and
modifies the certain background event yields: {bi}→{bi(δτ)}. In order to help define the NFs(0τ)
from a CR, a fitting variable is introduced to get background pure bins in the histogram, which is
also discussed in Section 11.2. The pre-fit values of NFs(0τ) are set to 1.

=== PDF PAGE 167 ===
Chapter 11. Statistical description of data 153

yield of that bin: bi(0θ,0τ) → γibi(0θ,0τ).
As the last term, γ parameters are estimated with the data per bin
LBkgStat(0γ) =

The profile likelihood ratio can be defined to test a certain hypothesis with the fixed value µ≃*1

154 11.2 Analysis specific fit model and method
Here, the background-only hypothesis is considered, which means the case of µ≃ = 0 is tested.
The test statistic is defined as q0 in Eq. (11.7). In order to check the comparison between the
observed data and the background-only hypothesis, p-value can be used, which is defined as
p0 =
' ∞
q0,obs
f(q0|0)dq0 (11.8)
where f(q0|0) is the probability density function of q0 under the assumption of µ = 0 and q0,obs is
the value of q0 which is observed with the data.
With this p-value, significance Z can be defined by the number of Gaussian standard deviations
over the background-only hypothesis as
Z = Φ−1
(1 − p0), (11.9)
where Φ−1 is the inverse of Gaussian cumulative distribution function. The signal sensitivity of
each POI(µ) can be evaluated with this significance Z. If only the statistics is considered while
calculating the significance, and if Eq. (11.1) is substituted for Eq. (11.6), then the significance
Z is equivalent with the Asimov significance Eq. (8.6). The particle physics community tends to
reject the background-only hypothesis above Z = 5, and treats the signal process as ”discovery”.
11.2 Analysis specific fit model and method
While determining the best fit values of the fit parameters in the fit, the SRs and CRs are consid￾ered simultaneously, and the background estimation is performed, followed by the signal extrac￾tion for both the signal strength (inclusive cross-section) measurement and the fiducial differential
cross-section measurement. The difference between these two measurements is only the degree of
freedom of the POI in the signal extractions as shown in Figure 11.1. In this section, the specific
fit models for this analysis such as the POI, variables in CRs, and BDT optimal variable in SRs are
explained, followed by the fit methods such as pruning NPs, and unblinding sensitive data.
Parameter of Interest (POI)
In the signal strength measurement in the inclusive regimes,
• 2 POIs: µbb

and VH(H → cc¯) are separately measured. For the 4 POIs, the signal strengths are measured in

As discussed in Section 1.3.2 and Section 5.2.4, in the fiducial differential cross-section mea￾surement, the differential cross-section in each theoretically well-defined truth STXS bin is ex￾tracted in the fit. The truth STXS bin is defined by theoretically simulated truth variables accord￾ing to the STXS framework. In the VH measurement, pV
T
,truth, the number of truth ISR jets, and
WH, ZH production modes are used as the truth variables as shown in Figure 1.12. The simulated
signal events are classified with the truth variables before the fit. In the maximisation of the like￾lihood function, the POIs are assigned to all truth STXS bins. The µ of each POI is applied to the
simulated signal events in each truth STXS bin, then the fiducial differential cross-section in each
truth STXS bin can be measured*2. In this extraction, the normalisation uncertainty of the simu￾lated signal events is cancelled out, but the acceptance uncertainties due to the migration between
the reconstructed regions and the truth STXS bins need to be considered, which is discussed in
Section 9.2.2.
In this thesis, the following POIs are prepared for the fiducial differential cross-section measure￾ment.
• 10 POIs: µ of {WH, ZH ⊗ pV

,truth}
• 15 POIs: µ of {WH, ZH ⊗ pV
T
,truth⊗ (the number of truth ISR jets)}

Variables used in the fit
The profile likelihood fit handles 59 SRs (27 regions for the VH(bb¯) regimes, 32 regions for the
VH(cc¯) regimes), and 97 CRs, simultaneously. The BDT output distributions are used in the SRs
to discriminate the signal process and to increase the signal sensitivity. These BDT distributions
are optimised to get smooth background distributions and to have the highest signal sensitivity as
discussed below. As discussed in Section 9.2.3, the fit variables and their binning in the CRs are set

BDT output optimisation
The BDT output score is produced with a 500 bins of histogram. While merging and reducing
the bins for the fit, the binning is optimised to obtain a smooth distribution for the background
*3 Some bins of the fit variable distribution need to be purely dominated by a certain background so that the background
can be strongly constrained in these bins.
*4 The mj1, j2 is used in the High-∆R CR of the CTCL-tag and CTCT-tag regions in the VH(cc¯) regime, since Top(qq)

=== PDF PAGE 172 ===
158 11.2 Analysis specific fit model and method
processes in the low BDT score and to form fine binning for the signal process to increase the
significance. The optimisation of the BDT score is performed based on the following formula
(11.10),
Z = zs
ns
Ns
+ zb
nb
Nb
(11.10)
where the Ns and Nb are the total number of signal and background events, ns and nb are the number
of signal and background events in an arbitrary bin, and zs,zb are tunable parameters to configure
the number of signal-enriched bins and background-enriched bins after the optimisation. The bins
are merged from the highest score to the lowest score, until the ns and nb increase to satisfy Z > 1.
This scanning process forms one bin in the optimised histogram and the process restarts from the
next unmerged bin. zs, zb are tuned in different phase spaces as shown in Table 11.2 to make finer
bins in regions with higher statistics, and rough bins in regions with lower statistics. Total number
of bins after the optimisation varies from 3 to 15.

- -
In order to avoid making empty or very low statistic bins, at least 3 signal and background events
are required in an optimised bin. Also, the optimisation is designed to ensure that the statistical
uncertainty of the simulated background in each bin is less than 20 %.
Pruning the nuisance parameters
Several nuisance parameters have a negligible impact on the fit. In order to converge the fit with
less computation time, these parameters are pruned. The pruning criteria are as follows:
• Systematic uncertainties inducing a normalisation effect smaller than 0.5 % of the nominal
event yields.
• Shape uncertainties are neglected if none of the bins in the distribution has a deviation over
0.5 % of the nominal shape distribution after the overall normalisation.
• For very small background processes, both the shape and normalisation uncertainties are
neglected depending on whether it’s in the sensitive regions. In the sensitive regions, the
uncertainties are pruned only if these process event yields are less than 2 % of the signal
event yields, while, in non-sensitive regions, the uncertainties are pruned if they have an

=== PDF PAGE 173 ===
Chapter 11. Statistical description of data 159
impact lower than 0.5 % of the total background event yields.
After the pruning, the total number of NPs (0θ) is ∼ 560 in both VH fit and VZ fit.
Blinding the signals
In order not to be biased by the data when the analysis method or the systematic uncertainties
are implemented, the analysis is proceeded by blinding the signal information as follows.
• Data is not superimposed in a histogram bin if the expected simulated signal comprises
greater than 5 % of the total simulated signal and background event yields in the bin.
• Higgs (VH) signal strength is fixed to the SM prediction (µ=1) in the fit with data.
• Same treatments are done for the diboson (VZ) event, which is used as the signal in the
cross-check analysis.
Once, all the necessary systematic uncertainties are ready and the fit seems to be converged, first,
the VZ signal is unblinded. If no fit issue is found, the VH signal is unblinded.
11.3 Fit validation
Asimov datasets
Before performing the fit on the real dataset, it’s important to know how the numerous sys￾tematic uncertainties affect the POI especially when blinding the signals. To obtain such knowl￾edge, pseudo dataset can be useful, which corresponds to the sum of all simulated signal and
background samples, referred to as “Asimov dataset”. A fit, which measures the expected sig￾nal strength (µ = 1), can be performed with the Asimov dataset, referred to as “Expected fit”.
When the uncertainties of signal strength are evaluated in a performance study of new analysis
method, the expected fit has the advantage of knowing the pure impact of the uncertainties, since
the uncertainties are scaled by µ value.
Correlation matrix
Because the likelihood is a function of the multiple parameters, the fit parameters affect each
other in the multi-dimensional space, referred to as the correlation. The correlation of two arbitrary
parameters can be derived from the covariance matrix that is given by the inverse of the Hesse
matrix around the point of maximal likelihood [129]. When several fit parameters explain the data
in the same bin, this correlation can be used to comprehend the features of data.

=== PDF PAGE 174 ===
160 11.3 Fit validation
The Hesse matrix Hij can be written as
Hij = δ2(− ln(L))
δθiδθj
, (11.11)
where θ is a nuisance parameter in the fit, i, j is two arbitrary parameters, and L is the likelihood
Eq. (11.5).
The covariance matrix is given as
Covij = (H)
−1
ij . (11.12)
By normalising the covariance matrix, the correlation can be derived as
Corij = Covij
&
CoviiCovjj
. (11.13)
Similarly, the correlation of two POI (µi,µj) can be derived by replacing the θ and µ in the
equations above.
