Chapter 12
Results and discussion
This chapter describes the final results of the diboson cross-check measurement, the signal strength
µ measurement, and the fiducial differential cross-section measurement in Section 12.1. After that,
the evaluations of signal strength measurement results for the VH(H → bb¯) process are discussed
in Section 12.2.
12.1 Results
12.1.1 Diboson cross-check measurement: VZ(Z → bb¯)
A diboson cross-check fit is performed by using the VZ(Z → bb¯) process as the signal process,
which is discussed in Section 1.4.3.
For the VZ(Z → bb¯) inclusive process, the observed (expected) significance over the
background-only hypothesis is 13.8 (15.1) standard deviations. The measured signal strength is
µbb
VZ = 0.91+0.13
−0.11 = 0.91 ± 0.05 (stat.)+0.12
−0.10 (syst.). (12.1)
The total uncertainty of the signal strength improves ∼ 14 % from the previous VH(H → bb¯)
analysis [12].
For the WZ, Z → bb¯ and ZZ, Z → bb¯ processes, the significance are summarised in Table 12.1.
The measured signal strengths are
µbb

−0.18 (syst.), (12.2)
µbb

The BDTVZ post-fit distributions and the impact of the uncertainties on the µbb
VZ are shown in
Appendix I. The measurements of diboson processes are supposed to be consistent with the SM
prediction while validating this analysis. Hereby, this result proves that the methods of this analysis
can probe the signal process in SRs correctly.
Since this paper assesses the VH physics through the physics analysis, new BSM physics for the
diboson (VZ) process won’t be discussed in this paper. The discussion on new BSM physics for
the VZ process must be conducted in the VZ dedicated analysis [126], which has dedicated SRs
for the VZ process without any VH process contamination.
Since the diboson process is a minor background process in this analysis, it’s difficult to deter￾mine both the µbb
VZ and µbb
VH in the fit. When µVH is set to the SM(1) in this VZ signal strength
measurement, the observed VZ signal strength is consistent with the SM prediction. Therefore,
the µVZ is fixed to the SM in the VH signal strength measurement in this analysis.
12.1.2 Signal strength measurement: VH(H → bb¯)
The signal strength in the inclusive VH(H → bb¯) process is measured in the fit with 2 POIs, and
the signal strengths of the separate WH(H → bb¯) and ZH(H → bb¯) processes are measured in the
fit with 4 POIs.
For the VH, H → bb¯ inclusive process, the observed (expected) significance over the
background-only hypothesis is 7.4 (8.0) standard deviations. The measured signal strength for the
VH, H → bb¯ signal is
µbb
VH = 0.92+0.16
−0.15 = 0.92 ± 0.10 (stat.)+0.13
−0.11 (syst.). (12.4)
According to the measurement of signal strength, the observed inclusive VH cross-section times
V → leptons and H → bb¯ branching ratio is 170.1 +29.6
−25.9 [fb] within the analysis phase space. The
precision of this measurement is the best in the world as of 2025, and improves ∼ 14 % from the

previous VH(H → bb¯) analysis [12]. This result shows good agreement with the SM prediction
within its uncertainty. The significant deviation from the SM is not observed and the impact of the
BSM physics, introduced in Section 1.1.3, cannot be confirmed from this result.
For the WH, H → bb¯ and ZH, H → bb¯ processes, the significance are summarised in Table
12.2. As discussed in Section 11.1.2, the 5 σ of observed significance is the important criterion
to evaluate the discovery of a certain physics process in particle physics. The observation over 5
σ is achieved in the WH, H → bb¯ process for the first time in the VH(H → bb¯) analysis*1. The
measured signal strengths are
µbb

−0.14 (syst.). (12.6)
The both measurements of signal strengths show good agreement with the SM prediction. The
improvement of total uncertainties of both signal strengths is observed by 23 % and 10 % for the
WH and ZH processes, respectively, compared with the previous VH(H → bb¯) analysis [12].
In this analysis, the signal strength of the VH(H → cc¯) process is also measured simultaneously.
As the results, no signal event is observed (µ = 0 consistent) within its uncertainty, but an upper
limit of 11.5 times the SM predictions at 95 % CL (Confidence Level) is observed, whose sensi￾tivity improved by approximately a factor of three rather than the previous VH(H → cc¯) analysis
[31] (See Ref. [105]).

a value reported in the legend for better visualisation. The range of the combined statistical and
systematic uncertainties for the sum events of the fitted signal and background (S+B) are indicated
by the hatched band at the top of the filled histograms. The ratios of the data event yields over the
sum of the fitted signal and background event yields are shown in the lower panel. The plots are
also published in Ref. [105].

12.1.3 Fiducial differential cross-section measurement: VH production in STXS
The fiducial differential cross-sections of the separated WH and ZH production modes as func￾tions of pV
T
,truth and the number of “truth” ISR jets (Ntruth
ISRjet) are measured for the first time in the fit
with 15 POIs. Thanks to the available statistics and the smaller systematic uncertainties, a further
split into Ntruth
ISRjet = 0 and Ntruth
ISRjet ≥ 1 can be performed in the measurement in 75 < pZ
T
,truth
< 400
GeV of the ZH production mode and in 150 < pW
T
,truth
< 400 GeV of the WH production mode.
Figure 12.3 shows the results of these new measurements. In the upper panel, the observed value
of VH cross-section times the H → bb¯ and V →leptons branching ratio with its total uncertainty
and statistical uncertainty is shown with the SM prediction value with its theoretical uncertainty.
In the lower panel, the ratio of the measured value to the SM prediction is shown with its relative
uncertainty. In all STXS bins, the data statistical uncertainty is the dominant uncertainty, which
also considers the impacts from the normalisation factors on the major background processes, ex￾cept for the 75 < pW
T
,truth
< 150 GeV bin where the W+jets modelling uncertainty is dominant.
The results of cross-section measurements are consistent with the SM prediction within one stan￾dard deviation, except in the 400 < pW
T
,truth
< 600 GeV and pZ
T > 600 GeV STXS bins where the
observed values are within two standard deviations. Over-estimation of the background processes
causes the observation of µ ≤ 0 in some STXS bins. By discussing the physics results, the µ = 0
consistent means there is no signal event in certain STXS bins, so the upper limit is supposed to
be presented in the plot. However, because the inclusive cross-section measurement observed the
significant signal events, and because the VH(H → bb¯) analysis conventionally shows the post-fit
POI values after splitting the phase space, this paper presents µ values in the different STXS bins in
the plots as the result of statistical processing in the fit. As the conclusion, the significant deviation
from the SM is not observed in this result.
The fiducial differential cross-sections of the separated WH and ZH production modes as a
function of pV
T
,truth are also measured to evaluate the improvement from the previous analysis [12]
in the fit with 10 POIs. The results are shown in Figure 12.4. Compared with Figure 1.13, the
75 < pW
T
,truth
< 150 GeV bin and new split at pV
T
,truth = 400 GeV are added, and the relative
uncertainties in the same STXS bins are decreased by ∼ 10 %. The major improvement is caused
by the additional treatments of event categorisations, which are discussed in Section 5.2.4. The
new split in the boosted VH(bb¯) regime is realised because the 50 % improvement of the expected
significance is confirmed by introducing the BDT score as the final discriminant variable in the fit
and the improved b-tagging algorithms. Over-estimation of the background processes causes the
observation of µ ≤ 0 in some STXS bins such as the high pV
T bins, but still the plot only shows the
µ value results and doesn’t discuss the upper limits for the µ = 0 consistent result.

12.2 Discussion
Especially, the evaluations of the signal strength measurement results in Section 12.1.2 are dis￾cussed in this section.
12.2.1 Impacts of the uncertainties on the µbb
VH
The contributions of the different sets of uncertainties to the signal strengths for the
VH(H → bb¯), WH, H → bb¯, and ZH, H → bb¯ processes are shown in the breakdown Table
12.4. A contribution of a set of systematic uncertainties (Nuisance Parameter in the fit, NPs) to
the total uncertainty of the signal strength is defined as the difference in quadrature between the
total uncertainty and the uncertainty derived from a fit where the relevant NPs are fixed at the
best fit values. The contribution of the statistical uncertainties is defined as the uncertainty of a
fit with all NPs fixed at the best fit values, except for the Normalisation Factors (NFs). The total
systematic uncertainty is calculated as the difference in quadrature between the statistical and the
total uncertainty. The sum in quadrature of all the different sets of uncertainties in the breakdown
differs from the total uncertainty due to correlations between the NPs. The background modelling,
especially V+jets processes, and signal modelling provide the largest contribution to the total
systematic uncertainty, besides the experimental uncertainties of jet and flavour tagging are
sub-dominant in the total systematic uncertainty. In the signal strength measurements of WH and
ZH production modes, the size of statistical and systematic uncertainties are similar. Comparing
between the uncertainties of signal strength in WH and ZH production modes, the dedicated
uncertainties differ according to the topologies of their production modes: For example, the signal
strength of the ZH production mode has larger Emiss
T experimental uncertainty because of handing
Z → νν topology, and has a larger signal modelling uncertainty because of the limited precision
of the theoretical computation of the gluon-initiated processes, for which the lowest order is a box
or triangle diagram as shown in Figure 1.7. Whereas, the signal strength of the WH production
mode has larger modelling uncertainties of top quark backgrounds since they contribute most to
the background in the 1-lepton channel.

12.2.2 Estimation results of major backgrounds
The normalisation factors (NFs) after the fit for the measurement of VH(H → bb¯) process are
summarised in Table 12.5 for the W+jets processes, Table 12.6 for the Z+jets processes, and Table
12.7 for the top quark processes, where the floating normalisations are applied. A similar behaviour
is observed between the W+jets and Z+jets: the normalisation factors increase with the number
of jets and with pV
T. These trends are consistent with a measurement of the Z + hf [130], where
hf(heavy flavour) is denoted as the truth flavour composition of bb and cc. The normalisation
factors of the top quark background processes decrease with pV
T since the NLO Powheg +Pythia 8
generators simulates the cross-section overhead the data as pV
T increases. This trend is consistent
with a measurement of the top quark transverse momentum in tt
¯ events [131].

12.2.3 Effects of improved analysis technique on the uncertainties of µbb
VH
The impact of the improved analysis technique on the uncertainties of signal strength measure￾ment is also evaluated by comparing the fits between configurations of this analysis and the previ￾ous analysis. As discussed in Section 11.3, to evaluate the uncertainties without scaled by µ value,
the contributions of NPs are evaluated in the expected fit with the Asimov dataset (µ = 1). Here,
the signal strength of the resolved VH(bb¯) regime (µbb
VH resolved) is studied. The contributions of
the sets of NPs are compared in the following three cases: (a) the fit of previous analysis in the
resolved VH(bb¯) regime [28], (b) the independent fit of this analysis in only the resolved VH(bb¯)
regime, and (c) the simultaneous fit of this analysis in all three regimes but the “µbb
VH resolved”
POI is targeted in the fit of 3 POIs as defined in Section 11.2. The breakdown for the contributions
of the sets of NPs, NFs, and other µs are shown in Table 12.8. The difference of the analysis
methods between the previous and this analysis can be evaluated by comparing between (a) and
(b). Since the events in 75 < pV
T < 150 GeV in the 1-lepton channel are added from this analysis,
the statistical uncertainty decreases by ∼ 17 %. Thanks to the improvement of jet flavour tagging
performance, the b-tagging experimental uncertainty decreases by ∼ 48 %. While the uncertainty
from the simulated sample size decreases by ∼ 21 % due to the updates of MC simulation of
V+jets, the modelling uncertainties of V+jets and signal processes increase by a factor of 2 after
re-estimation in the new phase space in this analysis. The effects of performing the fit in all three
regimes simultaneously can be evaluated by comparing between (b) and (c). Because the major
backgrounds with mixing flavour jet events and light flavours jet events can be constrained from
the dedicated CRs in the VH(cc¯) regime, and the W+hf events can be constrained from the Low-
∆R CR exploiting the BDTCRLow, the uncertainty of background floating normalisations decreases
by ∼ 14 %. Moreover, the modelling uncertainties of V+jets, top quark backgrounds decrease. The
modelling uncertainty of diboson background doesn’t decrease because it doesn’t have a dedicated
CR. Since the strong correlation exists between the jet energy scale uncertainty related to flavour
composition and the floating normalisations of major backgrounds, the experimental uncertainty
of the jet decreases by ∼ 24 %.

Chapter 13
Conclusion
This study has a huge interest in the Higgs boson production associated with a Z/W boson (VH
production), since the differential cross-section of the VH production has high sensitivity for new
physics beyond the SM (BSM) in high momentum of a vector boson. In order to obtain enough
statistics of the VH production, a decay mode of a pair of two bottom quarks with a branching
fraction of 58 % is chosen. The coupling between the Higgs boson and a bottom quark also has
high sensitivity for BSM. The significant deviation from the SM prediction was not observed in
the previous VH(H → bb¯) analysis within its uncertainty. Meanwhile, it’s still possible to im￾prove its precision, since the major sources of its large uncertainty are experimental and modelling
variations rather than its statistics.
This thesis presents the improved precise measurement of the VH(H → bb¯) process using the
LHC proton-proton collisions data collected in the ATLAS experiment in 2015-2018. The centre
of mass energy is 13 TeV, and the integrated luminosity is 140 fb−1. The VH(H → bb¯) and
VH(H → cc¯) signal processes are analysed simultaneously in order to constrain their common
background. The FSR recovery is introduced to reduce statistical uncertainties of the differential
cross-section measurement for the first time; when splitting the region of differential cross-section
measurement by the number of additional jets, the statistical uncertainties decrease up to ∼ 8 %.
All the experimental and modelling uncertainties are upgraded compared to the previous analysis
[28, 12]. In terms of the estimation of shape uncertainty of the fit variables, the CARL method is
first adopted in this analysis; the impact of diboson modelling uncertainty on the signal strength
measurement of VH(H → bb¯) process is reduced by ∼ 2 %.
The measurements of the diboson process VZ, Z → bb¯ are performed as a validation of the
analysis methodology and of background estimations. The signal strength of VZ, V → bb¯ process
is µbb
VZ = 0.91+0.13
−0.11 = 0.91 ± 0.05(stat.)
+0.12
−0.10(syst.) The WZ, Z → bb¯ process is measured with an

=== PDF PAGE 192 ===
178
observed significance of 6.4 standard deviations, while the ZZ, Z → bb¯ process is measured with
an observed significance of more than 10 standard deviations. This measurement is consistent with
the SM predictions within their uncertainties.
The signal strength of VH(H → bb¯) process is µbb
VH = 0.92+0.16
−0.15 = 0.91 ± 0.10(stat.)
+0.13
−0.11(syst.),
which is the most precise result in the world. The precision improved by ∼ 14 % from the pre￾vious analysis [12]. The measurement of the VH(H → bb¯) process has an observed (expected)
significance of 7.4(8.0) standard deviations. The significant impact of the BSM physics cannot be
confirmed from this result. The individual production of WH with H → bb¯ is established with an
observed (expected) significance of 5.3(5.5) standard deviations, which is the first observation of
this process. This measurement is consistent with the SM predictions within their uncertainties.
The finest differential cross-section measurement of the VH production as functions of Z/W
boson transverse momentum and the number of additional jets, which is not induced from the
Higgs boson, is first performed in the world. The division of the phase space into the number of
jets reduces a theoretical uncertainty caused by QCD scale variations. The uncertainties vary from
50 % in the sensitive regions to 400 % in the lowest and the highest Z/W boson momentum regions.
The differential cross-section measurement as a function of a Z/W boson transverse momentum is
also performed, where the uncertainties decrease ∼ 10% from the previous analysis [28, 12]. All
measurements are consistent with the SM predictions within their uncertainties. The significant
deviation from the SM is not observed in these results.
In the future prospects, this VH(bb¯) analysis can probe the properties of the VH(H → bb¯) pro￾cess in higher precision with more statistics of the ATLAS data. The LHC-ATLAS experiment
plans to collect ∼ 3000fb−1 at →s = 14 TeV in the HL-LHC project as described in Section
2.1. The measurement results of the VH(H → bb¯) process are extrapolated with the ∼ 3000fb−1
dataset with the same analysis method of the previous analysis [12] as described in Appendix J.
This extrapolation shows that the dominant uncertainty of expected signal strength is still from the
systematic uncertainty, while the statistical uncertainty generally is improved ∼ 50%, and the total
uncertainty of expected signal strength is ∼ 6%. Therefore, the analysis techniques improved in
this thesis can help to suppress the systematic uncertainty, which may result in the observation of
significant deviation from the SM prediction in the Higgs property measurements.

Appendix

=== PDF PAGE 194 ===
180
Appendix A
Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics
system
This chapter discusses the development and study of control system of the TGC front-end electron￾ics system for the HL-LHC. The overall TGC electronics system for the HL-LHC is summarised
in Section A.1. The functionality of hit-signal processor in the front-end (PS Board) is described
in Section A.2. To configure, control, and monitor the front-end modules, especially the PS Board,
a hub module (JATHub) is developed. This is described in Section A.3. A control system driven
by the JATHub is studied in their combined test bench, and is described in Section A.4.
A.1 Overall TGC electronics system for HL-LHC
As described in Section 2.1, the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) accelerator at CERN will be
upgraded to increase its peak instantaneous luminosity up to 7.5 × 1034 cm−2s−1, referred to as
“High-Luminosity LHC(HL-LHC)”, from 2026 [33]. Due to this upgrade, the number of pileup
events per bunch crossing would reach to ∼200. To cope with the high luminosity environment
and collect collision events to search for physics of interest efficiently, the ATLAS experiment is
going to upgrade the ATLAS detector and the trigger data acquisition (DAQ) system, denoted as
the Phase-2 upgrade (2026 - 2030) [132].
The Thin Gap Chamber (TGC) of the ATLAS detector is responsible for the trigger on muons in
the end-cap region as shown in Figure A.1 (a), at the hardware-based trigger stage as described in
Section 2.2.3. In the TGC sub-detector system, the front-end and back-end electronics will be up￾graded, as illustrated in Figure A.1 (b), to send and receive binary hit-map at every bunch crossing
(BC) to the back-end system. The upgraded TGC system consists of the primary processing boards

=== PDF PAGE 195 ===
Chapter A. Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics system 181
(PS Board), hub modules for slow control (JTAG Assistance Hub (JATHub)) in the front-end, and
Sector Logic boards (SL) in the back-end. Here, the PS Board will be placed on the TGC detector
and the JATHub be placed in the blue VME crate on the rim of the TGC detector. The front-end
electronics will be implemented in the ATLAS cavern, and the back-end modules will be placed in
the counting room, 60 - 100 m apart from the ATLAS cavern. Besides the digital components, the
analogue Amplifier-Shaper-Discriminator (ASD) chips are not replaced from the current system
and are mounted on the TGC detectors to send the hit signal. The current TGC detectors will be
kept for the HL-LHC.
(a) TGC front-end (b) TGC electronics system
Fig. A.1: The ATLAS and TGC detector. (a) shows the TGC detector and the placement of TGC
front-end electronics. (b) illustrates the overall TGC electronics system with the front-end and
back-end electronics.
A.2 Functionality of the PS Board
The PS Board is equipped with eight Patch Panel (PP) ASICs and one Xilinx Kintex-7 FPGA,
as shown in Figure A.2 (a).
The PP ASICs receive hit signals from the ASD and adjust the signal timing to align the signal￾timing difference due to time of flight and length of signal cables of each channel, using variable
delays available at the beginning firmware logic in the PP ASIC. The PP ASICs also perform hit
BC identification (BCID) with a configurable gate-width. The variable delay and configurable
gate-width unit are controlled by a Phase Locked Loop (PLL)-based circuit exploiting the LHC

=== PDF PAGE 196 ===
182 A.3 Development of the JATHub
clock synchronised to the BC. Then, the binary hit-map is sent to the FPGA at every BC. The
FPGA receives hit signals from the PP ASICs and transfers the binary hit-map at every BC to the
SL via optical links, running at 8 Gbps without data reduction. The use of high-speed optical links
successfully simplifies the system design and improves the flexibility of the trigger algorithm in
the SL.
The FPGA receives the 40 MHz LHC clock distributed from the SL with optical links with fixed
latency and adjusts the clock phase using the built-in PLL of the FPGA clocking resource within
O(100) ps precision. An on-board clock jitter attenuator distributes the LHC clock to the PP ASICs
and provides a system clock for the FPGA operation and a reference clock to the optical serial link
transmitters of the FPGA.
(a) PS Board (b) JATHub
Fig. A.2: The prototype modules of TGC front-end electronics for phase-2 upgrade. These mod￾ules are developed exploiting the intellectual property of Open-It. The plots are also published in
Ref. [133].
A.3 Development of the JATHub
The JATHub is a control hub module for the FPGAs on the PS Boards. The JATHub is equipped
with the Xilinx Zynq-7000 System-on-a-Chip (SoC) device. The Zynq SoC integrates an ARM￾based processor with Kintex-7 FPGA and serves as the main driver of the control system. The
JATHub is a 9U-sized VME module.
The JATHub can control up to 11 PS Board modules. The JATHub has an Ethernet interface
via optical fibres (1000base-X), exploiting built-in transceivers in the Programmable Logic (PL)
part of the Zynq. The processor system (PS) of the Zynq runs applications to control the JTAG

=== PDF PAGE 197 ===
Chapter A. Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics system 183
signals for the PS Board FPGAs programming and debugging. Therefore, the connected PS Board
FPGAs can be remotely programmed over the Ethernet connection. Moreover, the JATHubs assist
the fine-tuning of the front-end LHC clock phase.
The JATHub also takes a role in recovering PS Board FPGAs for unrecoverable SEU errors
in the configuration memory during physics data taking. The PS Board FPGAs run the Xilinx
Soft Error Mitigation (SEM) controllers [134] to monitor, detect, and automatically recover the
SEU errors. The FPGAs will send a recovery request signal when a SEM controller observes
unrecoverable SEU error. Then, the JATHub will start the entire reprogramming process based on
flash memory on the PS Board. The recovery functionality is independent of the PS to run stably
regardless of possible PS CPU crashes during the data taking. A similar mechanism is used to
recover the PL of the Zynq devices of the JATHubs, exploiting connections between JATHubs.
For radiation damages on the storage device used for booting, the JATHub system introduces
redundancy in the boot procedure. One quad SPI flash memory (QSPI) and two SD cards can store
the boot images on a JATHub as shown in Figure A.2 (b). The QSPI is used as the primary boot
device of the Zynq-7000 SoC, and the SD cards is the secondary persistent storage to store the
PL bit-stream and kernel image. Redundancy is introduced by having boot images duplicated in
these devices. A procedure to switch the choice of files for both primary and secondary boot files
remotely is developed and tested. The QSPI is also accessible directly from outside via VME path
to assure its redundancy.
According to the design of the control system, a prototype mode of the JATHub was fabricated.
Major functionalities for the designed control system driven by the JATHubs have been developed
and tested with the JATHubs (see Figure A.2 (b)), and integration tests with the PS Board are held
in a combined system, as discussed in Section A.4. The mass production model of the JATHub is
fabricated after the testings of the designed control system.
A.4 Combined test and system-level study
The prototype modules of the PS Board and JATHub are intensively tested at the broad level and
then integrated into a test bench system for the system-level commissioning at KEK, as shown in
Figure A.3. Sector Logic (SL) module which is used in the Run 3 of LHC-ATLAS sufficiently em￾ulates the back-end SL for HL-LHC in this size of the test bench. The optical links are connected
to emulate the connections between the back-end and front-end systems for hit bit-map readout,
register control, and timing signal distribution. The test bench consists of two prototype PS Boards
and one prototype JATHub. 15 m or 30 m category 6 cables connect the PS Boards and JATHubs
to demonstrate the developed functionality in the equivalent hardware setup as the ATLAS cavern.

=== PDF PAGE 198 ===
184 A.4 Combined test and system-level study
Fig. A.3: The test bench of the TGC electronics system for Phase-2 upgrade.
For the JTAG access from the JATHub to the FPGAs on the PS-Boards, applications and
firmware for the Xilinx Virtual Cable (XVC) [135] and Serial Vector Format (SVF) player
[136] have been developed. The XVC application allows us to use the Xilinx GUI interface for
programming, debugging, e.g. Xilinx Integrated Logic Analyzer [137], and testing, e.g. Xilinx
Integrated Bit Error Ratio Tester [138], the remote FPGAs via Ethernet. The SVF player realises
fast programming using bit-streams stored in the SD card of the JATHub. The clock frequency
in the JTAG signals has been optimised for the length of the category 6 cables, and the JTAG
successfully runs at 1.25 MHz, as expected in this setup.
The recovery procedure for an unrecoverable SEU error case is demonstrated. The JATHub
successfully initiates the reprogramming using the onboard flash memory of the PS Board on
the request. In addition, based on the experience during the demonstration on the test bench,
the stability in the signal lines to initiate reprogramming have been improved. Any unexpected
short asserted signals (∼80 ms) in the signal line will be discarded on the PS Board owing to an
additional debouncer device (see Figure A.4).
The clock distributions to the PS Boards with a fixed latency via serial links between the PS
Board and the SL have been successfully implemented and demonstrated. A variation of latency of
less than 20 ps is observed for power cycling and resynchronisation procedure of the optical links.
Phase monitoring and adjustment functions have also been implemented. The measurements of
the LHC clocks on the JATHub are successfully performed in accuracy of 1/56 ns, and the results
are retrieved by software running on the Zynq PS. Functionality to control the clock phase on the
PS Board FPGAs successfully runs with a delay step of 1/56 ns. The adjustment of clock signals
distributed to the two prototype PS Boards with different fiber length has been demonstrated. The

=== PDF PAGE 199 ===
Chapter A. Phase2 upgrade of TGC electronics system 185
Fig. A.4: The yellow input signal shows a short asserted signal (∼10 ns) from the JATHub, and the
blue output signal shows that the debouncer discards the asserted signal.
timing difference of the clocks is observed as expected in the machinery, and the clock phase is
correctly adjusted.

Appendix C
SMEFT interpretation
In the previous VH(H → bb¯) analysis [12], from the results of fiducial differential cross-section
measurement, the SMEFT interpretation, which is introduced in Section 1.1.4, is performed and
the SMEFT parameters are derived by exploiting the SMEFTsim package [8]. The same LHC￾ATLAS Run2 full dataset as this thesis is used in the previous analysis.
In this analysis, only D = 6 dimension is considered, since D = 5 and D = 7 dimension
operators violate lepton or baryon number conservation, and D ≥ 8 dimensions are negligible
which are suppressed by the energy scale 1/ΛD−4. The Wilson coefficients and corresponding
SMEFT operators, which is significantly sensitive in this analysis, are shown in Table C.1.A
differential fiducial cross-section measurement in this analysis constrains the Wilson coefficients
in the ’Warsaw’ formulation [146].
The differential cross-section of VH production is sensitive to following three operators [10,
11, 9]. Q(3)
Hq has a strong impact for both the ZH and WH cross-section depending on momentum
of Z/W boson, while QHu has a similar impact for only the ZH cross-section. QHW works for an
overall modification of the VH cross-section.
The interference terms between the SM and BSM contributions are proportional to the coef￾ficients (linear parameterisation), corresponding to O(1/Λ2), while BSM contributes are propor￾tional to the square of coefficients (quadratic parameterisation), corresponding to O(1/Λ4). Since
the linear D = 8 terms cannot be included in the interpretation for now, results for both the linear
and linear plus quadratic D = 6 terms are studied to indicate possible effects from the linear D = 8
terms.
Maximum-likelihood fits between the experimental data and simulated samples are performed
across the STXS regions to constrain the limits of the Wilson coefficients. The results of the
constraints of the Wilson coefficients c
(3)
Hq, cHu, cHW are shown in Figure C.1 as a function of their

In an inclusive number of ISR jets bin (a pV
T bin), the gg → ZH process (Figure 1.7) doesn’t
contribute much, thus the gg initiated ZH process is fixed to the SM prediction in this result.
However, differential cross-section of the gg → ZH production should be measured independently
in future VH(H → bb¯) analysis. Modification of the gg → ZH production cross-section can be
derived from either higher-dimension (D ≥ 8) operators or QCD theoretical correction in NNLO.
Since the effect of linear D = 8 terms is confirmed in quadratic terms, which appears in the negative
values of the Wilson coefficients in Figure C.1 (b) (c), it’s worth separating the events of qq → ZH
and gg → ZH production as much as possible and to look the effect of quadratic D = 6 terms. In
the number of ISR jets ≥ 1 bin, more contribution of the gg → ZH process is expected than that
in the number of ISR jets = 0 bin. In terms of more sensitivity on the gg → ZH process in the
multiple jet bin, splitting the STXS bin with the number of ISR jets is motivated.

Appendix D
Overlap between boosted VH, H →
bb¯ and VH, H → cc¯ regimes
The resolved VH(bb¯) and boosted VH(bb¯) regimes are orthogonal due to the pV
T cut at 400 GeV:
events with pV
T < 400 GeV are handedl in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime, while events with higher
pV
T are handled in the boosted regime. From the description in Section 5.2, it is also clear that
all the regions in the resolved VH(bb¯) and VH(cc¯) regimes are designed carefully to avoid double
counting an event in two different regions. However, since the VH(cc¯) regime is currently ex￾ploiting all events without any upper limit of pV
T, the events that pass the VH(cc¯) selection with
pV
T > 400 GeV can also pass the boosted VH(bb¯) selection, which result in an overlap between
these two regimes. This is because a loose b-tagging requirement is used in the boosted VH(bb¯)
selection (85 %).
The overlap study shows that in the data, around 99.6 % of VH(cc¯) SR events do not overlap
with boosted VH(bb¯) SRs and 95.7 % of boosted VH(bb¯) events are not contained in VH(cc¯) SRs.
Since this overlap is not significant, no special treatment is needed for it.

Appendix G
Additional materials of the FSR re￾covery
G.1 Truth study
In order to validate the FSR properties, truth study is conducted with generator truth samples
of qq-initiated ZH, Z → ((, H → bb¯ signal process. The validation with truth samples is done in
the resolved VH(bb¯) regime in the 2-lepton channel. MC weight is only applied for whole event
weight. Normalization with cross-section is not applied.
Truth dRB1J3+dRB2J3 vs pV
T plots are present in Figure G.1, truth fraction of FSR events in
each number of jet region is present in Figure G.2, and truth mbb j plots with truth FSR, ISR events
are present in Figure G.3.
Truth dR(B1J3)+dR(B2J3) vs pV
T plots for the tt
¯ events are also present in Figure G.4. The
3rd gluon-induced jets tend to have the same kinematic feature as the VH events in the relative
position against two b-jets. These events are concentrating in the low pV
T region. The 3rd jet in the
low dR(B1J3)+dR(B2J3) distributes nearly or between the two b-jets, and it is coming from the
hadronic decaying W or between two top quarks with small close angle.

200 G.2 Effective order of correction for the FSR recovery
G.2 Effective order of correction for the FSR recovery
The timing to apply FSR recovery during the jet correction in Section 6.1 is studied. Perfor￾mance of mBB resolution was compared in the following three cases.
A Applying FSR recovery after the muon-in-jet correction, before the PtReco, KF
B Applying FSR recovery after the muon-in-jet correction, the PtReco and before the KF
C Applying FSR recovery after all the correction
The comparisons of mass resolution between the case A and case B is shown in Figure G.5. The
comparisons of mass resolution between the case B and case C is shown in Figure G.6.

Fig. G.5: FSR recovered mBB comparison plots. Upper: OneMu + FSR + PtReco, Lower: OneMu
+ PtReco + FSR.
.
There is no big difference in performance as shown in Figure G.5 and Figure G.6. Therefore,
FSR recovery is applied mainly after the PtReco in VHbb resolved, or after muon-in-jet correction
in VHcc
However, when the KF applied, FSR recovery is applied after KF due to KF’s theoritical reason;
Its transfer function in the KF likelihood is defined with all reconstructed jets (flavour-jets, FSR
jets, ISR jets), so FSR jets should be separately treated from the b-jets.

Fig. G.7: Comparison of Higgs mass resolution (mbb) before and after the FSR recovery. The
Higgs mass distributions in all three pV
T regions in the 2-lepton channel in the resolved VH(bb¯)
regime are made with the signal nominal MC sample (ZH(Z → ((, H → bb¯) process). The jet
mass corrections of the Muon-in-jet and PtReco are already applied. Plot in first column shows
the mass distribution of events with only an FSR identified jet in the 3-jet region before the event
migration, where these events are migrated into the 2-jet region. For them, a blue line shows
invariant mass of two b-jets (mbb), and a red line shows invariant mass of two b-jets and an FSR
identified jet (mbb j(FSR)). The red distribution in second column is added to the mbb distribution in
the 2-jet region (a). Plot in second column shows the comparison in the 2-jet regions, and plot in
third column shows the comparison in the 3-jet region. For plots in the both column, a black line
denotes the mass distribution before the FSR recovery, and a red line denotes the mass distribution
after the FSR recovery.

Understanding of the significance results and feasibility study to improve the significance
Table G.1: Comparison result of the significance (S ) before and after the FSR recovery. The
expected significance are measured in all different lepton channels in the inclusive region in the
resolved VH(bb¯) regime. The expected significance in the different number of jet (Njet) regions
in inclusive pV
T region are also listed. For the 0-lepton channel, the significance in 2-jet and 3-jet
regions are shown, and for the 2-lepton channel, the significance in 2-jet and > 3-jet regions are
shown. Both mbb and BDTVH based expected significance are derived with Eq. (7.2). The relative
changes (Diff. %) are also derived.

To understand the results of significance, checking the distribution changes of auxiliary variables
for the BDT training and the BDT input variables is valid. Figure 7.8 shows the changes of event
yields in different nJet regions by the FSR recovery for the VH signal, tt
¯, and W+jets processes
in the 1-lepton channel. As described in Section 8.3, the information of nJet region is used in the
BDTVH training. Figure 7.9 shows the changes of the distributions for the important BDT input
variables in the 1-lepton channel (mbb, mbb j) for the VH signal, tt
¯, and W+jets processes.
As described above, the events in peak around 125 GeV of mbb j distribution of the VH signal
process in 3-jet region are identified and migrated to the mbb distribution in 2-jet region, as shown
in Figure 7.9 (a) and (d). Since the peak around 125 GeV of mbb j is one of the important feature to
discriminating the signal and background processes, the mbb j was ranked to the 2nd highest in an
importance list of the 16 BDT input variables in the BDT training of 3-jet region in the 1-lepton
channel, where the highest ranked variable is always the mbb. In the BDT training after the FSR
recovery, mbb j is ranked down to the 4th highest in the importance list, because a small peak is left
around 125 GeV as shown in Figure 7.9 (d), which still helps the discrimination.
To increase the significance, the FSR recovery should not collect the tt
¯background events. Thus,

=== PDF PAGE 223 ===
Chapter G. Additional materials of the FSR recovery 209
some optimisations are demanded in the FSR identification in order to collect only the VH signal
events with FSR jets. There are three suggestions for the optimisations. The second and third
suggestions are already studied as described below. Firstly, it should be reasonable to apply the
FSR recovery only in 2-jet and 3-jet events in the 1-lepton channel. This would provide large
impact on the significance in the 3-jet region, since most of the events in the 4-jet region migrate
to the 3-jet region. Secondly, an alternative scheme to apply the FSR recovery toward only the
additional jet in the phet
T > 30 GeV can reduce the impact on the significance, while it doesn’t affect
on the fiducial differential cross-section measurement. Since almost half of the FSR-identified jet
of the VH signal process distributes in the 20 < p
jet
T < 30 GeV and the additional jets of the tt
¯
process distributes more in the low p
jet
T region, the alternative scheme may reduce the decrease of
significance. As the results, by applying the FSR recovery with the alternative scheme, the BDTVH
significance in the 1-lepton channel decreases by ∼ 1 %, which is not applicable enough to adopt
the FSR recovery in the 1-lepton channel. Finally, it seems to be good to add some discriminant
kinematic variable in the cut function, since the tt
¯ with multi-jet events have the same kinematic
feature as the VH events for the relative position of the 3rd gluon-induced jet against the two b￾jets, as shown in Figure G.4. The most powerful kinematic variable to distinguish the VH signal
and tt
¯ processes is the invariant mass of top quark (mtop), which is defined in Section 8.2. Figure
G.13 shows distributions of mtop by comparing these distributions between the VH signal and tt
¯
background processes in 3-jet events with only an FSR identified jet in the 1-lepton channel. The
distributions are normalised to their event yields in the different analysis regions. The difference
of distributions is significant in the high pV
T region as shown in Figure G.13 (c), but otherwise the
mtop is not discriminant between the VH and tt
¯ events. Since the FSR tends to be radiated in the
low pV
T region as shown in Figure 7.4, it cannot be expected to improve the significance by simply
adding the mtop into the FSR cut function.

Fig. G.14: Comparison of the result of fiducial differential cross-section measurement without fix￾ing the cross-section to the SM prediction before and after the FSR recovery in all lepton channel.
The plot shows the observed value of the VH cross-section times branching ratio of V → lep. and
H → bb¯ (σ × B) which is normalised to the SM prediction. The value of each POI is presented.
The fit configuration with the finest POIs is used in this measurement. The POIs are defined as 15
POIs (Section 11.2) The observed value is measured. The total uncertainty on the observed value
is shown with a black bar and value in the ‘Tot.’ column. The statistical (‘Stat.’) and systematic
(‘Syst.’) uncertainties are also shown, where the statistical uncertainty is shown with a green bar.
The signal theoretical uncertainty of each POI is computed and shown with gray band.

The effect of the FSR recovery in the 0-, 2-lepton channels is assessed in the same ZH STXS
bins. To evaluate the effect in the 0-, 2-lepton channels separately, another measurement is per￾formed by applying the FSR recovery only in the 2-lepton channel. The same fit configuration
is adopted as the measurement in Figure 7.10 and Table 7.3. Table G.2 shows the differences of
the total, statistical, and systematic uncertainties in the different STXS bins between the results
before and after applying the FSR recovery in only the 2-lepton channel. Similarly, the statistical
uncertainty improves by maximum of ∼ 4.7 %, and the total uncertainty improved by maximum
of ∼ 1.3 %.

Chapter H. Additional materials of Diboson modelling study 217
H.3 Alternative models in detail
H.3.1 Electroweak(EW) correction
The full NLO EW calculations for Vector-boson-related events contain contributions from vir￾tual loops and from real emissions of gauge bosons. The Sherpa 2.2.11 samples only contain the
virtual-loop contributions, neglecting real emission both for simplicity and because real emissions
of vector bosons would lead to a fundamentally different experimental signature that is typically
produced in separate samples. The NLO EW corrections are then combined to the NLO QCD
terms (reminding from table H.2 that the Sherpa 2.2.11 ME are computed at NLO in QCD up to 2
extra partons) according to three different schemes: additive, multiplicative and exponentiated. A
summary of the three approaches is given in the following: full details about their motivation and
the approximations applied can be found in Sec 3.1 of [147].
The cross-section calculated at NLO QCD and NLO EW accuracy for a 2 → n final state, where
the higher-order terms in QCD and EW couplings are summed (additive scheme), can be expressed
as:
σNLO
n,QCD+EW = σn,LO + ∆σNLO
n,QCD + ∆σNLO
n,EWvirt (H.1)
where σn,LO denotes the Born-level cross-section and ∆σNLO
n,QCD the NLO QCD corrections to the
Born process. The ∆σNLO
n,EWvirt term only contains the virtual-loop components.
Two additional NLO QCD and NLO EW combination schemes are provided by the
Sherpa framework. The first is the multiplicative scheme, which is defined as:
σNLO
n,QCD×EW = (σn,LO + ∆σNLO
n,QCD) × (1 + ∆σNLO
n,EWvirt). (H.2)
The second scheme is referred to as the exponentiated scheme, and is defined as:
σNLO
n,QCD×eEWvirt = (σn,LO + ∆σNLO
n,QCD) × e
∆σNLO
n,EWvirt . (H.3)
The multiplicative and exponentiated schemes are motivated by the factorization of the QCD
and EW Sudakov corrections at high energies [147]. Small variations are observed for these two
schemes comparing to the additive scheme.
Comparisons of the NLO QCD-only prediction with and without the virtual electroweak correc￾tions are shown in Figure H.2. pV
T shape changes are expectedly observed in figure H.2. The higher
pV
T, the more yields decrease, which arises as NLO EW correction due to the emission and reab￾sorption of electroweak gauge bosons during high-energy scattering processes. Yields decrease by
ZZ:∼7.4%, WZ:∼3.8%, WW:∼4.6% in this analysis phase space. However, the additive scheme

=== PDF PAGE 232 ===
218 H.3 Alternative models in detail
of EW correction is applied to nominal shape and nominal normalization in this analysis, since the
yield change is less than the PMG’s observation and the change is negligible rather than Diboson’s
total acceptance uncertainties.
(a) 2L resolved VH(bb¯) (b) 2L boosted VH(bb¯)
(c) 1L resolved VH(bb¯) (d) 1L boosted VH(bb¯)
Fig. H.2: EW correction effect on the Diboson’s pV
T distribution in each channel and each regime.
Upper panel shows the kinematic distributions, and grey line of bottom panel shows the relative
shape change by the EW correction

H.3.2 Comparison of SHERPA 2.2.11 and SHERPA 2.2.1
The Sherpa 2.2.11 nominal samples are being used for the first time in this analysis. For refer￾ence with the previous VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) analyses, a comparison of the most important differences
- from the stand point of this study - between the new and previous setups of the Sherpa generator
is shown in Table H.2.
Configuration S herpa 2.2.1 S herpa 2.2.11
Generator version Sherpa 2.2.1 Sherpa 2.2.12
PDF set MC Replica NNPDF 3.0nnlo Hessian NNPDF 3.0nnlo
EW input scheme Effective sin2
θeff
QCD accuracy 0–2j@NLO+3,4j@LO 0–2j@NLO+3–5j@LO
EW corrections No Yes
Subtraction scheme Default Catani–Seymour Modified Catani–Seymour
Special treatment for unordered histories No Yes
Scale for H-events STRICT METS H≃
T
Gluon colour/spin exact matching Yes No
Core process for K-factor 2 → 4 2 → 2
Phase-space strategy Inclusive Analytic enhancement
Table H.2: Summary of the Sherpa 2.2.1 and Sherpa 2.2.11 configurations.
The peak of invariant mass of 2 b-jets shifts to high mass because the fragmentation function of
the Sherpa is changed between these two versions [77]. This peak change can be observed in figure
H.3. The plots show the comparison of mass distribution between EW corrected Sherpa 2.2.11 and
Sherpa 2.2.1. Since the EW correction only scales the pV
T shape but doesn’t change the mass shape,
this mass shift is caused by the Sherpa version change. This mbb peak change can also be observed
at the truth level as shown in Figure H.4. Because there is little evidence that which Sherpa version
can describe the data better, the Sherpa 2.2.1 is introduced to estimate fragmentation systematic
uncertainty.
Comparing to the Sherpa 2.2.1, the cross-section differs ∼5-10 %, which can be covered by the
total acceptance uncertainty.

H.4 Systematic uncertainties
H.4.1 Normalization and acceptance uncertainties
All the acceptance uncertainties are computed by enveloping each variation of all alternative
sources with quadrature-sum calculation. For alternative MC models’ source, the largest variation
(Sherpa 2.2.1 or Powheg + Pythia 8) or the largest statistical uncertainty is quadrature-summed,
where nominal NLO QCD (µR = µF = 1) model of Sherpa 2.2.11 is treated as the nominal model.
For each internal weight’s source except the alternative schemes of EW correction, the largest

=== PDF PAGE 236 ===
222 H.4 Systematic uncertainties
variation is quadrature-summed, where nominal NLO QCD (µR = µF = 1) model is treated as the
nominal model. For the alternative schemes of EW correction, the both two schemes’ variations
are quadrature-summed, where the additive scheme of EW correction is treated as the nominal
model. Table H.3 shows the calculation example of the acceptance uncertainty to enveloping the
all variations.

Relative acceptance uncertainties for signal components: VZbb and VZcc
Because VZbb process has high statistics and the purest region in VH(bb¯) regime, VZbb un￾certainty numbers computed in VH(bb¯) regime are applied to all regimes. Since VZbb and VZcc
are topologically the same objects but they have only the flavor tagging difference, these VZbb
numbers are also shared with VZcc process for harmonization purpose among the Diboson signal
components.
For the Stewart-Tackmann (ST) uncertainty [118], a correction factor K is used to prevent from
over-estimation. K = 0.5 for ∆pV
T uncertainties, and K = 0.8 for ∆nJet uncertainties.

Appendix I
Additional materials of final results
I.1 Comparison of BDT-based fit and mbb-based fit
In addition to the fit using BDTVZ in SRs, the fit using mbb in SRs is also performed for the
VZ, Z → bb¯ process as the verification of BDTVZ’s advantage. The observed significance of BDT￾based fit is ∼ 20 % greater than that of mbb-based fit.
In addition to the fit using BDTVH in SRs, the fit using mbb in SRs is also performed for the
VH, H → bb¯ process as the verification of BDTVH’s advantage. The observed significance of
BDT-based fit is ∼ 60 % greater than that of mbb-based fit.
I.2 Signal strength results in the different lepton channels or in
different production modes
In Figure I.1, the trends of observed signal strength of the VH(H → bb¯) process resemble that
of the VZ(Z → bb¯) process.

Fig. I.3: The BDTVZ post-fit distributions in the resolved VH(bb¯) regime. The plots in all lepton
channels in all pV
T regions are shown. The plots in 2-jet region are selected. The background events
normalised in the VZ fit are shown as filled histograms. The diboson signal process VZ(Z → bb¯)
is shown as a filled histogram on top of the fitted backgrounds normalised to the signal strength
measured from data (µ = 0.91), and shown as an unfilled histogram, scaled by a value reported in
the legend for better visualisation. The range of the combined statistical and systematic uncertain￾ties for the sum events of the fitted signal and background (S+B) are indicated by the hatched band
at the top of the filled histograms. The ratios of the data event yields over the sum of the fitted
signal and background event yields are shown in the lower panel. The plots are also published in
Ref. [105].

=== PDF PAGE 255 ===
241
Appendix J
Extrapolation of the signal sensitiv￾ity in VH(H → bb¯) process at the HL￾LHC
In the HL-LHC senario, dataset of →s = 14 TeV proton-proton collisions corresponding to an
integrated luminosity of 3000 fb−1 is assumed*1. The same analysis method of the previous
VH(H → bb¯) analysis [12] is applied, and normalized with the expected dataset for the HL-LHC.
The systematic uncertainties used in this extrapolation analysis are scaled from the previous anal￾ysis [12], and are listed as Table J.1. This configuration is extrapolated from the expected perfor￾mance of the ATLAS experimental setup for the HL-LHC project [149, ?]. The expected best-fit
results are derived with the Asimov dataset.
The signal strength of the inclusive VH(H → bb¯) prcess and the separated WH, H → bb¯,
ZH, H → bb¯ processes are derived as [150],
µbb
VH = 1.00 ± 0.06, (J.1)
µbb
ZH = 1.00 ± 0.07 = 1.00 ± 0.03(S tat.) ± 0.06(S yst.), (J.2)
µbb
WH = 1.00 ± 0.08 = 1.00 ± 0.04(S tat.) ± 0.07(S yst.). (J.3)
The precisions of signal strengths improved by ∼ 55 % for the inclusive process, ∼ 73 % for the
ZH, H → bb¯ process and ∼ 68 % for the WH, H → bb¯ process from the previous analysis [12].
The jets experimental uncertainty and signal modelling uncertainty is dominant in the systematic
*1 The latest extrapolation to the HL-LHC senario for the VH(H → bb¯/cc¯) analysis is published (25 Mar 2025) as a
public note [148] from CERN after the defence of this doctoral dissertation (19 Mar 2025).

uncertainty of these signal strength. This precision still cannot afford to evaluate the deviations
from the SM prediction, unless upgrading the systematic estimation.

the correlation of each STXS bins in Figure J.1 (b) [150].

the HL-LHC [150]. (a) The separated signal strength of the VH(H → bb¯) process in the reduced

=== PDF PAGE 257 ===
Chapter J. Extrapolation of the signal sensitivity in VH(H → bb¯) process at the HL-LHC243
The previous analysis presents the separated signal strengths results in the same reduced STXS
scheme. The uncertainties of these 5 POI varies from ∼ 30 % in the low pV
T regions to ∼ 85 % in
the high pV
T regions. As the results of this extrapolation analysis, the uncertainties varies from ∼ 8
% in the high pV
T regions to ∼ 18 in the low pV
T regions. This precision may be able to afford to
observe the deviations from the SM prediction in the high pV
T regions.
